Google LCS AM 面试总手册

SHEIN 与 app retail 客户线 · 自我介绍 + 战前情报 + 必答 6 题 + HQ 28 题 + BQ 23 题 · 中文原稿配英文口语逐字稿

0自我介绍
整场面试的锚，先背熟这一段（1 条）

中文（逐字照搬）

hello，我叫litchi，中文名是李庆祺，目前就职于腾讯，在腾讯广告BU下面，负责家清纸品头部客户的日常运营，为品牌GMV负责 我可以将工作分为三部分，第一部分是日常对客与客情维护。因为我负责的是头部商家，组织架构会更加立体和多元，所以在日常的对客沟通中，我不仅需与执行层共同解决运营中出现的问题，还需要和决策层保持密切联系，通过日常的答疑解惑以及深度的经营诊断分析，目前虽然仅入职三个月，但家清纸品头部24个品牌，已经和10个品牌建立了深度合作，也通过T2T会议等形式促进了品牌内部架构的更新调整；第二部分是作为运营中台，我需要产出不仅针对单客，更是普适于行业的解决方案，这需要我对所负责的行业有深入洞察从而挖掘出品牌痛点以及机会点，目前ongoing的项目就是在产出家清纸品行业解决方案，预计8月底会落地头部24家客户；最后一部分就是内部的合作，因为我们隶属于广告BU，所以需要和销售密切配合，在日常工作中，我会和销售一起对品牌和行业做未来规划，分别从GMV和广告视角出发做方案共创 而之前我在欧莱雅，则是以品牌方的视角，在天猫的人，货，场进行全方位的运营，并最终deliver店铺的生意。在我任职期间，负责过的大促以及IP活动都以超额达成目标完结 "想要来谷歌工作我觉得可以从两个方面来说：一、想要做平台视角下的销售，真正量化自己的产出；二、对谷歌产品能力的认可 一、在结合欧莱雅的电商销售角色和腾讯平台运营角色的经验之后，我发现我自己真正想要的工作是站在更宏观的平台视角下，做一个产出有直接量化的工作，which is 广告销售，在对客的过程中，我不仅想要站在中台去给到策略，更想和客户有更深的链接，因为只有这样才会更深入业务，但同时又有更宏观的视角 二、在我和阿里的工作中我也接触了很多国内电商平台的广告工具，但是我认为在国内电商一片红海的竞争格局下，很难帮助到中小商家去拓圈，但是对于谷歌来说却不一样，背靠全球最大的搜索引擎平台，我觉得我能够利用好谷歌的资源和优势去帮助更多中国商家做好出海的品牌营销，这也是为什么我想要选择谷歌的原因

English

Hi, I'm Litchi — Chinese name Li Qingqi. I'm currently at Tencent, in the Tencent Ads BU, where I own the day-to-day operations of our top home-care and tissue clients and am accountable for their brand GMV. I divide my work into three parts. The first is day-to-day client engagement and relationship management. Because I cover top merchants — whose org structures are more layered and more diverse — my daily work goes beyond solving operational issues with the execution level. I also stay closely connected to the C-level. Through day-to-day support and in-depth business diagnosis, even though I've only been here three months, I've already built deep partnerships with 10 of the 24 top home-care and tissue brands, and through T2T (top-to-top) meetings and similar formats I've helped drive internal org restructuring within these brands. The second part is operating as a central operations team behind sales. I produce solutions that work industry-wide — not just for a specific client. That requires deep industry insight to surface each brand's pain points and opportunities. The ongoing project right now is building the home-care and tissue industry solution, which is expected to roll out to all 24 top clients by the end of August. The third part is internal collaboration. Since we sit in the Ads BU, I work closely with the sales team. In day-to-day work, the sales team and I plan the future of brands and the industry together, co-creating solutions from both the GMV and the advertising angle. Before Tencent, I was at L'Oréal, on the brand side, running the full-scope operations of people, merchandise, and storefront on Tmall and ultimately delivering the store's revenue. During my tenure, every major promotion and IP campaign I owned finished above target. As for why Google, there are two reasons. First, I want to do sales from a platform perspective and truly quantify my own output. Second, it's my recognition of Google's product capabilities. On the first point — after combining my e-commerce sales role at L'Oréal with my platform operations role at Tencent, I realized what I really want is to stand on a more macro, platform-level view and do work whose output is directly measurable. That is, ads sales. With clients, I don't want to just hand down strategy from the middle office; I want a deeper connection with clients, because only then can I go deeper into the business while still keeping a macro perspective. On the second point — through my work with Alibaba, I've touched many domestic e-commerce ad tools. But in the red-ocean competition of domestic e-commerce, it's very hard to help brands expand their territory. Google is different. Backed by the world's largest search engine, I feel I can leverage Google's resources and strengths to help more Chinese brands do great overseas brand marketing. That's why I want to come to Google.
1战前情报
面试前先读这两条（2 条）

中文

【用法】这张卡是你在面试里所有 SHEIN 判断的出处。每条都标了来源和口径，说的时候加一句"基于公开财报和媒体报道"，不要说成内部数据。 ■ 公司披露口径（港交所公告 / 招股书 / 业绩公告） · 2026 H1 净收入 201 亿美元，同比 +1%；订单量 5.49 亿单，同比 +6.4% → 单均收入被压缩（上市后首份中期业绩，2026-09-28） · 2026 Q2：欧洲净收入约 38 亿美元，同比 -13.9%。公司自己的归因是"我们提价了，并且主动降低了线上广告投放"，以应对免税政策取消 · 2026 Q2：美国收入同比 -6%；其他地区（主要是拉美）+21.6% · 2026 Q2 调整后净利率 6.2% → 2.1%，公司归因于油价与海运费上涨（中东地缘） · 欧盟月活：2025 年底约 1.56 亿 → 2026 年 6 月 1.28 亿，少了约 2800 万 · 2026 Q1：营收同比 +1.1%，净亏损 9900 万美元；美国收入 20.4 亿美元，同比 -14.3% · 2025 全年净收入约 418 亿美元，净利 20.6 亿美元（较 2024 年减少约四成） · 香港 IPO：2026 年 9 月初挂牌，发行价约 48.56 港元，募资约 17 亿美元，对应估值约 265 亿美元（对比 2022 年私募融资时约 982 亿美元）；上市后股价一度跌超 27% · 服务收入（3P 平台相关）：2023 年 8.68 亿美元 → 2025 年 47.4 亿美元，占净收入 2.7% → 11.3%；2026 Q1 达 14.3% · IPO 募资用途：约 80% 投向技术能力、品牌建设与全球市场拓展 ■ 政策（这条是 SHEIN 当前最大的外部变量） · 美国：2025-05-02 起取消 800 美元以下小包免税，综合进口税率从 0–62.5% 抬到 10–87.5% · 欧盟：2026-07-01 起取消 150 欧元以下免税（2026-02 理事会通过海关改革） · 法国：对超快时尚征费，最高约 20 欧元/件 ■ 券商梳理 / 第三方数据（面试时说"券商估算""第三方数据"，别当公司披露讲） · 2026 H1 GMV 约 315 亿美元，同比 +16%，净利率约 5%（券商估算） · 地区结构估算：北美 30% / 欧洲 22% / 拉美 20% / 中东 17% / 东南亚 6% · 模式结构估算：1P 48% / 全托管 26% / 半托管 16% / POP 10%，半托管与 POP 增速最快 · 履约：截至 2026-06 全球仓库网络约 600 万平方米；2026 年本土发货比例过半 · 巴西本地化生产：投资 1.5 亿美元，约 300 家合作工厂，占巴西销量约 55%，2026 年底目标 85% · 品类：服装占比降至约 65%，家居 / 美妆 / 3C 等非服装超 35% · 竞争：法国低价市场份额 SHEIN 从 17.6% 降至 9.4%，Temu 升至 20.6%（媒体引用第三方份额数据） · 2025-03～05 SHEIN 美国日活下降约 25%（Sensor Tower，券商引用） ■ 一句话总结（面试里可以这么说） SHEIN 不是卖不动了，是单位经济模型被两头挤：成本端关税和运费吃毛利，需求端涨价后价格敏感用户流失。结果是订单还在涨、收入不动，客单价掉了。它现在的命题不是"买更多用户"，是"买更值钱的用户、并把低价心智换成品牌心智"。

中文

■ 客户画像：跨境 DTC / 电商 app 广告主 共同特征：① 生意 100% 在 app 和 web 上，所有投放最终回到"安装 + 首单 + 复购"；② 内部有很强的增长团队和自建数据/归因能力，很多比代理商还懂；③ 多市场并行（一个团队同时跑十几个国家），预算按市场切；④ 决策极快，也砍得极快，今天加预算，明天关税变了就砍一半；⑤ 对价格敏感，习惯拿 Google 和 Meta/TikTok/Amazon 做横向比价。 跟传统品牌客户最大的区别：他们不要"提案"，要"结论 + 实验设计"。 ■ Google 侧 2026 能卖的东西（按客户的漏斗分层） · 拉新/效果：App campaigns（安装 + 应用内事件优化）、PMax（跨 Search/YouTube/Display/Discover/Gmail/Maps）、Shopping / Product feed、AI Max for Search（AI 驱动的搜索扩量，DSA 已并入方向） · 上层与品牌：YouTube（含 Shorts）、Demand Gen（视觉发现场景：YouTube / Discover / Gmail） · 出价方向：tROAS / value-based bidding ， 对 SHEIN 这种客单价在掉的客户，这是最该推的方向（从"买便宜点击"转向"买高价值用户"） · 测量：Meridian（Google 开源的 MMM）、增量实验（geo experiment）、Enhanced Conversions、Consent Mode、Data Manager（把各方第一方数据归集） · 创意：Asset Studio、素材自动化与 A/B 测试 · 商业化新方向：AI Mode / 对话式搜索里的新广告形态、agentic commerce（UCP 类） 注意：产品名和新功能迭代很快，面试里只说你确实用过/确定的，不确定的名字宁可用"Google 在 AI 搜索和 agentic 方向的新广告形态"这种方式带过，别背错名字。 ■ 面试当天必须确认的三件事（第一句话就要问清楚） 1. 地点：这个岗位的 base 是上海还是北京？（这是你上一轮没走通的那件事，必须在第一轮确认，不要到最后才发现） 2. 客户盘子：SHEIN 之外，"app retail 类"具体是哪些客户、多少家、是 LCS 还是带 scaled/中小盘的混合盘子？ 3. 团队结构：这个组是纯 AM，还是 AM + 分析师 + gTech 的 pod 模式？你接手是有前任交接还是从零建？（决定你 90 天计划怎么讲）
2必答六题
这六题基本躲不掉，答顺了后面都好说（6 条）

中文

三层：离开什么 → 去哪里 → 为什么是现在。别讲成腾讯不好，讲成我要的下一件事在腾讯拿不到。 ■ 第一层：为什么离开 我在腾讯做的工作，我自己很喜欢，但它有两个天花板。 第一，我的产出是"被采纳"，不是"被衡量"。我做行业解决方案、做经营诊断，品牌采纳了、生意涨了，但这个结果很难直接挂到我一个人头上，它中间隔着销售、隔着代运营、隔着品牌自己的执行。我想做的是那种"这个数字涨了，是我直接谈出来、推出来的"的工作。 第二，我在腾讯的位置是中台。中台往上走是专家路线，会越来越深、越来越窄；而我想要的是往客户那一侧走，往生意结果那一侧走，而不是往方法论那一侧走。 ■ 第二层：为什么是 Google、为什么是广告销售 这要从我自己的两段经历说起。我先在欧莱雅做品牌方，负责天猫的人货场，那时我是花钱的人；后来到腾讯做平台方，负责行业运营，那时我是站在平台这一侧看整个行业。这两边都待过之后，我发现最能发挥我的位置，是"站在平台视角、但直接对一个客户的生意结果负责"，这就是广告销售。 而之所以是 Google，不是别的平台，原因很具体：中国品牌出海是未来十年最确定的结构性机会，而 Google 是唯一一个同时握着搜索、YouTube、Android、地图，还能覆盖全球几乎所有市场的平台。我在腾讯看过太多商家在国内的红海里互相卷价格，我不想卖那种"在存量里抢份额"的广告，我想帮中国商家去挣全球市场的钱。 ■ 第三层：为什么是现在 因为在腾讯，我要拿的东西已经拿到了：头部客户的行业运营、从 0 搭行业解决方案、和销售一起做共创，这三件事我都完整跑过一轮了。再往下是重复。 而 LCS 这边的时机也很具体：现在中国出海客户正在从"买量换增长"转向"要单位经济模型、要品牌溢价"，这个转向特别需要一个既懂广告产品、又能跟客户谈生意结构的人。我恰好两边都做过。所以是现在。 ■ 关于地点（如果面试官没问，你也要在回答里主动落地） 我 base 在上海，也打算长期留在上海，这一点我在最开始就想确认清楚，因为它决定了我们能不能长期合作。

English · spoken script

So let me take this in three parts, what I'm moving away from, what I'm moving toward, and why now. First, why I'm leaving. Honestly, I like what I do at Tencent. But there are two ceilings I keep hitting. The first one is that my output gets adopted, but it doesn't get measured. I build industry playbooks, I run business diagnostics, brands adopt them and their business grows, but that result can't really be attributed back to me. There's a salesperson in between, an agency in between, the brand's own execution in between. What I want is a job where I can say: that number moved, and I'm the one who pushed it there. The second ceiling is that I sit in a central strategy function. Going up from there is a specialist track, deeper and narrower. What I want is to move toward the client and toward business outcomes, not toward methodology. Now, why Google, and why advertising sales. This comes from the two sides I've worked on. I started at L'Oréal on the brand side, running Tmall, I was the one spending the money. Then I moved to Tencent on the platform side, running industry operations, I was looking at the whole category from the platform's seat. Having done both, the place where I'm most useful is: platform perspective, but directly accountable for one client's business result. That's advertising sales. And why Google specifically, not another platform, it's concrete. Chinese brands going global is the most certain structural opportunity of the next decade, and Google is the only platform that holds search, YouTube, Android, and Maps at the same time and covers nearly every market in the world. At Tencent I watched too many merchants fight each other over price in a domestic red ocean. I don't want to sell advertising that helps someone grab a slightly bigger slice of a fixed pie. I want to help Chinese merchants go earn money in global markets. Third, why now. Because at Tencent I've already taken what I came for. I ran industry operations for the head brands, I built a category playbook from zero, I co-created plans with sales, I've done a full cycle on all three. What's next there is repetition. And the timing on this side is just as specific: Chinese advertisers going overseas are shifting from buying growth with volume to caring about unit economics and brand premium. That shift needs someone who understands ad products and can also talk to a client about their business structure. I've done both sides of that. So, now. One more thing I want to put on the table early, because it matters: I'm based in Shanghai and I intend to stay in Shanghai. I'd rather confirm that in the first conversation than discover it at the end.

中文

【讲法】这题最忌讳列形容词（"我很努力、我很会沟通"）。每条都必须是"我能做、而大多数候选人做不到"的具体动作，且挂一个真实例子。 ■ 差异化一：我在牌桌两边都坐过 大多数候选人只有一边，要么一直在品牌/广告主那边，要么一直在平台/媒体这边。我在欧莱雅是花钱的人（天猫人货场，对店铺生意负责），在腾讯是平台的人（广告 BU，对行业和品牌 GMV 负责）。 这带来一个很具体的优势：我跟客户开会时，我听得懂他们内部在吵什么。市场部要品牌声量、电商部要 ROI、财务要看 margin、供应链怕压货，这些矛盾我在品牌方亲身经历过。所以我不会只递一份"提升 ROAS 20%"的提案给一个其实在为毛利发愁的客户。我能把 Google 的产品翻译成他内部的语言，也能把他内部的矛盾翻译回 Google 的账户结构。 ■ 差异化二：我做过"把供给方从 0 养到 1"，这是零售 app 客户的同一个命题 我在腾讯视频号做家清纸品行业运营，本质上是：把品牌接进平台、过合规、配资源、拉私域和人群、设里程碑、盯复购，从 0 到 1 把供给方养大。 零售 app 客户的核心命题是同一件事：把一个用户从首次安装，养成会复购、客单在涨的高 LTV 用户。换了个行业，动作是一样的。这就是为什么我看 SHEIN 的问题，第一反应不是"广告投得不够"，而是"它拉来的人不对、留存价值在掉"。 ■ 差异化三：我对数据有"归因洁癖" 举个例子。24 年双十一，我投一个叫超级直播的产品，开播三小时 ROI 就到 7 以上，是平时的两倍，按计划应该继续加投。但我去看分渠道的生意数字，发现达人直播间完成率 110%，我自己的品牌自播间不到 50%，跟 ROI=7 完全对不上。 我下钻之后发现，它是末次点击归因：消费者在自播间被广告曝光，然后去比价、在更划算的达人直播间下单，钱被算在了我的点位上，但我的直播间一单没多。我先让投手停投，把省下的预算挪 30% 到搜索类的点位（入口完全不同，避免人群重叠和跳转比价），最后把自播间的生意救了回来。 这件事放到 LCS 的客户上一模一样：现在很多 app 客户看到的 ROAS 很好看，但生意没涨，因为归因窗口和触点重叠，把自然量算成了广告量。我习惯性地会去追这一层，而不是看板说涨了就加预算。 ■ 差异化四：我是真的用 AI 干活的人，不是用 AI 写邮件的人 我自己是 AI 产品出身，日常就在搭 agent 和自动化工作流，把重复性的运营动作消化掉。大客户 AM 现在最大的瓶颈其实就一个：一个人要覆盖的市场、客户、账户太多了，靠人力堆不过来。大多数人用 AI 写封邮件、润色个提案；我用 AI 是把一整条重复性流程自动化掉。对 LCS 这种"少数大客户 + 一堆中小客户"的盘子来说，这是能不能把服务做深的分水岭。 ■ 收尾一句 如果只能留一条：我听得懂客户内部的生意矛盾，也讲得出 Google 的产品逻辑，能在两边之间做翻译。这是两边各待了几年换来的，不是学出来的。

English · spoken script

I'll give you four things, and I'll attach a real example to each one, because adjectives don't mean anything here. The first one: I've sat on both sides of the table. Most candidates have only one side, either they've always been on the brand or advertiser side, or they've always been on the platform side. I was the one spending the money at L'Oréal, running Tmall and owning the store's P&L. Then I was the platform at Tencent, in the ads organization, owning a category and brand GMV. And what that gives me is very concrete: when I sit in a client meeting, I can hear what their internal fight actually is. Marketing wants brand, e-commerce wants ROI, finance is staring at margin, supply chain is scared of inventory. I've lived those conflicts from inside a brand. So I'm not going to walk in and hand someone a "20% ROAS uplift" deck when the thing keeping them up at night is margin. I can translate Google's products into their internal language, and translate their internal tension back into an account structure. Second: I've actually done the work of growing a supply-side partner from zero to one, and that is exactly the problem these retail app clients have. On WeChat Channels I ran industry operations for the home-care and paper category. What that really means is: onboard a brand, get them through compliance, allocate resources, build private-domain and audience ops, set milestones, watch repeat purchase. Growing a supplier from zero to one. A retail app client's core problem is the same thing: take a user from first install to a repeat buyer whose basket keeps growing. Different industry, identical motion. And that's why when I look at SHEIN's situation, my first reaction isn't "they're not spending enough on ads", it's "they're acquiring the wrong users and the retained value is dropping." Third: I have what I'd call an attribution allergy, I don't trust a metric until I've chased it down. Let me give you an example. Double Eleven last year, I was running a livestream ad product. Three hours into the broadcast, ROAS was above 7, about double normal. By the plan, I should have kept adding budget. But I went and looked at the channel-level sales numbers: the affiliate livestream rooms were at 110% of target and my own brand livestream room was under 50%. That doesn't reconcile with a ROAS of 7. So I dug in, and I found the product used last-click attribution. A user would be exposed to the ad in my brand livestream, then go price-compare and buy in a cheaper affiliate room. The revenue got credited to my placement, but my room didn't sell a single extra unit. I paused the spend first, then moved 30% of the freed budget into a search-style placement, completely different entry point, no audience overlap, no hopping to compare prices, and I recovered the brand room's business. That maps directly onto LCS clients: a lot of app advertisers today see beautiful ROAS while their business isn't growing, because attribution windows and overlapping touchpoints are counting organic demand as paid demand. Chasing that layer is a habit for me, not something I do when someone asks. Fourth: I actually use AI to do the work, not to write about the work. I come from an AI product background, and I build agents and automated workflows to absorb repetitive operational work as a normal part of my day. The biggest bottleneck for a large-customer AM right now is simple: one person, too many markets, too many accounts. You can't solve that with headcount. Most people use AI to draft an email or polish a deck. I use it to automate an entire recurring process. On a book that mixes a few very large clients with a long tail, that's the difference between servicing deeply and servicing shallowly. And if I had to keep one: I can hear a client's internal business tension and Google's product logic at the same time, and translate between them. That didn't come from a course. It came from spending years on each side.

中文

【开场一句话】 SHEIN 现在的问题不是"卖不动了"，是单位经济模型被两头挤。它接下来的命题也不是"买更多用户"，而是"买更值钱的用户，并把低价心智换成品牌心智"。 ■ 诊断：三层 第一层，它原来的增长飞轮是什么。三件套：极致的供应链速度（小单快反，100-200 件起单测款，每天上新几千款）× 极致的价格 × 极其精准的买量。飞轮转起来的前提是两件东西都便宜：流量便宜、跨境小包便宜。 第二层，为什么现在转不动了。这两件东西同时变贵了。成本端：美国 2025 年 5 月取消 800 美元以下小包免税，欧盟 2026 年 7 月取消 150 欧元免税，法国还要对超快时尚单件征费；再加上运费和油价。需求端：它被迫涨价（媒体口径 9%–30%），价格最敏感的那批用户就开始比价了，而 Temu 就在旁边等着。 两头一挤的结果就写在财报里：2026 上半年订单量 +6.4%，净收入 +1%。单均收入在掉，也就是说，现在买的人买得更便宜、或者更依赖折扣。 第三层，最危险的那一层。欧洲二季度收入 -13.9%，公司自己的解释里有一句"我们主动降低了线上广告投放"。同一时间，欧盟月活从约 1.56 亿掉到 1.28 亿，少了 2800 万。这形成一个恶性循环：涨价 → 少投广告 → 用户流失 → 剩下的用户更价格敏感 → 只能继续打折。它现在的打法，其实是在用品牌资产换短期 margin。 而资本市场已经用脚投票了：估值从 2022 年的约 982 亿美元，到这次香港上市约 265 亿美元。市场不再愿意为"纯低价 + 买量"这个故事付溢价。 ■ 那它怎么把生意做更大？四条路径，每条我都能对应到 Google 能帮什么 第一条，从"买便宜的点击"转向"买高价值的用户"。客单价在掉，本质是拉来的人和留存价值不对。做法是把出价目标从单次转化成本，换成预测的用户价值：按首单价值、90 天价值分层出价，把预算从最便宜的人群挪到最值钱的人群。这是 Google 侧 value-based bidding（tROAS）+ 应用内事件价值回传 + 受众分层最直接能帮上忙的地方。对 SHEIN 这种客单价承压的客户，这是我最想推的第一件事。 第二条，把"便宜"这个唯一心智，替换成"上新快 + 会穿"。它现在的问题是品牌层一停，用户就走。所以欧洲那笔广告预算不该砍，该改结构：效果层继续按 ROI 跑，但品牌层（YouTube、视觉发现类的场景）要保持常年在线，把心智从"最便宜"抬到"最会挑"。IPO 募资的 80% 里有一块就是品牌建设，说明公司自己也认同这个方向。 第三条，地理再平衡。美国和欧洲在承压，其他地区 +21.6%，主要是拉美。巴西本地化生产已经占当地销量约 55%，目标是 2026 年底 85%，本地供给上来了，本地流量必须同步跟上，否则产能闲置。Google 在新市场冷启动上是最强的：多语言、多市场的搜索和购物覆盖、YouTube 的用户规模、加本地化的素材。这一块后面我会单独讲怎么做。 第四条，3P 平台化，这是它真正的天花板突破。服务收入占净收入从 2023 年的 2.7%，到 2026 年一季度 14.3%，它正在从一个零售商变成一个平台。平台化带来两个新需求：一是招卖家、招品牌（这是 B2B 获客），二是它自己在建站内广告系统。这里有个微妙的地方我必须点出来：SHEIN 今天是我的广告主，未来它也可能变成一个卖广告的人。作为 AM，我要看得懂这层竞合，不能只盯着一个钱包。 第五条，补一条更基础的：提高客单和复购要靠品类。非服装（家居、美妆、3C）已经超过 35%，这些品类天然客单更高。跨品类连带，买了连衣裙的人推家居，正是购物类广告和商品 feed 最擅长的事。 ■ 收尾 一句话回答"他们怎么做更大的生意"：别再用更便宜的价格换更多订单，改用更高价值的用户、更贵的品类、更多的新市场去换单均收入。 【口径提醒】以上判断基于公开财报、招股书和媒体报道，不是内部信息。面试里说的时候加一句"基于公开信息我的判断是"，反而显得你严谨。

English · spoken script

Let me start with the one-line version. SHEIN's problem right now is not that it can't sell. It's that the unit economics are being squeezed from both ends. And the job ahead isn't buying more users, it's buying more valuable users, and trading the cheap-price perception for an actual brand. So the diagnosis, in three layers. Layer one: what the original flywheel was. Three things: extreme supply chain speed, they test with 100 to 200 unit runs, thousands of new styles a day, times extreme low price, times very precise paid acquisition. And that flywheel needed two things to be cheap: traffic had to be cheap, and cross-border small parcels had to be cheap. Layer two: why it's not spinning anymore. Both of those got expensive at the same time. On the cost side: the US removed the de minimis exemption for sub-$800 parcels in May 2025, the EU removed the €150 exemption in July 2026, France is adding a per-item fast fashion fee, and then freight and fuel on top. On the demand side: they had to raise prices, and the most price-sensitive users started comparing, with Temu sitting right there waiting. You can see the squeeze in the numbers: first half of 2026, order volume up 6.4%, net revenue up 1%. Revenue per order is falling. Translation: the people buying now are buying cheaper, or they're buying on discount. Layer three, and this is the one that worries me most. Europe revenue was down 13.9% in Q2, and inside the company's own explanation there's a line: we lowered online advertising spend. In the same period, EU monthly actives went from about 156 million to 128 million, down roughly 28 million. That's a loop: raise prices, cut ads, lose users, the users left are even more price-sensitive, so you discount again. Right now they are, in effect, trading brand equity for short-term margin. And the market has already priced that: a valuation of roughly $98 billion in 2022 down to about $26.5 billion at the Hong Kong listing. Investors are no longer paying a premium for a cheap-plus-paid-acquisition story. So, how do they get bigger? Four paths, and each one maps onto something Google can actually do. Path one: stop buying cheap clicks, start buying valuable users. Falling basket size means they're acquiring people whose retained value is wrong. The move is to shift the bid target from cost per conversion to predicted user value, bid differently by first-order value and by 90-day value, and move budget from the cheapest audience to the most valuable one. That is exactly where value-based bidding and in-app event value feedback on the Google side earns its keep. For a client whose basket size is under pressure, this is the first thing I'd want to push. Path two: replace "cheap" as the only thing they stand for. The problem is that when brand spend stops, users leave. So that European budget shouldn't have been cut, it should have been restructured. Keep performance running on ROI, but keep brand, YouTube, visual discovery environments, always on, and move the perception from cheapest to best-curated. And by the way, a chunk of the IPO proceeds is earmarked for brand building, so the company clearly agrees with the direction. Path three: rebalance geography. US and Europe are under pressure, rest of world was up 21.6%, mostly Latin America. Local production in Brazil is already about 55% of local sales with a target of 85% by end of 2026. If they're building local supply, local demand has to ramp with it or that capacity sits idle. New-market cold start is where Google is strongest, multilingual, multi-market search and shopping coverage, YouTube's reach, localized creative. I'll come back to that separately. Path four: the 3P marketplace, which is the real ceiling-breaker. Service revenue went from 2.7% of net revenue in 2023 to 14.3% by Q1 2026. They're turning from a retailer into a platform. And that creates two new needs: recruiting sellers and brands, which is B2B acquisition, and their own on-site ads business. Here's the delicate part I'd want to name out loud: SHEIN is my advertiser today, but it may also become someone who sells advertising. As an account manager I need to understand that tension, not just stare at one budget. And one more basic one: basket size and repeat rate come from category mix. Non-apparel, home, beauty, electronics, is already over 35%, and those categories naturally carry higher baskets. Cross-category affinity, someone who bought a dress and then gets shown home products, is exactly what shopping and product-feed driven campaigns are built for. So if you want the one-line answer to "how do they get bigger": stop trading lower prices for more orders. Start trading higher-value users, higher-value categories, and more new markets for higher revenue per order. The first one is what made them. The second one is what they have to learn next.

中文

【开场】服务 SHEIN 这类客户，和传统品牌客户的方法必须换一套。他们不缺报表、不缺提案，缺的是"结论 + 实验设计 + 内部资源"。所以我的服务模型是四件事。 ■ 一、先画客户的"内部 KPI 地图"，再谈任何投放 SHEIN 这种体量的客户不是一个客户，是一组团队：用户增长/UA、品牌市场、各区域市场、财务、法务合规、数据科学，可能还有平台招商团队。每个团队背的指标不一样，而且经常互相冲突。 我接手第一件事不是做提案，是搞清楚三件事：谁的指标是什么、谁能拍板预算、谁有一票否决权。否则我把一份"提升 ROAS"的方案递给一个正在为毛利发愁的人，方案再漂亮也不会被采纳。 ■ 二、用他们的口径对话，不用我的话术 这类客户自己有归因系统、有数据科学团队，很多时候比我还懂。所以我第一步一定是问清楚：你们的 success metric 到底是什么？是 D7 ROAS、是边际贡献、还是新客 CAC？这个数字怎么算出来的、扣没扣退货、扣没扣关税成本？ 问清楚之前，我不谈 Google 的产品。因为一旦我用我的口径讲 ROAS，用他的口径一算发现不对，这一单的信任就没了。Googleyness 里那句"以用户为先"，放到 B2B 就是"以客户的指标为先"。 ■ 三、我提供的是速度和资源，不是报表 他们自己拉报表比我快。我的价值在三件事： 第一，他们看不到的横向信息，同一个打法在别的市场、别的品类、别的客户身上跑得怎么样。这是只有站在平台这一侧才有的信息。 第二，Google 内部的调动能力，产品团队、技术支持、政策申诉、新功能的提前接入。客户自己找不到人的时候，我能把事情推动起来。 第三，提前预警。比如搜索侧的产品形态在往 AI 方向迁移、某些旧广告形式要退出；这种变化如果我提前一个季度告诉他，他不用临时救火。 ■ 四、不要一上来谈大战略，先赌一个小的 信任是打出来的，讲不出来。我会找一个明确的、有争议的、能在一个月内出结论的小问题，先做一次实验：比如"某个承压市场恢复品牌层投放，对 90 天留存和单均收入有没有帮助"，成功标准事先跟客户对齐好，用他的口径算。赢一次，后面才有资格谈年度计划。 ■ 90 天计划 Day 1–30，听。客户侧：内部 KPI 地图、现有账户结构、历史做过哪些实验、结论是什么、他们自己的归因口径怎么算。内部侧：跟前任 AM 交接、认识 gTech 和产品团队、把客户的历史工单和痛点读一遍。这 30 天我不做新提案。 Day 31–60，证明一个点。挑一个高价值、有争议、可验证的问题做实验，事先书面对齐成功标准和测量方法，用他们的口径出结果。无论赢输，都要给结论。 Day 61–90，放大并制度化。把实验结论变成季度联合计划里的增长项目；建立双周运营会 + 月度业务回顾 + 季度规划的节奏；把 Google 的产品路线图和新功能接入他们的日历，尤其是 Q4，那是他们自己说的最重要的促销窗口（双十一、黑五、网一、圣诞）。 ■ 一条绝对不能犯的错 永远不要向客户承诺具体的 ROAS 或收入数字。价格是竞价市场决定的，不是我定的，也不是客户定的。我能承诺的是"什么时候跑通"、"什么时候能出结论"、"出问题我多久响应"。承诺了数字又做不到，是这类客户最不能原谅的事。 ■ 收尾一句 对这类客户，我的价值就两件事：让你更快拿到一个可信的结论，以及帮你把 Google 内部调动起来。

English · spoken script

Servicing a client like SHEIN needs a different playbook than a traditional brand client. They don't need more reports and they don't need more decks. What they need is a conclusion, an experiment design, and access to internal resources. So my service model is four things. One: I map the client's internal KPI landscape before I talk about a single campaign. A client at that scale isn't one client, it's a set of teams, user acquisition, brand marketing, regional markets, finance, legal and compliance, data science, maybe a marketplace recruiting team. Each one carries a different metric and they conflict constantly. So the first thing I do is not a proposal. It's answering three questions: who owns which metric, who can actually sign off budget, and who has veto power. Because if I hand a "lift your ROAS" deck to someone who is actually panicking about margin, it doesn't matter how good the deck is, it's not getting adopted. Two: I speak in their measurement language, not in my product language. Clients like this have their own attribution stack and their own data science team, and frankly sometimes they know more than I do. So my first question is always: what is your actual success metric? Is it D7 ROAS, is it contribution margin, is it new-customer CAC? And how is that number computed, is returns netted out, is tariff cost netted out? I will not talk about a Google product until I understand that. Because the moment I quote a ROAS in my definition and they run it in theirs and the numbers don't match, the trust for that whole deal is gone. There's a Googleyness line about focusing on the user first, in a B2B seat that literally means focusing on the client's metric first. Three: what I sell is speed and access, not reporting. They can pull their own reports faster than I can. My value is three things. First, the horizontal view they can't see, how the same playbook performs in another market, another category, another advertiser. That information only exists on the platform side. Second, the ability to move Google internally, product teams, technical support, policy appeals, early access to new features. When a client can't find the right person, I'm the one who makes the thing move. Third, advance warning. If the search product surface is shifting toward AI experiences and some older formats are being retired, and I tell them a quarter early, they don't have to firefight. Four: don't open with a grand strategy, open with one small bet. Trust is earned, not presented. I look for one specific, contested, answerable-within-a-month question and run it as an experiment. For example: in a market that's under pressure, does restoring brand-layer spend actually move 90-day retention and revenue per order? I agree the success criteria in writing up front and I measure it in their definition. Win once, and then I've earned the right to talk about an annual plan. The ninety days. Day one to thirty, I listen. On the client side: the KPI map, the current account structure, what experiments they've already run and what they concluded, and how their own attribution is calculated. Internally: handover from the previous AM, meet the technical and product teams, read the client's historical escalations and pain points. In those thirty days I do not bring a new proposal. Day thirty-one to sixty, I prove one point. I pick a high-value, contested, testable question, I align success criteria in writing, and I report the result in their measurement language. Win or lose, I give a conclusion. Day sixty-one to ninety, I scale it and institutionalize it. The experiment result becomes a growth workstream in the quarterly joint business plan. I set up the cadence: bi-weekly ops, monthly business review, quarterly planning. And I plug Google's product roadmap into their calendar, especially Q4, which by their own words is their most important promotional window: Singles Day, Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas. And one thing I will never do: I will never promise a specific ROAS or revenue number. Price is set by an auction, not by me and not by the client. What I can promise is when something will be live, when we'll have a conclusion, and how fast I respond when something breaks. Promising a number and missing it is the one thing this kind of client won't forgive. Last line: with a client like this my value is two things. Getting you to a credible conclusion faster, and getting Google moving on your behalf.

中文

Google 内部的申请记录是留存的，同一个 recruiter 能看到上一轮结果，这题躲不掉。主动、简短、不辩解地过掉它。 ■ 答法（控制在 40 秒内） 是的，我之前走过一轮 LCS 的流程，最后没有走通，卡在工作地点上，当时那个岗位的地点优先级和我的 base 对不上。 这件事我没有回避，因为它是真实的约束，不是我能力上的问题。但反过来它也说明一件事：我这半年一直在认真看 Google 的岗位，不是海投。中间我还自己去看过别的团队的机会（比如变现侧那条线），看过之后我更确定，我最想做的还是帮中国品牌出海，还是 LCS 这条线。 所以这次我第一件事就是想确认地点这件事能不能对得上。如果对得上，我认为没有别的障碍。 ■ 三条纪律 1. 不解释细节、不抱怨流程、不说"我觉得当时判断有偏差"。一句话带过事实，越短越好。 2. 不要说"上次是对方的问题"。哪怕你心里这么想。 3. 一定要把话题从"过去"拉回"这次"：确认地点 → 讲匹配度 → 结束。不要在这个话题上停留超过一分钟。 ■ 如果对方追问"那如果这次地点还是不匹配呢" 就直说："那我会如实说，不会浪费双方的时间。这也是为什么我想在第一次沟通就把它确认掉。" ■ 英文备用（如果需要英文回答） Yes — I did go through a process with Google before, and it didn't work out. It came down to location: the role's location priority and where I'm based didn't line up. I'm not going to hide that, because it was a real constraint, not a reflection of fit on the work itself. If anything it shows I've been looking at Google seriously rather than spraying applications. In between I actually looked at other teams too, and going through that made me more certain that what I want is still this: helping Chinese brands build business overseas, on this side of the house. So the first thing I want to confirm this time is whether location lines up. If it does, I don't see another obstacle.

中文

【讲法】这题考两件事：你是不是把 Google 当跳板，以及你对自己的成长有没有真实的设计。别讲"我想三年做 manager"这种纯 title 答案。 ■ 答案 我把它分成两段。 前两年，我想先把一件硬本事练扎实：成为中国出海客户里最难的那类客户的 AM。我说的"难"是很具体的，客户自己有数据科学团队、自己有归因体系、预算随时会因为一个政策变化而重构。能不能在这种客户面前站得住，不看我会不会讲产品，看我能不能在他自己的口径里赢一次实验、拿到下一次预算。这件事我至少要完整跑三轮大促周期才敢说自己会。 两年之后，我想往两个方向之一走，取决于团队需要：一个是横向地把我在某个品类（比如零售和电商 app）里积累的打法变成可复制的东西，带一批新人；另一个是纵向地做更大的客户、更复杂的跨市场盘子。 我不会说我想在几年内做到什么 title，因为说实话我现在还没做过这个岗位，谈 title 是不负责任的。我能说的是我想成为什么样的 AM：那种客户在遇到真正的生意难题时会第一时间打电话的人，而不是季度复盘时才出现的人。 ■ 为什么这么答 野心落在能力里程碑上，不落在职位上，而且承认自己还没做过这个岗。这比一个漂亮的 title 目标可信。

English · spoken script

I'd split it into two phases. The first two years, I want to get genuinely good at one hard thing: being the account manager for the hardest kind of Chinese advertiser going overseas. And "hardest" is specific here, clients who have their own data science team, their own attribution stack, and whose budget can get rebuilt overnight because a policy changed. Whether I can hold my own with a client like that doesn't depend on how well I present products. It depends on whether I can win one experiment in their own measurement language and earn the next round of budget. I think I'd need to run at least three full promotional cycles before I'd say I actually know how to do this. After that, I'd want to go one of two directions, depending on what the team needs. One is horizontal: take what I've learned inside a category, retail and commerce apps, say, and turn it into something repeatable that I can teach to newer people on the team. The other is vertical: take on bigger clients and more complex multi-market books. I'm not going to say I want to be a certain title in a certain number of years, because honestly I haven't done this job yet, and naming a title would be irresponsible. What I can tell you is what kind of account manager I want to be: the person a client calls first when they hit a real business problem, not the person who shows up at the quarterly review.
3HQ · SHEIN 与大客户线
12 道题，都是开场定位 → Clarify → 分支深挖 → 收口（12 条）

中文

【开场定位】 我接这种体量的客户，第一个月不会提任何新东西。这类客户内部比外部复杂，我一个月内能看到的只是局部，这时候提的优化说错一次，后面就没机会了。所以 90 天我这么排。 【Clarify】 我先确认三件事，因为它们会完全改变我的 90 天怎么排： ① 交接状态：这个客户是前任 AM 完整交接给我，还是我接手时团队已经空了一段时间？如果是后者，我的前 30 天要先把信任补回来，计划全部后移。 ② 盘子范围：我接的是 SHEIN 的全球预算，还是只负责某个区域（比如大中华团队对接的那部分）？跨市场的账户和单一市场的账户，玩法完全不同。 ③ 关系现状：客户现在对 Google 的态度是"想加大投入"、"维持现状"还是"正在考虑把预算挪走"？这个决定了我是进攻还是防守。 【主分支 · 假设：完整交接 + 我负责跨市场盘子 + 客户态度中性】 第一个月，只听不做。 · 客户侧三件事：一是画内部 KPI 地图，谁背什么指标、谁拍预算、谁有一票否决；二是把现有账户结构和历史实验全部读一遍，重点是那些"做过但失败了"的实验，因为失败的原因往往比成功案例信息量更大；三是问清楚他们自己的归因口径怎么算：扣不扣退货、扣不扣关税、归因窗口多少天。 · Google 内部三件事：跟前任做完整交接、认识 gTech 和技术支持对口的几位、把过去半年的工单和升级事件看一遍。 · 这 30 天我明确不提新预算、不提新产品。 第二个月，赌一个小的。 挑一个问题，标准有三个：客户内部有争议（这样结论才有观众）、能在一个月内出结果、能用客户自己的口径测量。我会挑"价值出价"这个方向，因为它正好对着它客单价下滑的痛点。事先把成功标准写成一页纸跟客户对齐，包括"什么算赢、什么算输、样本量够不够"。跑完出一份结论，输也要出结论。 第三个月，把一次实验变成一个机制。 把实验结论变成季度联合计划里的一个增长项目，配资源和节奏；建立双周运营 + 月度业务回顾 + 季度规划的节奏；把 Google 的产品路线图接进客户的日历，特别是 Q4（双十一、黑五、网一、圣诞是他们自己的财报里点名的最重要窗口）。 【分支二 · 假设：没有交接、团队空了一段时间，客户情绪负面】 顺序全部重排。前两周我只做一件事：响应性修复，把过去挂着的未解决工单、申诉、技术故障全部清一遍，给客户一份"这些问题现在的进展"的清单。信任不是靠计划赢回来的，是靠"你说的事我办成了"赢回来的。 第 3–6 周，我才会提出第一个小实验，而且一定要挑客户自己最痛、最不抱希望的那个点，因为只有这种点赢了才有说服力。 第三个月才谈年度计划和预算结构。整个节奏比正常情况慢一个月，我不会为了好看而压缩。 【分支三 · 假设：我只负责单一区域市场（比如大中华团队对接的部分）】 打法从"全球架构"变成"单点打透"。我不去碰全球账户结构，而是把我这个市场的增长故事做到极致，然后用它去影响全球盘子的分配。 具体做法：把我这个市场做成对照组，比如某个出价策略或素材方向，在我这个市场跑出可验证的结论，然后把这份结论推给负责全球盘子的同事和客户总部。大客户组织里，区域 AM 的影响力不是靠职位，是靠手里有没有一份别人做不出来的证据。 同时我要更早地把本地信息往回传：这个市场的竞争动态、政策变化、本地团队的内部矛盾，这些是全球团队拿不到的。 【Call back】 三个月走完，我手上要有三样东西：客户内部的权力地图、一次跑完并出了结论的实验、一个能延续下去的沟通节奏。前两样是给客户看的，第三样是留给自己的。

English · spoken script

With a client this size, my instinct for month one is to propose nothing new. Their org is more complicated than their account, and anything I say in the first month is built on partial information. Say it wrong once and I lose the room. First, three things I'd clarify, because they completely change how I sequence the ninety days. One, handover: am I taking this over cleanly from a previous account manager, or has the seat been empty for a while? If it's been empty, my first thirty days go to rebuilding trust and everything else moves back. Two, scope: am I covering SHEIN's global budget, or just one region, the part that the Greater China team works with? A multi-market account and a single-market account are different games. Three, where the relationship stands right now: is the client leaning in, flat, or actively considering moving budget away? That tells me whether I'm attacking or defending. Main branch, say it's a clean handover, I own a cross-market book, and the client is neutral. Month one, I listen and I don't act. On the client side, three things: map the internal KPI landscape, who owns which metric, who signs budget, who has veto; read the entire current account structure and every experiment they've run, with special attention to the failed ones, because the reason something failed usually carries more information than a success story; and pin down how their own attribution is calculated, is returns netted out, is tariff cost in, how long is the attribution window. Internally, three things: full handover, meet the technical and support people who actually touch this account, and read every escalation from the last six months. In those thirty days I explicitly do not pitch new budget and I do not pitch new products. Month two, one small bet. I pick one question, and it has to meet three tests: there's internal disagreement about it, so the conclusion has an audience; it can produce a result within a month; and it can be measured in the client's own definition. I'd pick value-based bidding, because it points straight at their basket-size problem. I write the success criteria on one page and align it up front, including what counts as a win, what counts as a loss, and whether the sample size is even enough. Then I run it and I publish a conclusion. If it loses, I still publish the conclusion. Month three, turn one experiment into a mechanism. The result becomes a growth workstream inside the quarterly joint plan, with resources and a cadence. I set up the rhythm: bi-weekly ops, monthly review, quarterly planning. And I plug Google's roadmap into their calendar, especially Q4, which their own reporting calls their most important promotional window: Singles Day, Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas. Now, branch two, say there's no handover, the seat's been empty, and the client is unhappy. I'd reorder everything. The first two weeks I do exactly one thing: responsive repair. I clear every open ticket, every appeal, every unresolved technical issue, and I hand the client a list that says here's where each one stands. Trust doesn't come back because you presented a plan; it comes back because you did the thing you said you'd do. Weeks three to six I propose the first small experiment, and I deliberately pick the thing they're most pessimistic about, because that's the only kind of win that actually persuades anyone. Only in month three do I talk about annual plans and budget structure. The whole thing runs a month slower than normal, and I won't compress it just to look decisive. Branch three, say I only own one region. Then the game changes from designing a global structure to winning one market completely. I don't touch the global account structure. I make my market's growth story undeniable and I use that to influence how the global budget gets allocated. Concretely: I turn my market into the test cell. If a bidding approach or a creative direction produces a verifiable result in my market, I take that evidence to the colleagues who own the global book and to the client's headquarters. In a large-customer organization, a regional account manager's influence doesn't come from their title, it comes from holding a piece of evidence nobody else can produce. And I push local intel back harder and earlier: competitive dynamics, policy shifts, internal tensions inside the local team. That's the stuff the global team can't see. So three things by day ninety: a map of who actually decides inside the client, one experiment that finished and produced a verdict, and a cadence that keeps running. The first two are for the client. The third one is for me.

中文

【开场定位】 先把边界划清楚：它增速掉到 1%，根子不在广告，在关税、涨价和竞争。广告能改的是投放结构，不是营收增速。这句话我会当着客户说。 【Clarify】 四件事必须先问： ①客户说的"增速掉"指的是什么口径？是 GMV、净收入、还是某个市场的收入？因为 2026 上半年它的订单量还涨了 6.4%，只是收入没涨，这两个数字对应的动作完全不同。 ② 现在的预算是哪一段在被砍？是品牌层被砍、还是效果层被砍？还是某个区域整体被砍？砍品牌层和砍效果层的应对是相反的。 ③ 客户的内部目标是什么？这一轮是要保利润、保收入、还是保用户规模？保利润和保规模的投放策略是冲突的。 ④ 时间窗：这是接下来一个季度的预算决策，还是明年的年度规划？ 【主分支 · 假设：口径是净收入、砍的主要是欧洲效果层、内部目标是保利润】 我的建议是三句话：不要整体砍，要换结构；不要按渠道砍，要按用户价值砍；把省下来的钱投到还在涨的地方。 具体三层： 第一层，效果层内部换标尺。客单价在掉，说明现在用"单次转化成本"这个标尺拉来的人，价值越来越低。我会建议把出价目标从成本换成价值，按首单价值和 90 天价值分层出价，把预算从最便宜的人群挪到最值钱的人群。短期内 CPI 会变难看，但单均收入会往上走。这一步要提前跟客户对齐"你允许我用几周换取一个更健康的收入结构"。 第二层，品牌层不能停。欧洲那边收入 -13.9% 的同时月活少了 2800 万，公司自己的解释里有一句是主动降低了线上广告投放。这是典型的恶性循环：停投 → 用户流失 → 剩下的用户更价格敏感 → 只能继续打折 → 客单价继续掉。我会建议哪怕总盘子在缩，品牌层也要保一个最低水位，因为它保的是复购和客单，不是拉新。 第三层，地理再分配。其他地区 +21.6%，主要是拉美，而且巴西本地产能已经上来了（本地化生产占当地销量约 55%，目标是年底 85%）。供给在那儿了，流量必须跟过去，否则产能是闲置的。所以我会建议把砍下来的预算按比例挪到新市场的冷启动上。 我不建议的一件事：不建议用更高的折扣去换订单量。因为它现在的问题恰恰是单均收入在掉，再打折只会让这个数字更差。 【分支二 · 假设：客户目标从保利润切换成保规模/保份额】 那我的建议会反过来一部分：价值出价仍然要做，它是修结构、不是砍量；但品牌层和效果层都不能缩，甚至要加。这时候我要跟客户算的是另一笔账，它的竞争对手（Temu 这类）如果在这个窗口继续投，它退一步丢掉的用户，后面要用更高的价格才能买回来。这份账我会用它在法国那种市场的份额变化来佐证（媒体口径 SHEIN 份额从 17.6% 掉到 9.4%，同期 Temu 升到 20.6%）。 同时我会建议把新增预算优先放在"竞争强度低、而它供给已经本地化"的市场，也就是拉美和中东，而不是在欧美跟 Temu 拼价格。 【分支三 · 假设：客户直接要求"我要把 Google 的预算砍 30% 挪给 Meta / TikTok"】 我不会第一反应去防守 Google 的预算。我会先问一个问题：你这 30% 是打算解决什么？如果是觉得 Google 的量贵，那我们要看的是"同等价值的用户在哪边更便宜"，而不是"哪边 CPI 低"，这两件事经常不一样。 然后我提一个方案：不要直接砍，做一次对照实验。拿出一个明确的市场或一条明确的产品线，两边同时跑，用客户自己的归因口径比 90 天价值，跑四周看结论。如果 Google 输了，我帮他设计怎么砍得最不伤生意；如果 Google 赢了，那 30% 就留下了。 我的立场是：我不怕比，我怕的是在没有对照的情况下按感觉砍。客户砍预算不可怕，可怕的是砍完之后没有人知道这次砍是对是错。 【Call back】 收口一句：广告救不了它的营收增速。我能做的是把拉来的人变得更值钱、把还在涨的市场跑起来、让品牌层别断档。这三件事我会当着客户讲清楚，因为装作广告能解决营收停滞，下次复盘我就没得谈了。

English · spoken script

Let me draw the boundary first, because I'd say this to the client too: growth falling to 1% isn't an advertising problem. It's tariffs, it's price rises, it's competition. What I can change is the structure of the spend, not the revenue line. Four things I'd clarify first. One, what metric is actually declining, GMV, net revenue, or one market's revenue? Because in the first half of 2026 their order volume still grew 6.4% while revenue didn't. Those two numbers call for completely different moves. Two, which layer is being cut, brand, performance, or a whole region? Cutting brand and cutting performance are opposite situations. Three, what's the internal objective this round: protect profit, protect revenue, or protect user scale? Protecting margin and protecting scale are in direct conflict. Four, the window: is this a budget decision for next quarter, or for next year's plan? Main branch, say the metric is net revenue, the cut is mostly in European performance, and the internal goal is protecting profit. My recommendation is three sentences: don't cut across the board, restructure; don't cut by channel, cut by user value; and put whatever you save into the places still growing. Three layers underneath that. Layer one, change the ruler inside performance. Basket size is falling, which means the people we're acquiring on a cost-per-acquisition ruler are worth less and less. I'd move the bid target from cost to value, bid differently by first-order value and 90-day value, and shift budget from the cheapest audience to the most valuable one. In the short run CPI gets uglier. Revenue per order goes up. And I'd align that trade explicitly up front: give me a few weeks, and I'll give you a healthier revenue structure. Layer two, don't stop brand. Europe revenue was down 13.9% while monthly actives fell by roughly 28 million, and the company's own explanation includes actively lowering online advertising spend. That's a textbook loop: stop spending, lose users, the users who remain are more price-sensitive, so you discount, so basket size falls further. I'd argue that even if the total budget shrinks, brand has to hold a floor, because brand is protecting repeat rate and basket size, not acquisition. Layer three, reallocate geographically. Rest of world was up 21.6%, mostly Latin America, and local capacity is already there, local production is about 55% of Brazil sales with an 85% target by year end. If the supply is there, the demand has to follow or that capacity sits idle. So I'd move the freed budget proportionally into new-market cold start. And the one thing I would not recommend: buying order volume with deeper discounts. Their problem is precisely that revenue per order is falling. Discounting harder makes that number worse. Branch two, say the objective flips from protecting profit to protecting scale or share. Then part of my recommendation inverts. Value-based bidding still happens, because that's fixing structure, not cutting volume. But neither brand nor performance can shrink, arguably both go up. And now the math I do with them is different: if a competitor like Temu keeps spending through this window, the users SHEIN gives up today will cost more to win back later. I'd support that with what happened in a market like France, where coverage put SHEIN's share at 17.6% down to 9.4% while Temu went to 20.6%. And I'd put incremental budget into markets where competition is lighter and their supply is already localized, LatAm and the Middle East, rather than fighting a price war with Temu in the West. Branch three, say the client says directly: I'm cutting 30% of Google budget and moving it to Meta or TikTok. My first move is not to defend Google's budget. It's to ask: what is that 30% supposed to fix? If the answer is "Google traffic is expensive," then the right comparison is where a user of equal value costs less, not which platform has the lower CPI. Those two things are often not the same. Then I propose this: don't cut, run a controlled test. Pick one market or one product line, run both sides simultaneously, compare 90-day value in their own attribution definition, four weeks, then decide. If Google loses, I'll help them design the least damaging way to cut. If Google wins, the 30% stays. My position is: I'm not afraid of the comparison. What I'm afraid of is a cut made on instinct with no control, where afterward nobody knows whether it was the right call. To close: advertising can't fix their revenue growth. What it can do is make the users they acquire worth more, get the markets that are still growing moving, and keep the brand layer from going dark. I'd say all three out loud, because if I pretend ads can fix a stalled top line, I have nothing left at the next review.

中文

【开场定位】 客单价我自己改不了，它是三个变量算出来的结果。所以第一件事是拆开看，掉的是哪一个。 【Clarify】 三问： ① 客单价掉，是"卖的件数少了"、"件的单价低了"、还是"折扣打得更狠了"？这三种原因对应完全不同的动作。 ② 掉的是所有市场一起掉，还是集中在欧美这些涨价压力大的市场？如果是区域性的，那更可能是购买力/价格弹性的问题，而不是人群质量的问题。 ③ 客户有没有按用户分层看客单价？是老客的客单掉了，还是新客的客单掉了？老客掉是忠诚度问题，新客掉是拉来的人不对。 【主分支 · 假设：主要是新客客单拉动的下滑，集中在欧美，且折扣力度在加大】 这说明一件事：我们现在拉来的人，是冲着便宜来的。我的动作分三层。 第一层，改出价标尺。只要出价目标是"最低 CPA"，系统就会去找最容易转化的人，而最容易转化的人往往就是最价格敏感的人。所以我会把标尺换成价值：把首单价值和后续价值回传给系统，让它去找"会买得更贵的人"，而不是"最容易下单的人"。这跟客户在腾讯做过的逻辑是一样的，我在欧莱雅时用品类词而不是品牌词去抓新客，本质上也是同一件事：不追最容易转化的那批人，追最有价值的那批人。 第二层，改商品结构而不是改折扣。我会建议把广告预算往高客单品类倾斜，非服装（家居、美妆、3C）已经占它品类的 35% 以上，这些品类天然客单更高。做法上是用商品 feed 和购物类的广告，把高价值品类单独建组、单独出价，而不是混在一个大盘里被低价品拉走预算。 第三层，用跨品类连带提客单。买了连衣裙的人推家居、买了手机壳的人推配件：这类连带是购物广告最擅长的，而且它不需要额外折扣。 我这三层都不靠打折，因为打折是它现在的问题本身，不是解法。 【分支二 · 假设：老客客单也在掉，是忠诚度问题】 那第一层要换。老客客单掉，通常是两件事：复购频次下降，或者连带率下降。我在欧莱雅做过一次一模一样的 deep dive，我把老客按购买最近时间和频次切开，发现大部分老客的生意来自过往大促的客群，而且复购大多止于第二次，同时跨品类连带率低于行业水平。原因是缺乏对连带的定向激励。 放到这个客户身上，我会建议两件事：一是把老客按生命周期分层做召回（不同回购周期的人用不同的触达节奏），二是用购物广告做"买了 A 的人推 B"的定向连带。这里的广告角色不是拉新，是提高每个用户的年度价值。 同时我要提醒客户一个反直觉的点：老客在广告里的 ROAS 通常很好看，但那是因为他们本来就会买，所以老客投放要看增量，不看 ROAS，否则我们会把钱花在本来就会发生的成交上。 【分支三 · 假设：客户说"我要靠更深的折扣把客单和转化拉回来"】 我会先把代价算给他看：客单价本来就掉，再加深折扣，单均收入会继续掉；而它现在的资本市场故事已经从"增长"变成"盈利能力"，毛利是资本市场盯着的指标。所以他真正需要的不是更高的转化率，是更高的单均收入。 然后我提一个替代方案：把折扣的预算，挪一部分到"价值更高的用户"和"更高客单的品类"上，做一次四周的对照，一边继续打折冲量，一边按我前面那三层跑，比 90 天的单均收入和毛利贡献，而不是比转化率。我依然不阻止他打折，我只是要求这次能分清楚哪一种做法真的带来了收入。 【Call back】 收口：客单价掉，广告侧能做的是把预算对准更值钱的人和更值钱的商品。前提是变量先拆清楚，拆不清就给方案，那是个对谁都成立的通用答案。

English · spoken script

Basket size isn't a dial I can turn. It's the output of three variables, so I start by taking it apart and finding which one is actually falling. Three questions. One: is the drop in units per order, in price per unit, or in how deep the discount is? Those call for completely different moves. Two: is it falling everywhere, or concentrated in markets under the most price pressure, like the US and Europe? If it's regional, it's more likely a purchasing-power or price-elasticity problem than a user-quality problem. Three: has the client looked at basket size by cohort? Is it existing customers' basket falling, or new customers'? Existing customers falling is a loyalty problem. New customers falling means we're acquiring the wrong people. Main branch, say it's mostly new-customer basket dragging it down, concentrated in the US and Europe, with discounting getting deeper. That tells me one thing: the people we're acquiring right now are coming for the cheap price. Three layers of action. Layer one, change the ruler. As long as the bid target is lowest CPA, the system goes and finds whoever converts most easily, and the people who convert most easily are usually the most price-sensitive. So I move the target to value: feed first-order value and downstream value back to the system, and let it go find people who buy more expensively, not people who buy most readily. This is the same logic I ran at L'Oréal, where I bought category keywords instead of brand keywords to reach new customers, deliberately not chasing the easiest convertors, chasing the most valuable ones. Layer two, change the merchandise mix, not the discount. I'd tilt budget toward higher-basket categories. Non-apparel, home, beauty, electronics, is already over 35% of their mix, and those categories naturally carry bigger baskets. Mechanically: use the product feed and shopping surfaces to put high-value categories in their own campaigns with their own bids, rather than letting them sit in one big pot where cheap items eat the budget. Layer three, lift basket through cross-category affinity. Someone who bought a dress gets shown home; someone who bought a phone case gets shown accessories. Shopping is built for this, and it doesn't require an extra discount. Notice none of those three rely on discounting, because discounting is the symptom here, not the cure. Branch two, say existing customers' basket is falling too, so this is a loyalty problem. Then layer one changes. Falling basket among existing customers usually means one of two things: repeat frequency is dropping, or cross-category attach rate is dropping. I ran exactly this deep dive at L'Oréal. I cut existing customers by recency and frequency and found that most of their business came from people acquired in past mega-promotions, that repeat purchases mostly stopped after the second one, and that cross-category attach rate was below category benchmark, because we had no targeted incentive for it. Applied here, I'd recommend two things: segment existing customers by lifecycle and召回 them on different cadences based on repurchase cycle, and use shopping to run targeted affinity, bought A, show B. Here the role of advertising isn't acquisition, it's raising annual value per user. And I'd flag one counterintuitive point: ROAS on existing customers usually looks great, but that's because they were going to buy anyway. So existing-customer spend has to be judged on incrementality, not ROAS, otherwise we're paying for conversions that would have happened for free. Branch three, say the client says: I'm going to use deeper discounts to pull basket and conversion back. First I'd price out the cost. Basket is already falling; discounting harder pushes revenue per order down further. And their story to the market has shifted from growth to profitability, margin is what investors are watching. So what they actually need isn't a higher conversion rate, it's higher revenue per order. Then I offer an alternative: move part of the discount budget into higher-value users and higher-basket categories, and run a four-week control. One side keeps discounting for volume, the other side runs my three layers. Compare 90-day revenue per order and margin contribution, not conversion rate. I'm still not blocking the discounting. I'm just asking that this time we can tell which approach actually produced revenue. To close: when basket size falls, the job isn't selling cheaper. It's pointing the budget at more valuable people and more valuable products. And that only works if I've taken the metric apart first, because otherwise I'm handing over an answer that fits any client and therefore fits none.

中文

【开场定位】 一个新市场能不能起来，广告是最后一环。前面那件事没做，投多少钱都是打水漂，所以我第一句话问的是供给。 【Clarify】 ① 供给到位了吗？这个市场的货从哪儿发，本地仓、本地工厂，还是跨境直邮？本地化生产占巴西销量约 55%，目标是年底 85%。如果供给没到位就投广告，投出来的需求会撞上履约失败和退货，钱全浪费。 ② 这是一个全新的市场，还是已经有自然流量、只是没投过广告？这两种打法完全不同：前者要教育，后者要收割。 ③ 有没有本地团队？本地团队决定了素材能不能本地化、客服能不能接住、支付和退货能不能闭环。 ④ 成功标准是什么？是安装量、首单成本、还是 90 天价值？新市场第一年最怕用拉新的 KPI 去衡量一个需要时间成熟的市场。 【主分支 · 假设：拉美某市场，本地供给已到位，有本地团队，全新市场】 我分三个阶段。 第一阶段（0–4 周）：先做"需求探测"，不要上来就铺量。用搜索和购物这类高意图的渠道，找到当地人在搜什么、搜的词跟我们现有商品库对不对得上。这一步最值钱的产出是一份"本地需求地图"：哪些品类有真实需求但我们供给不足（反馈给客户的招商团队），哪些词翻译错了（本地化问题）。这个阶段预算小，目的是买信息。 第二阶段（1–3 月）：把探测出来的高意图需求规模化，同时开上层。搜索和购物承接意图，视频和视觉发现类做心智。素材必须本地化，不是翻译，是重新拍。中东和拉美的审美、穿着场景、模特选择完全不同，用国内素材直接投是这类客户最常见的浪费。 第三阶段（3–6 月）：建立本地的测量基线。新市场最大的问题是没有历史数据，出价系统学不动。所以我要在这个阶段把价值信号（首单价值、复购）回传做扎实，并跑一次增量实验，确定这个市场的真实边际贡献，才能决定明年的预算盘子。 贯穿三个阶段的一件事：每个阶段结束我都要给客户一份"这个市场我们学到了什么"，而不只是一份消耗报表。 【分支二 · 假设：中东市场，特点是非服装品类和家庭消费占比高、季节性强（斋月等）】 节奏要围着当地的消费日历重排，而不是围着客户的全球大促日历。这个市场我最强调的是日历对齐：大促节点、宗教节日、发薪周期，这些决定了预算的脉冲。Google 侧的做法是针对节点提前 4–6 周预热，用视频和视觉发现类做心智铺垫，节点当周集中用搜索和购物收割。 另外一个具体点：这个市场 app 使用时长和视频消费占比高，素材策略应该是视频优先，而不是沿用欧美那套以静态图为主的商品广告。 【分支三 · 假设：供给还没到位（还在跨境直邮），但客户想先投广告试水】 我会明确建议：先别铺量，先用最小预算做需求验证，同时把履约体验盯死。 原因是这类市场的用户如果第一次体验是"等了三周才收到、还要自己交关税"，他不会来第二次，而这个差评会留在应用商店里。广告能把人拉进来一次，拉不回来第二次。 我会跟客户提出一个明确的顺序建议：先投一个足够小、但足以验证需求的预算（比如只投两个核心城市、只投搜索），同步把本地仓的 SKU 铺上来，等妥投时效稳定了再放量。这个建议短期会让我少卖预算，但客户会记住我说的那句话是对的，这是我在这类客户身上唯一能积累的资产。 【Call back】 收一句：我最怕的是客户拿全球大促的节奏去套一个本地市场、拿国内素材投本地用户、拿拉新的 KPI 去考核一个还在教育的市场。所以每个市场我先把供给、日历、基线排清楚，再谈预算。

English · spoken script

Whether a new market works gets decided mostly before any ad runs. If the supply side isn't ready, budget doesn't fix it. So that's the first thing I confirm. Four clarifications. One: is supply in place? Where does the inventory ship from, local warehouse, local factories, or cross-border direct? Local production is roughly 55% of Brazil sales with an 85% target by year end. If we spend on ads before supply is ready, the demand we create hits failed fulfillment and returns, and the money is wasted. Two: is this a genuinely new market, or is there already organic traffic that's just never been paid against? Those are completely different plays, one is education, the other is harvesting. Three: is there a local team? That determines whether creative can be localized, whether customer service can catch the volume, whether payments and returns close the loop. Four: what's the success metric, installs, cost per first order, or 90-day value? The classic first-year mistake is judging a market that needs time to mature using an acquisition KPI. Main branch, say a Latin American market, local supply in place, local team exists, genuinely new market. Three phases. Phase one, weeks zero to four: demand probing, not volume. Use high-intent surfaces like search and shopping to find out what people are actually searching for locally and whether our catalog matches it. The most valuable output of this phase is a local demand map: which categories have real demand but insufficient supply, which I feed back to the client's seller-recruiting team, and which keywords are mistranslated. Budget stays small. The point is buying information. Phase two, months one to three: scale the high-intent demand we found, and open the upper funnel. Search and shopping harvest intent; video and visual discovery build the perception. And creative has to be localized, not translated, re-shot. Aesthetic, wearing occasions, model selection are completely different in the Middle East and LatAm. Shipping domestic assets into these markets is the single most common waste I see with clients like this. Phase three, months three to six: build the local measurement baseline. The problem with a new market is there's no history, so bidding systems can't learn. This is where I make sure value signals, first-order value, repeat purchase, are being fed back properly, and I run an incrementality test to establish the market's true marginal contribution before anyone sets next year's budget. Across all three phases, one constant: at the end of each phase I owe the client a document called "here's what we learned about this market," not just a spend report. Branch two, say the Middle East, where non-apparel and household categories carry more weight and seasonality is strong around things like Ramadan. The calendar has to be rebuilt around local consumption, not around the client's global promotional calendar. What I'd emphasize most here is calendar alignment: promotional moments, religious holidays, payday cycles, those determine the shape of spend. Mechanically: warm up four to six weeks ahead of a moment using video and visual discovery, then concentrate search and shopping during the moment itself. And one specific point: time spent and video consumption are high in this market, so the creative strategy should be video-first rather than the static-image-led shopping creative that works in the West. Branch three, say supply isn't ready yet, still cross-border direct mail, but the client wants to test with ads anyway. My recommendation is explicit: don't go for volume, run the smallest budget that still validates demand, and watch the fulfillment experience like a hawk. Because if a user's first experience is waiting three weeks and then paying a duty on delivery, they don't come back, and the one-star review stays in the app store forever. Advertising can buy a first visit; it can't buy a second one. So I'd give the client a sequencing recommendation: run a small enough budget to validate demand, two core cities, search only, while local warehouse SKUs get stocked, then scale once delivery time is stable. That recommendation costs me budget in the short term, but clients remember who was right about the sequencing. On accounts like this, that's the only asset I can actually accumulate. To close: what scares me in a new market is a client overlaying the global promo calendar onto a local market, running domestic creative at local users, and judging a market that's still being educated on an acquisition KPI. So I set supply, calendar and baseline first, then talk budget.

中文

【开场定位】 它现在不只是一个更大的广告主，它在变成另一种公司。我看客户习惯多看一层：明年它靠什么赚钱，那才是预算的来源。 【Clarify】 ① 它 3P 业务的战略重心在哪几个市场？半托管和 POP 主要在美欧，还是也在拉美中东？这决定了新需求出现在哪个市场。 ② 它的招商对象是谁？是中国的跨境大卖家，还是海外的本土品牌？这两类的获客渠道完全不同。 ③ 它自己的站内广告系统上线到什么程度了？有没有开始对外售卖流量？ ④ 我们内部有没有已经在对接它平台业务的团队（比如招商侧、云侧、支付侧）？别重复建设，也别踩到别人的盘子。 【主分支 · 假设：3P 重心在美欧、招商对象包含中国跨境大卖家、站内广告刚起步】 我看到两个新的预算池，和一个必须提前管理的风险。 第一个预算池：B2B 招商获客。它现在要招卖家、招品牌，这本身就是一个新的广告主需求，只不过它的"客户"变成了商家和品牌，而不是消费者。这类需求会落到搜索、视频和内容这类能触达决策者的场景上。我要做的第一件事是搞清楚这件事在我们内部归谁，然后去问一个问题：这个需求现在有没有人在服务？如果没有，那是我把这个机会带进来的机会。 第二个预算池：3P 业务的消费者侧获客。它平台上的商品变多了、品类变宽了（非服装已超 35%），这些新 SKU 需要新的流量。品类越宽，搜索和购物类广告能承接的意图就越多，因为用户在搜的不再只是"连衣裙"，还有"沙发套""手机壳"。这对我们是可测量的增量：每上一个新品类，就多一批可投的查询。 第三个，风险：它自己在建站内广告系统（媒体报道它在做竞价广告和站内推广）。这件事意味着未来我们的关系里会出现一层竞合，它既是买我们广告的人，也是卖广告的人。我不认为这是威胁，但它是我必须看懂的。看懂的表现是：我不会在它面前天真地谈"帮它做零售媒体"，除非我们内部已经有明确的策略；同时我要更早感知到它的预算结构变化，如果它的广告预算里有一部分开始从"对外买量"转向"站内变现"，我需要在它发生之前就看到，而不是在季度复盘时才发现。 【分支二 · 假设：3P 的重心在新兴市场（拉美、中东），招商对象是本土卖家】 那机会的形状变了。本土卖家招商是线下 + 本地化的活，我们能帮的部分变窄，主要是品牌曝光和可信赖度的建立，也就是说，这个预算更接近品牌预算而不是效果预算。 但同时它带来一个更大的消费者侧机会：本土卖家带来的是本土供给，本土供给配上本土流量，才跑得起来。所以我会把它 3P 招商的地图，当成我消费者侧预算分配的领先指标，它在哪个市场招到了多少本土卖家，我在那个市场就该提前把流量准备好。这是我站在平台侧能看到、而客户自己的 UA 团队未必会告诉我的信息。 【分支三 · 假设：客户直接问我们"能不能帮我们做站内广告 / 零售媒体"】 我不会当场答应，也不会当场拒绝。我会先接住需求，然后做两件事：一是回到内部确认这条线我们有没有对应的产品和团队（这会牵涉到不同的产品线和商务模式，不是我一个人能定的）；二是给客户一个明确的时间承诺，什么时候给他一个明确的答复，而不是"我回去问问"。 这类客户最怕的是"我回去问问"然后没有下文。我的原则是不在现场承诺我不确定能不能做到的事，但一定承诺一个回复时间。 【Call back】 收口：看这类客户，我不能只看它今年给我多少预算，得看它正在变成什么样的公司。零售商变平台，预算池会变多，也可能有一天它自己开始卖广告。提前半年看到这层，和后知后觉是两回事。

English · spoken script

SHEIN right now isn't only becoming a bigger advertiser. It's becoming a different kind of company. When I look at a client I try to see one layer further out: what will they be earning from next year, because that's where budget comes from. Four clarifications. One: which markets is the 3P business concentrated in? Is semi-managed and POP主要在 the US and Europe, or also LatAm and the Middle East? That determines where the new demand shows up geographically. Two: who are they recruiting, Chinese cross-border sellers, or local overseas brands? Those two are reached through completely different channels. Three: how far along is their own on-site ads system? Have they started selling inventory yet? Four: internally, does someone already own the relationship to their marketplace business, seller recruiting, cloud, payments? I don't want to duplicate or step on another team's book. Main branch, say 3P is concentrated in the US and Europe, recruiting includes large Chinese cross-border sellers, and on-site ads is early. I see two new budget pools and one risk I need to manage early. First pool: B2B seller acquisition. They now need to recruit sellers and brands. That is itself a new advertiser need, except their "customer" is now a merchant, not a consumer. That kind of demand tends to land on search, video, and content environments where you can reach decision makers. The first thing I do is find out who owns this internally, and then ask a simple question: is anyone serving this need today? If nobody is, that's my opportunity to bring it in. Second pool: consumer-side acquisition for the 3P catalog. More products, wider categories, non-apparel is already over 35%, means more SKUs that need traffic. And the wider the catalog, the more query intent we can harvest, because users aren't only searching for dresses anymore, they're searching for sofa covers and phone cases. That's measurable incremental volume: every new category adds a batch of addressable queries. Third, the risk: they're building their own on-site ads business, coverage indicates they're standing up auction-based promotion and on-site placement. Which means that at some point our relationship contains a layer of competition. They buy advertising from us and they sell advertising themselves. I don't think that's a threat, but it is something I have to understand. And understanding it looks like this: I won't naively pitch "let us help you build retail media" unless there's a clear internal position on it. At the same time, I need to sense the shift in their budget structure earlier, if part of their marketing budget starts moving from buying external traffic to monetizing internally, I want to see it before it happens, not at the quarterly review. Branch two, say 3P is concentrated in emerging markets like LatAm and the Middle East, recruiting local sellers. Then the shape of the opportunity changes. Recruiting local sellers is a field and localization game, so the part we can help with narrows, it looks more like brand budget than performance budget, mostly building credibility and awareness. But it creates a bigger consumer-side opportunity: local sellers bring local supply, and local supply only works if local demand scales with it. So I'd treat their 3P recruiting map as a leading indicator for my own budget allocation, whichever market they're signing local sellers in is the market where I should have traffic ready early. That's the kind of thing I can see from the platform side that their own UA team won't necessarily tell me. Branch three, say the client asks us directly: can you help us build on-site advertising, retail media? I'm not going to say yes on the spot and I'm not going to say no on the spot. I take the need, then I do two things: internally I confirm whether we have a product and a team for this, because it touches different product lines and commercial models and it's not mine to decide alone; and I give the client a specific commitment on when they'll get a definitive answer, not "let me check and get back to you." What clients like this hate most is "let me check" followed by silence. My rule is: don't promise in the room something I'm not sure we can deliver, but always promise a response date. To close: with a client like this I can't only look at this year's budget. I have to look at what kind of company they're turning into. Retailer to platform means more pockets of budget, and it also means one day they may be selling ads themselves. Seeing that six months early is a different job from finding out later.

中文

【开场定位】 如果我答"我帮你优化账户"，这题就砸了，他们优化得比我好。我能给的只有他们自己拿不到的东西。 【Clarify】 ① 他们内部的 UA 团队是集中式的还是按市场分的？集中式的话我只需要对一个人，分散式的话我其实在做内部协调。 ② 他们自己做不做跨渠道的预算分配决策，还是只管 Google 这一块？ ③ 他们有没有自己的测量体系（自建归因/MMM）？如果有，我的任何结论都必须能在他们的体系里被验证。 【主分支 · 假设：集中式 UA 团队，只管 Google 这一块，有自建归因】 我会把我的价值说成三件他们做不了的事： 第一，横向信息。他们能看到自己，看不到别人。同一个出价策略在另一个市场、另一个品类、另一个体量的广告主身上跑成什么样，这个信息只有平台侧有。我在腾讯做行业运营时最大的感受就是这个：单个品牌永远只能看到自己的数据，而平台能看到整个行业在发生什么。这份横向信息是我每周能给他们的、他们自己花钱也买不到的东西。 第二，内部调动。他们遇到账户故障、政策申诉、产品 bug，找不到人。我能找到。这不是因为我个人能力强，是因为我知道这条路在我们内部怎么走。对他们来说，"出问题多久能解决"比"投放建议多专业"重要得多。 第三，提前预警。他们不知道我们下个季度要改什么。产品在往 AI 方向迁移、旧的广告形式在退出、测量方式在变，这些如果我提前一个季度告诉他们，他们有时间调整；如果等发生了才说，他们要救火。 我把这三件事说清楚之后，再补一句：所以我不跟你们比谁更懂 Google Ads 的操作，我提供的是你们在这家公司内部拿不到的三样东西。 【分支二 · 假设：UA 团队分散在各个市场，各自为政】 那我多一层价值：内部一致性。分散的团队最大的问题是同一个错误在不同市场重复犯，同一个成功经验传不出去。 我会做两件事：一是建立跨市场的定期同步（哪怕只是月度），把各市场的实验结果汇总成一份共享的结论库；二是主动做跨市场的横向对标，让各市场看到彼此的表现。 这在腾讯我已经做过一次：当时我负责的 24 个品牌，每家经营状况不同，我没有一个个去 cold call，而是做了一套三维分层（生意规模 × 组织架构 × 商业化投入），然后对不同层用不同的服务方式。本质就是：面对分散的对象，先建立结构，再分而治之。 【分支三 · 假设：客户直接说"我们把 Google 当成一个自助渠道，不需要 AM"】 我接受这个前提，然后重新定义我的角色：那我就不要占用你的时间做账户 review，我改成"每两周给你一份别处看不到的信息 + 一个你可以自己做主的实验建议"。 也就是说，把服务方式从"陪跑"改成"供情报 + 供资源"。因为对这种客户，最好的服务是减少服务，他们要的是我别打扰他们，但该出手时我能在。 同时我会留一个钩子：任何一个他们自己搞不定的问题（政策、申诉、跨市场数据打通、新功能白名单），我保证这条路径是通的。等他们第一次用上，关系就建立了。 【Call back】 收口：我刚接手家清纸品那会儿，客户不熟、销售也不熟，我第一个动作不是去拜访客户，是先问自己手上有什么是别人没有的。这个习惯带到 Google 一样能用。

English · spoken script

If my answer is "I'll optimize your account," I've lost already, because they optimize better than I do. The only thing I can offer is what they can't get on their own. Three clarifications. One: is their UA team centralized or split by market? If centralized I only have one counterpart; if it's split, a big part of my job is actually internal coordination. Two: do they make cross-channel allocation decisions themselves, or do they only own the Google piece? Three: do they run their own measurement, in-house attribution, MMM? If they do, every conclusion I bring has to be provable inside their system, not mine. Main branch, say centralized UA team, they own only the Google piece, and they have in-house attribution. I'd frame my value as three things they cannot do for themselves. First: horizontal information. They can see themselves; they can't see anyone else. How the same bidding strategy performs in another market, another category, another advertiser at a different scale, that only exists on the platform side. I felt this acutely at Tencent: a single brand can only ever see its own data, while the platform sees what's happening across the entire category. That horizontal view is something I can give them every week and something they cannot buy. Second: internal navigation. When they hit an account issue, a policy appeal, a product bug, they can't find the person. I can. Not because I'm personally brilliant, but because I know how the path runs inside this company. For them, "how fast does this get fixed" matters far more than "how sophisticated is your optimization advice." Third: advance warning. They don't know what we're changing next quarter. Products are moving toward AI-driven surfaces, older formats are being retired, measurement is shifting. If I tell them a quarter early, they have time to adapt. If they hear about it when it happens, they're firefighting. And then I add the line: so I'm not competing with you on who knows Google Ads better. What I'm offering is three things you cannot get from inside your own company. Branch two, say the UA teams are fragmented by market and operate independently. Then I add a fourth layer: internal consistency. The problem with fragmented teams is that the same mistake gets repeated in five markets and the same win never travels. So I do two things: set up a regular cross-market sync, even monthly, and consolidate experiment results into one shared conclusions library; and proactively run cross-market benchmarking so each market can see the others. I did a version of this at Tencent. I had twenty-four brands, all in different states. I didn't cold-call them one by one. I built a three-dimensional segmentation, business scale, org maturity, commercial investment, and then serviced each tier differently. The principle is the same: when your counterpart is fragmented, build the structure first, then divide and conquer. Branch three, say the client says directly: we treat Google as a self-serve channel, we don't need an account manager. I accept the premise and redefine the role. Then I won't take your time with account reviews. I'll switch to a bi-weekly note: information you can't get elsewhere, plus one experiment suggestion you can execute yourself if you want to. In other words, I move from "running alongside you" to "supplying intelligence and access." Because with this kind of client, the best service is less service, they want me to stay out of the way, but be available when it matters. And I leave one hook in: anything they genuinely can't solve themselves, policy, appeals, cross-market data plumbing, early access, I guarantee that path is open. The first time they use it, the relationship is built. To close: I tested this once at Tencent. When I took over the home-care category I didn't know the clients and I didn't know the salespeople, and my first move wasn't a client visit. It was asking what I had that nobody else did. That habit transfers.

中文

【开场定位】 这题不能辩。我一开口解释我们的归因更准，我就成了立场方，而客户手里是有数据的。我的做法是把"谁的归因对"换成"我们怎么一起测出来"。 【Clarify】 ① 客户说的"没有增量"，是用什么方法算的？是末次点击、是多触点归因、还是 MMM/地理实验？这三种的可信度和偏差方向完全不同。 ② 他比较的 baseline 是什么？是"不投广告"还是"投其他渠道"？很多"无增量"的结论，其实是"Google 的量被别的渠道抢走了"，那不是无增量。 ③ 他的归因窗口多长？零售 app 的决策周期短，窗口太短会把上层渠道的贡献全部抹掉。 ④ 他有没有做过真正的对照实验（关停或地理分组）？如果没做过，"没有增量"其实只是"归因模型里显示的"。 【主分支 · 假设：客户用末次点击归因，窗口短，没做过对照实验】 我不否定他的数据，我承认一个事实，然后加一层。 承认的事实：末次点击确实会低估上层和前置触点，这在零售 app 上尤其严重，一个用户可能在 YouTube 刷到过、在搜索里比过价、最后从 app 推送进来下单，末次点击会把这一单全部算给推送或者自然量。 但我不会停在"所以我们的数据更好"，因为那只是换了一个对我有利的偏见。我会提第三种做法：跑一次真正的对照实验。 具体方案：选一个可分割的维度（地理是最稳的，按城市或区域分组；如果业务不允许，就按时间窗做开关实验），设定一组关停或降量、一组维持，跑 4–6 周，对比两组的整体成交差异，不是对比各自的归因数字，是对比生意总量。这是双方都能接受的裁决方式，因为它不依赖任何一方的归因模型。 我会主动把风险讲清楚：关停实验会让短期生意受损，所以我要跟客户一起定一个可承受的量级和时长，并且事先书面约定"无论结果如何，这个结论我们都认"。 如果结果证明增量确实小，我会认，并且反过来帮他设计怎么把这部分预算挪到更有效的地方。因为在这场对话里，我唯一不能失去的是"我说的话是可信的"。 【分支二 · 假设：客户做过地理实验，数据显示 Google 增量确实低于预期】 那我就不再谈测量，转去谈结构。增量低通常有三个可能，我会逐个排查： 一是素材和落地体验，点击之后的用户体验是不是在拖转化？二是人群重叠，Google 的量是不是跟它自有流量（app 推送、社媒）高度重叠，投的是本来就会来的人？三是品类 mismatch，是不是我们在投的品类，本来就不是它的优势品类？ 第三个可能是我最想查的：如果它在某些品类上有供给优势（服装），在另一些品类上没有（比如家居、3C 还在靠第三方卖家），那投后者的增量天然会低。这种情况下我的建议不是砍预算，是换投放的品类结构。 【分支三 · 假设：客户的 measurement 团队坚持用他们的 MMM，且不接受实验】 那我换一个不依赖实验的做法：找"自然实验"。比如某个市场因为政策或运营原因，历史上出现过投放量的自然波动（关税落地那个月主动降投、某个市场临时停投），把那段时间的自然数据拿出来做前后对比。这不需要客户额外配合，也不会影响生意。 同时我会提一个更长期的建议：把 Google 的价值信号（用户价值分层、转化价值回传）接进他们自己的模型里，让他们的 MMM 用更细的颗粒度看 Google，而不是把 Google 当成一个整块的渠道变量。整块看的模型，几乎必然会低估多渠道协同的渠道。 【Call back】 收口：测量争议里最容易犯的错，是把自己的产品当立场。我先认对方数据里合理的那部分，再把争议升级成双方都能接受的裁决机制。这题上赢了争吵输了信任，后面提案一个都过不了。

English · spoken script

This one you can't argue. The second I start explaining why our attribution is more accurate, I'm an advocate and they're the one holding the data. So I move the dispute from "whose attribution is right" to "how do we test it together." Four clarifications. One: how are they computing "no incrementality", last click, multi-touch, MMM, or geo experiments? Those differ enormously in credibility and in which direction they're biased. Two: what's the baseline? No advertising at all, or advertising elsewhere? A lot of "no incrementality" findings actually mean "Google's volume got captured by another channel," which is a different statement entirely. Three: how long is their attribution window? Retail app purchase cycles are short, and a short window wipes out the contribution of every upper-funnel touch. Four: have they ever run a real controlled test, a holdout or geo split? If they haven't, "no incrementality" really means "no incrementality as shown by the attribution model." Main branch, say they're using last-click with a short window and have never run a holdout. I don't reject their data. I concede one fact, then I add a layer. The fact I concede: last-click does systematically undercount upper-funnel and earlier touchpoints, and it's especially severe for retail apps. A user might see something on YouTube, compare prices in search, and then buy after an app push, and last click gives that entire order to the push or to organic. But I don't stop at "so our number is better," because that's just swapping in a bias that favors me. I propose a third path: run an actual controlled test. Concretely: pick a splittable dimension, geography is the most robust, so city or region groups; if the business can't do that, run an on/off test over time windows. Hold down or turn off one group, keep the other, run four to six weeks, and compare total business between the groups. Not each side's attributed number, total business. This is a resolution both sides can accept because it doesn't depend on either party's attribution model. And I name the risk myself: a holdout costs short-term revenue, so I'll agree with the client on a magnitude and duration they can absorb, and I'll get written agreement up front that whatever the result is, we'll both accept it. If the result says incrementality genuinely is small, I'll accept it, and then I'll help them design how to move that budget somewhere more productive. Because in this conversation, the only thing I cannot lose is that what I say is credible. Branch two, say they have run geo experiments and Google's incrementality really is below expectation. Then I stop talking about measurement and start talking about structure. Low incrementality usually has three possible causes and I'd work through them. One, creative and landing experience, is the post-click experience killing conversion? Two, audience overlap, is Google's volume heavily overlapping with their owned traffic, app pushes and social, so we're paying for people who were coming anyway? Three, category mismatch, are we spending on categories where they don't actually have an advantage? That third one is the one I most want to check. If they have a supply advantage in apparel but not yet in home or electronics, where they're still relying on third-party sellers, then incrementality in those categories will naturally be lower. And in that case my recommendation isn't to cut budget, it's to change which categories we're buying against. Branch three, say their measurement team insists on their MMM and won't run experiments. Then I switch to something that doesn't require an experiment: find a natural experiment. If a market historically had a natural swing in spend, the month tariffs hit and they主动 cut spend, or a market that paused temporarily, pull that period and do a before-and-after on business outcomes. It needs no cooperation and it doesn't cost them revenue. And I'd make one longer-term proposal: feed Google's value signals, user value tiers, conversion value, into their own model, so their MMM sees Google at a finer granularity instead of treating it as one block channel variable. Any model that treats a multi-surface channel as a single block will almost always undercount it. To close: the easiest mistake in a measurement dispute is taking your own product's side. I concede the legitimate part of their data first, then escalate the dispute into a mechanism both sides accept. Win the argument here and lose the trust, and nothing I propose afterwards gets adopted.

中文

【开场定位】 关税这种事，客户第一反应是砍预算。但情绪和决策是两件事，他情绪上要砍，不代表砍是最优解。我要先分清他这句话是哪一层。 【Clarify】 ① 砍的是已经承诺的年度预算，还是尚未分配的？前者涉及商务承诺，处理方式完全不同。 ② 客户内部是谁做的这个决定，财务、UA、还是 CEO？如果是财务层出于现金流做的决定，我讲 ROI 是没用的，我要讲的是"怎么用更少的钱保住更多的生意"。 ③ 砍预算的覆盖范围是全部市场，还是只砍受政策影响的市场？这决定了我是重构还是收缩。 ④ 时间：是一次性砍，还是接下来两个季度都要砍？ 【主分支 · 假设：砍的是受关税影响市场的未分配预算，决定来自财务，范围是美国】 我的动作分三步，顺序不能变。 第一步，24 小时内止血。客户在情绪里的时候，我要做的是"让事情不要失控"，不是劝他改变主意。具体：给出一份"如果一定要砍 30%，最不伤生意的砍法"，优先砍那些边际效率最低的、砍掉不影响品牌层连续性的部分，而不是让他自己随手砍。这一步的价值是：即使他砍了，砍法是我参与的，后面的结果我能解释。 第二步，一周内重构，而不是等比砍。我真正的建议是"不要均摊这 30%"：把这个市场的预算按价值重新排一次，砍掉低效那一块，把剩下的集中到还能跑的地方（比如该市场的优势品类、或者转向其他正在涨的市场）。等比砍是最简单、也最伤生意的做法，因为它把好的和坏的一起砍了。 第三步，留下证据。我会把这次调整前后做成一份完整的记录：砍了什么、为什么、预期影响、四周后的实际结果。这份记录有两个作用：一是给客户内部一个交代（他们的财务需要知道这个决定带来了什么），二是等政策或市场变化时，我有数据支持"该不该把预算加回来"。 【分支二 · 假设：客户情绪很激烈，直接要求立刻关停全部投放】 我不会劝他不要关停，那是他的钱、他的生意。我会做两件事： 第一，把"关停"这件事执行得干净：明确关停范围、时间点、后续恢复的手续和时间，避免他关了之后想恢复时又要重新走一遍冷启动（恢复成本比维持成本高得多）。 第二，同时给他一个"最小维持方案"的备选：比如只保留品牌词和高意图的搜索，量很小，但能保证系统学习不中断、品牌曝光不断档。我把这个方案的成本和影响都算清楚给他，让他自己选。我的立场是：我不阻止他做决定，但我确保他是在知道代价的前提下做的。 【分支三 · 假设：砍预算其实是因为客户要把钱挪去做海外仓/本地化】 这是我唯一会主动支持的"砍预算"。因为它是在修生意的基本面，本地供给起来了，后面所有的广告效率都会变好。 这时候我要做的是帮他设计"过渡期"：本地仓铺货需要时间，这段时间广告投得太猛会撞上履约问题，所以我建议一个"先抑后扬"的节奏，过渡期适度压量、把预算留给测款和需求探测，等妥投时效稳定后集中放量。 同时我提醒一件事：本地化之后，投放的地理颗粒度要变细（从国家到区域甚至城市），因为本地仓覆盖范围是有限的。投得太广会有一部分用户看到很长的配送时效，反而伤害转化。 【Call back】 收口：拦着他别砍不是我的活。我要让这次砍变成一个能解释、能复盘、能逆转的决定。政策周期会过去，半年后他会回来问我当时那次砍对不对，那时候我手里有没有那份记录，差别很大。

English · spoken script

With a tariff shock the first instinct is always to cut. But emotion and decision are two different things. Wanting to cut doesn't make cutting the best move. I need to know which one he's expressing. Four clarifications. One: is the cut against an already-committed annual budget or against unallocated budget? The first involves commercial commitments and is handled completely differently. Two: who made the call internally, finance, UA, or the CEO? If it's finance acting on cash flow, arguing ROI is useless. What I need to argue is how to protect more business with less money. Three: is the cut across all markets or only the affected one? That determines whether I'm restructuring or shrinking. Four: is it a one-time cut or two quarters of cuts? Main branch, say it's unallocated budget in the tariff-affected market, the decision came from finance, and the scope is the US. Three steps, and the order matters. Step one, within twenty-four hours, stop the bleeding. While the client is emotional, my job isn't to talk them out of it, it's to keep the situation from getting out of control. Concretely: I hand them the least damaging way to cut 30% if they're definitely cutting, cut the lowest marginal-efficiency spend first, cut the parts that don't break brand continuity, rather than letting them cut arbitrarily. The value of this step is that even if they cut, the cut was designed with me, so I can explain the outcome afterward. Step two, within a week, restructure instead of cutting proportionally. My actual recommendation is: do not shave 30% off everything. Re-rank this market's budget by value, cut the inefficient block, and concentrate what's left on the parts that still work, their advantaged categories in that market, or markets that are still growing. A proportional cut is the easiest thing to do and the most damaging, because it cuts the good along with the bad. Step three, leave evidence. I document the whole thing: what was cut, why, expected impact, and actual results four weeks later. That record does two jobs. It gives the client's finance team an account of what their decision produced. And when policy or market conditions shift, I have the data to argue whether budget should come back. Branch two, say the client is furious and demands an immediate full pause. I won't talk them out of pausing, it's their money and their business. I do two things instead. First, I make the pause clean: define exactly what's paused, when, and what the restart process and timeline look like, so that when they want to come back they don't have to redo a cold start, because restarting costs far more than maintaining. Second, I hand them a minimum-continuity option: keep only brand terms and high-intent search, tiny volume, but enough that bidding systems don't lose their learning and brand presence doesn't go dark. I price out the cost and the impact and let them choose. My position is: I'm not blocking the decision, I'm making sure the decision is made with the cost known. Branch three, say the real reason for the cut is that they're moving money into overseas warehouses and localization. This is the one kind of budget cut I actively support, because it repairs the fundamentals, once local supply exists, every advertising dollar gets more efficient. What I do here is design the transition. Stocking local warehouses takes time, and if we spend hard during that window, the demand collides with fulfillment problems. So I recommend a suppress-then-amplify rhythm: moderate volume during transition, reserve budget for testing and demand probing, then concentrate spend once delivery times are stable. And I flag one specific thing: after localization, geographic targeting has to get finer, from country down to region or city, because local warehouse coverage is finite. If we buy too broadly, part of the audience sees an eight-day delivery estimate and conversion suffers. To close: my job isn't to stop him cutting. It's to make this cut explicable, reviewable, and reversible. Policy cycles pass. Six months later he'll ask me whether that cut was right, and whether I still have the record from that week is the whole difference.

中文
