【开场定位】 讲功能对普通客户有用，对这种客户一定失败，他比我知道得细，而且早就有判断了。我只从一个问题开始：你现在哪个问题还没解决。 【Clarify】 ① 他现在最大的痛点是什么？是量不够、是效率下滑、还是人手不够覆盖不了这么多市场？不同的痛点对应不同的产品。 ② 他对自动化的态度是什么？这类客户常常有一批人是反自动化的，他们习惯手工控词、控出价，觉得交给系统就失控了。这个态度决定了我的说服路径。 ③ 他有没有在别的平台已经用过类似的自动化产品？用过的话，他的参照系是什么（成功还是失败的经验）。 ④ 我们内部这个产品在他所在的国家/市场是否已可用、有没有已知的适配问题？这条必须问清楚，推荐了用不了是最伤信任的。 【主分支 · 假设：痛点是覆盖不过来（市场多、SKU 多），态度偏怀疑自动化，内部已可用】 我的说服路径是三步，不讲一句产品功能。 第一步，先承认他的顾虑是对的。我会直接说：交给系统确实会失去一部分控制，而且在系统学习的头几周，效率通常是变差的。我先把这件事说在前面，因为如果我不说，他会在实验第二周自己发现，那时候我就失去了可信度。 第二步，把"控制权"重新定义。我会跟他说：你失去的是关键词层的控制，但你得到的是目标层的控制，以前你管几百个词，现在你管的是"我要什么价值的用户、在哪些市场、什么样的品牌安全边界"。对他这种规模来说，前者已经管不过来了，后者才是真正该管的东西。 第三步，用他的语言设计一个受控的试点。具体：挑一个品类或市场做试点（不要全盘切），事先书面约定学习期多长时间、用什么指标判断、什么时候可以下结论，并且设好护栏（品牌词的处理、落地页的限制、地理精准度）。试点期内我每周主动给一次进展，不等他问。 我的核心一句话：我不是让你相信这个产品，我是让你用一个受控的方式自己验证一次。 【分支二 · 假设：他明确反对自动化，理由是"我们试过 PMax，效果很差"】 我不会反驳"你测得不对"，我会先问：上次是什么时候、在哪个市场、跑多久、用什么指标判断的？ 因为这类失败通常有三个原因：一是学习期没跑完就下结论（自动化产品的学习期是真实的，四周以内的结论基本不可信）；二是价值信号没接好（系统不知道哪个用户更值钱，只能按最容易转化去找人，反而拉低质量）；三是没有护栏（品牌词被吃、流量被导到很薄的落地页）。 我会针对这三个原因逐个排查，并且给一个"重试的最小方案"：只挑一个品类、接好价值信号、设好护栏、跑满一个完整周期。如果这次还是不行，我认。 我还会补一句实话：不是所有客户都适合这类产品，如果你的品类和数据结构不适合，我不会硬推。这句话反而会增加我的可信度。 【分支三 · 假设：客户很激进，想全盘立刻切到最新产品】 这是另一种风险，我会踩刹车。理由很具体：全盘切换意味着失去对照组，事后无法判断这次迁移到底带来了什么；而且大客户一旦出问题，影响的是整个季度的生意。 我的建议是"分批迁移 + 保留对照"：按市场或品类分批，每一批都留一部分旧的作为对照，跑满周期对比。这样即使某一批出问题，损失可控，而且我们始终有答案。 我会把话说明白：我支持你走得快，但我需要你能回答"这次迁移值不值"，这个答案只有在有对照的时候才存在。 【Call back】 收口：我不做布道者，做实验设计者。他不需要听我讲产品有多好，他需要一个他没法反驳的结论，而结论只能来自他自己参与设计、事先认过标准的那次实验。

English · spoken script

Describing features works on a normal client and fails on this one, because he knows the details better than I do and he's already made up his mind. So I never open with the product. I open with one question: what's still unsolved for you. Four clarifications. One: what's his biggest pain, not enough volume, declining efficiency, or not enough people to cover this many markets? Different pains point to different products. Two: what's his posture toward automation? Clients like this often have people who are actively anti-automation, they're used to controlling keywords and bids manually and feel that handing it to a system means losing control. That posture determines my whole persuasion path. Three: has he already tried an equivalent automation product on another platform, and if so, what's his reference point, did it go well or badly? Four: is this product actually available in his markets, and are there known adaptation issues? I have to confirm that internally first, because recommending something he can't turn on is the fastest way to burn trust. Main branch, say the pain is coverage, his posture is skeptical, and the product is available. Three steps, and I won't describe a single feature. Step one, concede that his concern is legitimate. I say it directly: handing things to a system does cost you some control, and during the first few weeks of learning, performance usually gets worse before it gets better. I say that up front, because if I don't, he'll discover it himself in week two and I'll have lost credibility. Step two, redefine control. What you give up is control at the keyword layer. What you get is control at the objective layer. Previously you managed several hundred keywords; now you manage what kind of user value you want, in which markets, with what brand-safety boundaries. At his scale, the first one is already unmanageable. The second one is the thing actually worth managing. Step three, design a controlled pilot in his language. Pick one category or one market, not the whole account. Agree in writing how long the learning period is, what metric decides it, when we're allowed to conclude, and set the guardrails up front: how brand terms are handled, landing page restrictions, geographic accuracy. During the pilot I proactively send a weekly update; I don't wait to be asked. And the one line I keep coming back to: I'm not asking you to believe in this product. I'm asking you to verify it yourself, in a controlled way. Branch two, say he's explicitly against automation because "we tried Performance Max and it was bad." I don't argue that he tested it wrong. I ask: when, which market, how long did it run, and what metric did you judge it on? Because failures like that usually come from three places. One, concluding before the learning period finished, the learning period is real, and anything under four weeks isn't a credible read. Two, value signals weren't connected, so the system had no idea which users were worth more and defaulted to whoever converts most easily, which drags quality down. Three, no guardrails, brand terms got cannibalized, or traffic got routed to thin landing pages. I'd work through those three and propose a minimum viable retry: one category, value signals properly connected, guardrails set, run a full cycle. If it still doesn't work, I'll accept it. And I'd add one honest sentence: not every client is a fit for this kind of product, and if your category or data structure isn't suited to it, I won't force it. That sentence actually increases my credibility rather than decreasing it. Branch three, say the client is aggressive and wants to cut everything over immediately. That's the opposite risk and I'd put my foot on the brake, for a specific reason: a full cutover destroys the control group, which means afterward nobody can tell whether the migration did anything. And on an account this size, if it goes wrong, it costs the whole quarter. My recommendation is phased migration with a held-back control: go market by market or category by category, keep part of the old setup as a comparison, run full cycles, compare. That way if a phase goes wrong the damage is contained, and we always have an answer. And I'd say it plainly: I'm fine with moving fast. What I need is for you to be able to answer whether this migration was worth it, and that answer only exists if there's a control. To close: with a sophisticated client I'm not an evangelist, I'm an experiment designer. He doesn't need to hear how good the product is. He needs a conclusion he can't argue with, and that only comes from a test he helped design and whose standard he signed off on in advance.

中文

【开场定位】 大客户里，方案本身很少是障碍。障碍是这份方案在客户内部有没有人替你说话。所以我第一件事是找人，不是改方案。 【Clarify】 ① 谁拍板？谁影响？谁否决？这三个人常常不是同一个。 ② 各团队的指标是否冲突？最常见的冲突是：UA 要量、财务要毛利、品牌要调性。 ③ 客户内部有没有一个"跨部门的决策机制"（比如周会、季度评审）？如果有，我要把我的提案送进那个机制，而不是一对一游说。 ④ 我方内部是不是也只有我一个人在对接？如果我这边也有产品、技术、分析师，我得先把我们自己的阵型排好。 【主分支 · 假设：UA 拍板、财务有否决权、品牌团队态度冷淡，指标互相冲突】 我的做法是三步。 第一步，不写一份通用提案，写三份"翻译版"。同一件事，对 UA 讲"怎么帮你拿到更多有效量"，对财务讲"怎么在不增加预算的前提下改善单位经济"，对品牌讲"怎么让投放不伤害品牌调性，并且能借势"。内容是一套，语言是三套。很多人做不好这一步，是因为他把提案写成了自己最熟悉的那一种语言，然后指望对方自己翻译，不会的。 第二步，先找第一个信徒，而不是先说服最反对的人。我会选那个"我的方案能帮他解决他自己最痛的问题"的人，先把这一份做成一个他能拿去内部讲的版本，包括他要面对的质疑和答案。也就是说，我不是说服他，我是武装他。 第三步，把提案送进他们已有的决策机制，而不是另外约一次会。如果我另起一场会，等于让所有人都多一件事；如果我塞进他们本来就有的周会或季度评审里，阻力小得多。 关键一句：在客户内部，我永远不做那个"说服所有人的人"，我做那个"让对的人有弹药去说服剩下的人"的人。 【分支二 · 假设：客户内部两派明确对立（比如 UA 要加品牌预算、财务要砍）】 我不会当和事佬，也不会站队。我会做一件事：把争议变成一个有答案的问题。 具体：把双方的分歧翻译成一个可测的假设（比如"在这两个市场增加品牌层投放，是否能在 90 天内改善留存和单均收入"），然后设计一个小规模实验，让数据来裁决。 这么做的好处是：无论结果如何，我都没得罪任何一方，因为结论不是我下的，是数据下的。而如果我不这么做，我一旦表态支持某一方，另一方就会把我当成对方的人，我后面就没法服务了。 【分支三 · 假设：最关键的那个人一直不见我（比如真正的预算拍板者）】 我不硬追。我会做两件事： 一是把我能接触到的那几层服务到极致，让他们在内部自然地提到我。大客户里，拍板者不听汇报，但他听身边人的复述，如果他的 UA 负责人每周都在他面前说"Google 那个人帮我解决了一个卡了两个月的问题"，他迟早会想见我。这是慢路，但是唯一可靠的路。 二是找"自然的见面机会"：季度评审、年度规划、大促复盘这种他本来就会出席的场合，我要争取一个议程位，并且那个议程必须讲他关心的事（生意结构、竞争格局），不是讲我的产品。 【Call back】 收口：在腾讯接手陌生行业那次，客户不熟、销售不熟，而且中台和销售的 KPI 是冲突的。我先问了自己三个问题：我能做什么、销售要什么、客户要什么，然后先搞定销售，再借销售拿到客户信息。复杂组织里，顺序很重要。

English · spoken script

On large clients the proposal is rarely the obstacle. The obstacle is whether anyone inside will speak for it. So my first move is finding that person, not polishing the deck. Four clarifications. One: who decides, who influences, who can veto? Often three different people. Two: do the teams' metrics conflict? The classic one is UA wants volume, finance wants margin, brand wants tone. Three: is there an existing cross-functional decision mechanism, a weekly, a quarterly review? If there is, I want my proposal inside that mechanism rather than lobbying one-to-one. Four: on my side, am I the only person touching this account? If we also have product, technical, and analyst people, I need to line up our own formation first. Main branch, say UA decides, finance holds veto, brand is cold, and the metrics conflict. Three steps. Step one: don't write one generic proposal, write three translations. Same substance, three languages. To UA: how this gets you more effective volume. To finance: how this improves unit economics without adding budget. To brand: how this protects tone and can actually ride the wave. Most people fail at exactly this step, because they write the deck in the one language they're most comfortable in and then expect the other side to translate. They won't. Step two: find the first believer, don't start by convincing the biggest skeptic. I pick the person whose own most painful problem my proposal solves, and I build a version he can take into his own internal meeting, including the objections he'll face and the answers. In other words, I'm not persuading him. I'm arming him. Step three: route the proposal into a decision mechanism that already exists rather than booking a new meeting. If I create a new meeting, I've added a task to everyone's week. If I slot into the weekly or quarterly review they already run, resistance is far lower. And the key line: inside a client organization, I never try to be the person who convinces everyone. I'm the person who gives the right person the ammunition to convince the rest. Branch two, say there are two openly opposed camps inside the client, for example UA wants to add brand budget and finance wants to cut it. I don't play peacemaker and I don't pick a side. I do one thing: convert the dispute into a question that has an answer. I translate their disagreement into a testable hypothesis, does adding brand-layer spend in these two markets improve retention and revenue per order within 90 days, and then I design a small experiment and let the data decide. The beauty is that whichever way it goes, I haven't made an enemy, because the conclusion came from the data, not from me. Whereas if I publicly back one side, the other side treats me as the enemy's guy and I can't service them anymore. Branch three, say the one person who matters won't meet me. I don't chase. I do two things instead. First, I service the layers I can reach so well that they start mentioning me internally. In large accounts the decision maker doesn't read reports, but he hears what the people around him repeat, if his UA lead says every week that the Google person unblocked something that had been stuck for two months, eventually he wants to meet me. It's the slow road, but it's the only reliable one. Second, I look for a natural occasion: quarterly review, annual planning, a post-campaign retrospective where he's going to be in the room anyway. I fight for an agenda slot there, and what I present has to be about what he cares about, business structure and competitive landscape, not about my product. To close: I did this once at Tencent. I took over a category where I knew neither the clients nor the salespeople, and the central team's KPI fought with sales, one on GMV and one on ad spend. I asked myself three questions first: what can I do, what does sales need, what does the client need. Then I won sales over and reached the client through them. In a complicated org the order matters.

中文

【开场定位】 "卖更多广告"和"你的增长计划里 Google 占哪一块"是两种活法，前者是销售，后者是顾问。我做年度规划走第二条。 【Clarify】 ① 客户的年度生意目标是什么？GMV、收入、还是利润？如果我连他的目标口径都不知道，我定的增长目标就是自说自话。 ② 目标是我方内部的消耗目标，还是跟客户共同制定的？这两者冲突时怎么处理，我要明确知道。 ③ 客户的预算决策周期跟我的财年对不对得上？中国客户常常按自然年或财年，Google 按自己的季度，这两个错位会导致年底突击。 ④ 上一年的目标是怎么定的、完成了没有？如果去年定得太激进没完成，今年我要先修复可信度。 【主分支 · 假设：客户目标是收入与利润并重、双方共同制定、周期基本对齐】 我分四步。 第一步，从客户的目标倒推，不从我的产品出发。我会先拿到他的生意拆解：收入增长要来自哪些市场、哪些品类、是新客还是老客。然后我只做一件事，找出其中"哪几块是广告能影响的"，把不能影响的部分明确划掉。这一步很重要：如果我假装所有增长都能靠广告，我在年底一定会露馅。 第二步，把增长拆成三类来源，每类给不同的增长逻辑：一是存量效率（同样的预算买更多价值），二是结构迁移（把预算从低效的市场/品类挪到高效的），三是新增盘子（新市场、新品类上线带来的全新预算）。这三类里，第三类才是真正的增长，前两类是优化，我要对客户讲清楚这个区别，否则他会把"优化省下来的钱"当成"增长"。 第三步，跟客户共同设定三个数字：一个基础目标（大概率能完成）、一个 stretch 目标（要靠新项目才够得到）、一个明确的"什么情况下这个目标不成立"（比如政策变化、供给问题）。第三个数字是最容易漏的，但它决定了年底复盘时我们是在争论还是在复盘。 第四步，把年度目标落成季度项目制：每个季度 1–2 个明确的项目，每个项目有负责人、成功标准、时间点。年度数字是结果，季度项目才是过程。 【分支二 · 假设：客户的目标明显不现实（比如市场在萎缩但要求增长 30%）】 我不会直接说"做不到"，我会先把分歧具体化：给出一份"按现在的效率，这个目标需要多少预算、什么样的转化效率"的倒推，然后用数据指出缺口在哪。 然后我给三个选项，让他自己选：一是降低目标到合理区间；二是保持目标但增加预算（前提是我能证明边际效率还在）；三是保持目标和预算，但改变结构（比如砍掉低效市场、押注新市场）。 我真正要避免的是：当场答应一个我做不到的数字。因为答应的那一刻我很舒服，年底我一定难受，而且客户会记得是他"逼"我答应的，不会记得当时是他要求的目标。 【分支三 · 假设：我方内部目标远高于客户愿意给的预算】 这是我作为 AM 最真实的张力。我的处理方式是：把内部目标当成"我要创造多少增量"，而不是"我要卖出多少"。 具体做法：把缺口拆开看，哪部分是可以靠新项目创造的（新市场冷启动、新品类上线、新产品采用），哪部分是需要客户新增预算的。前一部分我可以通过证明价值来争取，后一部分我要在内部提前沟通，而不是到季度末才暴露。 我也会反过来管理内部预期：如果我判断某个客户的增长空间就是有限的，我会尽早把依据摆出来（市场数据、客户财报、竞争格局），而不是等到最后一刻才说做不到。提前说不好的消息，是我在内部保住可信度的方式。 【Call back】 收口：我最在意目标的来源。从我的 quota 倒推出来的目标迟早会失去客户，从客户的生意拆解里长出来的，我和他就是一边的。这个区别决定这个客户跟我合作一年还是三年。

English · spoken script

"Sell more advertising" and "here's the piece Google occupies in your growth plan" are two different jobs. One is a salesperson, the other is an advisor. I do the second one. Four clarifications. One: what is the client's annual business objective, GMV, revenue, or profit? If I don't know which, any growth target I set is just me talking to myself. Two: is the target an internal consumption number or something we build together with the client? When those two conflict, I need to know how it gets resolved. Three: does the client's budget cycle line up with our fiscal year? Chinese companies often plan on calendar or fiscal years while Google runs on its own quarters, and that misalignment creates year-end scrambles. Four: how was last year's target set and did it land? If last year's was too aggressive and missed, my first job this year is rebuilding credibility. Main branch, say the client targets both revenue and profit, the plan is jointly built, and cycles roughly align. Four steps. Step one: work backward from the client's goal, not forward from my products. I get their business decomposition first: which markets and categories is the growth supposed to come from, new customers or existing. Then I do exactly one thing, identify which of those blocks advertising can actually move, and explicitly cross out the ones it can't. That step matters, because if I pretend all growth comes from advertising, I'll be exposed at year end. Step two: split growth into three sources with different logic. One, efficiency on existing spend, more value from the same budget. Two, structural shift, moving budget from inefficient markets or categories to efficient ones. Three, genuinely new budget, new markets, new categories coming online. Only the third one is actual growth; the first two are optimization. I say that out loud to the client, because otherwise he'll book the money optimization saved as if it were growth. Step three: jointly set three numbers. A base target we'll most likely hit. A stretch target that requires new initiatives. And, the one people always forget, a defined set of conditions under which the target no longer holds: a policy change, a supply problem. That third number is what determines whether year-end is a review or an argument. Step four: convert the annual number into quarterly initiatives. One or two named projects per quarter, each with an owner, a success criterion, and a date. The annual number is the output; the quarterly projects are the process. Branch two, say the client's target is clearly unrealistic, the market is contracting but they want 30% growth. I don't say "impossible." I make the gap concrete: I show the reverse calculation, here's how much budget and what conversion efficiency that target requires at current performance, and here's where the gap is. Then I give three options and let them choose: lower the target to a defensible range; keep the target and add budget, assuming I can show marginal efficiency still holds; or keep target and budget but change the structure, exit inefficient markets, concentrate on new ones. What I absolutely avoid is agreeing in the room to a number I can't deliver. Agreeing feels good for ten seconds and then hurts for three quarters, and the client will remember that he pushed me into it, not that he asked for the number. Branch three, say my internal number is well above what the client is willing to commit. This is the most real tension an AM faces. My framing: I treat the internal target as "how much incremental value do I need to create," not "how much do I need to sell." Mechanically, I split the gap, how much can be created through new initiatives, new market launches, new category onboarding, new product adoption; and how much genuinely requires new client budget. The first part I can fight for by proving value. The second part I escalate internally early rather than exposing it in the last week of the quarter. And I manage internal expectations in the other direction too: if I believe a client's growth ceiling is genuinely limited, I put the evidence on the table early, market data, their financials, competitive landscape, rather than saying at the deadline that it can't be done. Delivering bad news early is how I keep credibility internally. To close: the thing I care about most in annual planning is where the number came from. Back-solved from my quota, and I lose the client eventually. Grown out of taking their business apart, and we're on the same side. That difference is whether this account lasts one year or three.

中文

【开场定位】 一个大客户加一堆小客户，最容易出的事是大客户把我的时间吃光。所以我不问怎么服务好每一个，我问时间花在哪产出最高。 【Clarify】 ① 这批客户的分布是什么形状？是几个中型加一堆小客，还是大量差不多大小的？形状决定分层方式。 ② 他们的问题是不是高度同质？（跨境 app retail 客户通常是的：同样的市场、同样的出价问题、同样的素材问题）同质化程度越高，1-to-many 越有效。 ③ 我有没有内部资源可用（分析师、技术支持、代理商）？有没有可以借力的合作伙伴？ ④ 公司对这些客户的期望是"维持"还是"增长"？如果是维持，我的打法应该是保住基本盘、提高效率；如果是增长，我要找出值得押注的那几个。 【主分支 · 假设：几个中型 + 一堆小客，问题高度同质，有内部支持，期望是增长】 我的做法分三层。 第一层，分层，但不是只按消耗分层。我在腾讯做过一次一模一样的事：当时我手上有 24 个品牌，我没有按 GMV 降序排，而是三维地看，生意规模、组织架构成熟度、商业化投入意愿。这样切出来的客户是四种，每种的服务方式不同：规模大且投入意愿高的，深度定制；规模大但意愿低的，先找痛点再谈投入；规模小但意愿高的，给标准化方案 + 轻咨询；规模小且意愿低的，用规模化手段覆盖。 放到这批客户身上我会用同样的逻辑：按"生意潜力 × 组织成熟度 × 投入意愿"切，而不是只看现在的消耗。因为只看消耗，我会永远服务昨天最大的客户，错过明天最大的客户。 第二个层，1-to-many。这类客户的问题高度相似，所以我不会一个个讲同样的东西。具体做法：把共性问题做成定期的小规模分享（webinar 或者群），把最佳实践做成可复制的模板（新市场冷启动清单、出价迁移清单、素材规格表），一对一的会议留给真正个性化的问题。这样我的 1v1 时间才值钱。 第三层，用自动化把重复性工作消化掉。这是我个人最擅长的一块，我自己是做 AI 产品的，日常就在搭 agent 和自动化工作流。具体能自动化的东西很多：批量账户健康检查、异常预警（消耗突降、报错率上升）、常规报表的生成和分发、素材规格检查。这些做完之后，我的人力才能集中在判断和关系上。 【分支二 · 假设：公司对这些客户的期望是"维持"，不增长】 那我的做法会变：不追求每个客户都深度服务，而是保住基本盘不出事 + 挑两三个押注。 "不出事"靠系统化：建立异常预警（谁的量突然掉了、谁的账户有报错、谁快到期了），用自动化的方式覆盖，把风险在客户来问之前就处理掉，因为这类客户流失往往不是因为效果不好，是因为"出了问题没人管"。 "押注"靠判断：我会在这批客户里挑 2–3 个有明确增长信号的（比如拿到新融资、上了新品类、进了新市场），把 80% 的额外精力放上去。剩下的用规模化手段维持。 【分支三 · 假设：某个中型客户突然长成大客户了】 这是好事，但也是最容易出事的时刻，因为我的服务方式要换，而客户会觉得"你以前服务得挺好的，怎么变了"。 我的处理是：主动跟客户谈一次"服务升级"，明确告诉他我认为他已经到了需要不同服务方式的阶段，然后列出我会增加什么（更频繁的沟通、专属的实验设计、接入更多内部资源），同时问清楚他希望增加什么。这比默默升级要好，因为客户能感知到"你在为我规划"，而不是"你突然忙了"。 同时我会在内部做一件事：确认这个客户是不是该交到更高一层的团队（如果公司有这个机制）。如果有，我要提前跟客户交代清楚，而不是等公司来安排，因为交接期是客户最容易流失的时候。 【Call back】 收口：混合盘子里我不追求服务好每一个，我关心时间花在哪产出最高。能规模化的规模化，能自动化的自动化，剩下的人力全押在少数值得深服的客户上。

English · spoken script

One very large client plus a long tail, and the failure mode is the large client eating every hour I have. So the question I ask isn't how to service everyone well. It's where my hours produce the most. Four clarifications. One: what shape is this book, a few mid-size plus many small, or a lot of roughly equal accounts? The shape determines how I segment. Two: are their problems highly homogeneous? Cross-border commerce app clients usually are, same markets, same bidding issues, same creative problems. The more homogeneous, the more effective one-to-many becomes. Three: what internal resources do I have, analysts, technical support, agencies? Four: is the expectation for these accounts to hold or to grow? If it's hold, my play is protect the base and raise efficiency. If it's grow, I have to pick the few worth betting on. Main branch, say a few mid-size plus many small, homogeneous problems, internal support available, expectation is growth. Three layers. Layer one, segment, but not by spend alone. I did exactly this at Tencent. I had twenty-four brands and I refused to sort them by GMV. I used three dimensions: business scale, how mature their organization was, and their willingness to invest commercially. That produced four tiers, each with a different service model: large and willing gets deep customization; large but unwilling gets pain-point discovery before any investment conversation; small but willing gets a standardized playbook plus light consulting; small and unwilling gets scaled coverage. Same logic here: I'd cut by business potential, org maturity, and willingness to invest, not by current spend. Because if I only look at spend, I'll spend forever servicing yesterday's biggest client and miss tomorrow's. Layer two, one-to-many. Their problems overlap heavily, so I refuse to repeat the same conversation one account at a time. Concretely: recurring small-group sessions on the shared problems, replicable templates for best practices, a new-market launch checklist, a bidding migration checklist, creative spec sheets, and I reserve one-to-one time for genuinely account-specific issues. That's the only way my 1v1 time stays valuable. Layer three, automate the repetitive work away. This is the part I'm personally strongest at, I come from an AI product background and I build agents and automated workflows as a normal habit. There's a lot that can be automated here: bulk account health checks, anomaly alerts when spend drops or error rates spike, routine report generation and distribution, creative spec validation. Once that's off my plate, my human hours go to judgment and relationships. Branch two, say the expectation for these accounts is hold, not grow. Then the play changes: don't try to service every account deeply; protect the base from breaking and pick two or three to bet on. Protecting the base is a systems job: build anomaly alerts, whose volume dropped, whose account is erroring, who's coming up for renewal, and handle risk before the client asks. Because these accounts usually don't churn because performance was bad; they churn because something broke and nobody was there. Betting is a judgment job: I pick two or three accounts with a clear growth signal, new funding, new category, new market, and put 80% of my discretionary effort there. The rest get scaled coverage. Branch three, say a mid-size account suddenly becomes a large one. That's good news, and it's also the moment most likely to go wrong, because my service model has to change and the client's reaction is "you were great before, what happened." My approach: proactively have a service-upgrade conversation. Tell them directly that I think they've reached the stage that needs a different model, list exactly what I'll add, more frequent contact, dedicated experiment design, more internal resources, and then ask what they want added. That's much better than quietly upgrading, because the client experiences it as "you're planning for me" rather than "you got busy." And internally I do one thing: confirm whether this account should graduate to a different team, if that mechanism exists. If it does, I tell the client early rather than waiting for the company to arrange it, because handover is exactly when accounts are most likely to churn. To close: with a mixed book the judgment that matters isn't how to service everyone well. It's where my hours produce the most. Scale what can be scaled, automate what can be automated, and put the remaining human hours on the few accounts that actually earn deep service.
4HQ · 通用客户经营
16 道客户管理题，换客户群也照样成立，跟上一节有三道主题重叠（16 条）

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 15 个名额给谁，不看客户大小，看这个季度我要什么。目标一定，名单从打分卡里推出来；目标一改，名单换人。 【Clarify · 先问再答】动手分配之前，我想先确认五件事：第一，这 100 个客户的构成，游戏、短剧、工具各占多少？处在什么生命周期阶段？第二，我接手的 portfolio 有没有现成的分层？消耗分布是长尾还是头部集中？投放漏斗上，开户、首耗、跑稳、在增长的客户各有多少家？第三，我的资源，就我一个人，还是有数据工具、内部专家、活动预算？第四，这个季度最关键的目标到底是什么，消耗的绝对增长、新客激活数，还是毕业率？第五，行业是顺风还是逆风？每一个答案都会改变我的分配逻辑。 【主分支：头部集中 + 漏斗卡在开户到首耗 + 单人 + 目标绝对增长 + 温和顺风，最常见组合】 拿到答案我逐项处理： 第一，客户构成，假设游戏四成、短剧三成、工具三成。构成决定打法差异：游戏要抓大促节点，短剧要抓素材迭代速度，工具要抓 eCPM 变现周期，名额不能按品类平均分，要按增长弹性分。 第二，portfolio 现状，已有分层，但漏斗卡在开户到首耗之间。这说明漏斗中段在漏水：如果 15 个名额全给头部，中段客户永远跑不上来，整个盘子的增长上限被锁死。所以名额里必须留出几个给"已开户但没跑起来"里潜力最高的客户。 第三，资源，就我一个人加标准工具。单人意味着 1v1 名额极其昂贵，所以每个名额必须配一个条件：服务这一家，要能沉淀出同品类可复用的打法，名额自带复利。 第四，目标，消耗绝对增长。名额优先给"基数大、Upside 空间也大"的客户：不是最大的（基数大但饱和），也不是最小的（弹性大但基数撑不起量），是中间偏上那一档。 第五，行业温和顺风。顺风意味着 headroom 来自市场本身，优先选"站在风口品类里、但 Google 占他预算比例还低"的客户，帮他接住红利。 综合起来：我按一张打分卡排名单，消耗基数 40%、增长弹性 30%、Google 份额空间 20%、打法可复用性 10%。15 个名额的分配：8 家头部保基本盘，4 家卡点疏通（开户未首耗里潜分最高的），3 家品类样板（每个主力品类一家，沉淀方法论）。名单每月复评一次，目标不变但客户状态会变。 【分支二：目标换成毕业率 + 有专家资源，口径一变名单就变】 同样的五问，答案变了： 第一，构成假设不变，但用途变了，毕业视角下，品类不再是打法标签，而是"上一层团队缺什么"的标签：上层如果缺游戏大客，我的毕业名额优先给游戏里接近门槛的。 第二，分层已有、漏斗健康，不需要留卡点名额，名额全部可以给增长。 第三，这次有内部专家资源，批量动作有人兜底，1v1 名额不用浪费在标准问题上，可以全部投给"离毕业线 20% 以内"的客户：推一把就毕业，一个名额换一个毕业指标。 第四，目标是毕业率，名额逻辑彻底改变：不给最大的，给最接近门槛的。毕业一个算一个，大客户留给下一年。 第五，顺风加速毕业，市场红利会自然把临界客户推过线，名额给"差临门一脚"的，顺风就是我的杠杆。 综合：名单变成"离毕业线最近的 10 家 + 3 家品类样板 + 2 家战略性大客"，后两家是明年的毕业储备。同一个组合，目标从增长换成毕业，13 个名额换人。 【分支三：没有分层、漏斗数据缺失，先修系统再选人】 第一，构成未知，品类比例都不清楚，任何分配都是盲分。 第二，没有分层：这是最关键的缺口：第一动作不是选 15 家，是建分层。用一周时间跑一个体量乘增速的三乘三矩阵，把 100 家摆进九宫格，名单从矩阵里长出来。 第三，单人，分层不靠逐家访谈，靠数据自动化：消耗、增速、Google 份额三个字段批量拉，人工只复核边缘案例。 第四，目标假设还是绝对增长。 第五，假设顺风。 综合：第一周建分层，第二周才出名单。先诊断后开药，诊断本身就是 deliverable，我会同步给老板看：这张矩阵就是我的 coverage 地图。 【分支四：单人无工具 + 逆风，防守型分配】 第三，资源：只有我一个人、没有工具，我连全量扫描都做不到，名额必须向"守存量"倾斜：先守住会掉的大户。 第五，行业逆风，Upside 只能来自份额战：名额给"竞品在抢、但关系在我手上"的客户，逆风期丢一家大户比错过三个机会更伤。 第一，客户构成，游戏四成、短剧三成、工具三成，同主分支。 第二，portfolio 已有分层、漏斗卡在开户到首耗，同主分支。 第四，季度目标，消耗绝对增长，同主分支。 综合：防守型名单，8 家守存量（有流失风险的大户），5 家打份额（竞品渗透中），2 家逆势加注（逆风里还在增长的异类客户，他们的打法值得深度研究复制）。 【Call back】 所以答案落在"这个季度的目标最需要哪 15 家"。目标一定，名单从打分卡推出来；目标一改，名单换人。

English 口语逐字稿

Fifteen slots, and who gets them isn't about client size. It's about what I need this quarter. Once the goal is fixed the list falls out of a scorecard; change the goal and the list changes. [Clarify — before I jump in] So before I allocate anything, there are five things I'd want to understand. First, the mix of these hundred clients, how much gaming, how much short-drama, how much utility, and what lifecycle stages are they at? Second, the portfolio itself, is there existing tiering? Is spend long-tail or top-heavy? And across the funnel, opened, first spend, steady, growing, how many at each stage? Third, my resources, am I solo, or do I have tooling, specialists, budget? Fourth, and this is the big one, what exactly is this quarter's goal? Absolute spend growth, new-client activations, or graduation rate? And fifth, is the industry in a tailwind or a headwind? Because honestly, every one of these answers changes my allocation logic. [Main branch — the most common combination] So let's say the answers come back like this: top-heavy structure, the funnel is stuck between account-opened and first-spend, I'm solo, the goal is absolute spend growth, and we're in a mild tailwind. Here's how I'd work through it. First, the mix, say forty percent gaming, thirty short-drama, thirty utility. The mix decides the play: gaming keys on promo nodes, short-drama on creative iteration speed, utility on eCPM monetization cycles. So slots don't split evenly by category, they split by growth elasticity. Second, the portfolio, tiering exists, but the funnel is leaking between opened and first-spend. And that matters because if all fifteen slots go to the head, the mid-funnel clients never climb up, and the book's growth ceiling stays locked. So several slots have to go to the highest-potential "opened but never spent" clients. Third, resources, solo with standard tooling. Solo means my 1v1 time is extremely expensive, so every slot carries one condition: serving this client must produce a playbook I can reuse across the same category. Slots have to compound. Fourth, the goal, absolute spend growth. So slots go to clients with both a large base and real Upside headroom. Not the biggest, big base but saturated. Not the smallest, elastic but can't carry volume. The upper-middle band. And fifth, a mild tailwind, which means headroom comes from the market itself. So I'd prioritize clients standing in a rising category where Google's share of their wallet is still low. Help them catch the tailwind. So putting it all together: I'd rank everyone with a scorecard, spend base at forty percent, growth elasticity thirty, Google share headroom twenty, playbook reusability ten. And the fifteen slots would go: eight to head accounts to defend the base, four to bottleneck-breakers, the best of the opened-but-never-spent, and three to category templates, one per major category, to distill the methodology. And I'd re-score the list monthly, because the goal holds but client states move. [Branch two] Now, if the goal switches to graduation rate and I've got specialist resources, then the picture changes. Same five questions. The mix is unchanged, but its use flips, through a graduation lens, category becomes a "what does the tier above lack" label. The tiering exists and the funnel is healthy, so no bottleneck slots, everything can go to growth. And because I have specialists backing me up, my 1v1 slots don't get wasted on standard problems, they all go to clients within twenty percent of the graduation line. One push and they graduate. The goal being graduation rate inverts the slot logic completely: not the biggest, but the nearest to the bar. And the tailwind just accelerates it, market momentum naturally pushes borderline clients over the line. So the list becomes: ten nearest the graduation line, three category templates, and two strategic majors, those last two are next year's graduation pipeline. Same portfolio, goal swapped, thirteen slots change hands. [Branch three] But what if there's no tiering and the funnel data is missing? Then the mix is unknown, and without category ratios, any allocation is blind allocation. The first move isn't picking fifteen, it's building the tiering. One week to run a base-times-velocity three-by-three matrix, place all hundred clients in nine cells, and let the list grow out of the matrix. And since I'm solo, the tiering doesn't come from interviews, it comes from data: spend, velocity, Google share pulled in batch, with humans only reviewing the edge cases. Week one builds the tiering, week two produces the list. Diagnose before prescribing, and the diagnosis itself is a deliverable I'd show my manager: this matrix is my coverage map. [Branch four] And if I'm solo with no tooling, in a headwind? Then I can't even run a full scan, so slots tilt toward defending the base, first secure the big accounts at risk of churning. Mix and tiering as before. And in a headwind, Upside only comes from a share war, so slots go to clients the competitors are courting, but where the relationship is mine. In a headwind, losing one major hurts more than missing three opportunities. So: a defensive list, eight to defend, five to fight for share, and two contrarian bets on the rare clients still growing against the wind. So the answer was never which fifteen are the best. It's which fifteen this quarter's goal needs most. Fix the goal and the list derives from the scorecard; change the goal and thirteen slots change hands.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 落后本身不可怕，可怕的是不知道缺口由什么构成。所以第一动作是拆，不是补。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要确认五件事：第一，40% 指的是已实现消耗，还是 pipeline 机会金额？第二，缺口集中在某几个客户身上，单点，还是三十家各掉一点：面状？第三，如果是面状的，客户里有多少家正在 Meta 或 TikTok 上加大投放？第四，历史季节性，下半年通常贡献多少？第五，目标有没有商量余地？ 【主分支：已实现消耗 + 面状缺口 + 竞品在抢 + 下半年占 60% + 目标不动，最常见也最凶险的组合】 逐项处理： 第一，40% 是已实现消耗，真实的落后，不是口径幻觉。这决定我后面所有动作都是"补业绩"而不是"重新定义业绩"。 第二，面状缺口，三十家各掉一点。这说明不是救某一家的事，是系统性问题：先按客户分层看是哪一层掉的。如果掉的主要在腰部，那是我的批量覆盖出了问题；如果均匀地掉，那是市场或产品的问题。 第三，一半客户在 Meta、TikTok 加投，这是份额战：量没有消失，只是换了地方。主攻"竞品在加投、但 Google 占比还低"的客户，竞品渠道的波动期恰恰是进场谈份额的最好时机。 第四，下半年历史占 60%，季节性站在我这边：剩下的时间窗本来就承载大头，所以缺口没有表面那么吓人。但反过来，动作必须前置：Q3 谈下的预算才能在 Q4 变成消耗，现在不动 Q4 就没了。 第五，目标不动，背水一战，那就把缺口拆成可验证的三块：季节性自然回归能补 X 个点、份额战能抢 Y 个点、沉睡激活能贡献 Z 个点。三块加总对着缺口，哪块缺口最大资源压哪块。 综合：周度追踪三块各自的进度；优先打份额战，因为它见效最快、且竞品波动期是唯一窗口。季节性是等来的，份额是抢来的，激活是磨出来的，三者的时间窗口完全不同。 【分支二：单点缺口，两三个大户掉了】 第一，已实现消耗。 第二，缺口集中在两三个大户，那这不是 pipeline 修复，是客户抢救：一家大户回来，缺口就关一半。逐家做 deep dive：账户诊断（量、成本、结构变化）加业务诊断（他们自己的生意是不是出了问题）。是账户问题我修账户，是生意问题我帮他省着投，缩量不缩服务，等他缓过来。 第三，与竞品无关，排除份额战，纯客户侧问题。 第四，季节性中性。 第五，目标可谈但要带着方案谈：如果 deep dive 结论是"客户业务结构性下滑、短期回不来"，我拿着这个结论去谈目标调整，筹码是诊断数据，不是抱怨。 综合：单点缺口的解法在客户身上，不在我的 pipeline 方法论上，这时候方法论再漂亮也没用，救人就是救数字。 【分支三：40% 是 pipeline 金额，口径幻觉】 第一，如果 40% 指的是 pipeline 机会金额，这个数本身就靠不住：机会金额是"我认为会成的"，已实现可能更低。第一动作是给 pipeline 做体检：每个 opportunity 标注阶段和成单概率，加权之后看真实数字。 第二，面状。 第三，假设竞品压力中等。 第四，季节性数据要重拉。 第五，目标先不动。 综合：先修 pipeline 管理，阶段定义、概率加权、每周更新；修完才知道真实缺口是 40% 还是 55%。在这一步之前，任何"补救动作"都是在错误的数字上使劲。 【分支四：目标可谈，把缺口变成资源谈判】 第五，如果目标有商量余地，谈判要带三样东西：缺口的结构拆解、市场环境数据、以及明确的资源请求（"要么调整目标，要么给我额外资源"）。缺口结构决定我请求什么：竞品抢量型的缺口要弹药（beta 名额、市场费用），市场下行的缺口要口径（"跑赢市场"替代"绝对增长"）。 第一，40% 指的是已实现消耗，同主分支。 第二，缺口是面状的，三十家各掉一点，同主分支。 第三，一半客户正在 Meta、TikTok 加投，同主分支。 第四，下半年历史贡献 60%，同主分支。 综合：谈判的姿势不是"我做不到"，而是"我算过了，这样能最大程度接近，前提是补上这个资源缺口"。老板拒绝资源也拒绝不了数学。 【Call back】 我交出去的是一张缺口成分表：季节性补几点、份额战抢几点、激活磨几点、口径松几点，每一项都有数字、有动作、有检查点。拆完，恐惧少一半，另一半变成待办。

English 口语逐字稿

Being behind isn't the problem. Not knowing what the gap is made of is. So my first move is decomposition, not activity. [Clarify — before I answer] Five things I'd want to know. One: does the forty percent mean realized spend, or pipeline opportunity value? Two: is the gap concentrated in a few accounts, a point gap, or thirty clients each down a bit, a surface gap? Three: if it's surface-level, how many of those clients are increasing spend on Meta or TikTok right now? Four: what does seasonality say, how much does the back half usually deliver? And five: is the target negotiable? [Main branch — the most common and most dangerous combination] So let's say the answers come back: it's realized spend, the gap is surface-level, half the clients are scaling up on Meta and TikTok, the back half historically carries sixty percent, and the target is locked. Here's how I'd work through it. First, it's realized spend. So this is a real shortfall, not a measurement artifact. Everything that follows is about closing real revenue, not redefining it. Second, the gap is surface-level, thirty clients each down a little. So this isn't about saving one account, it's systemic. I'd split the gap by tier first: if the drop concentrates in the middle tier, my batch coverage broke; if it's even across the book, it's market or product. Third, half the clients are scaling up on Meta and TikTok. So this is a share war: the volume didn't vanish, it moved. And that means I go after the clients increasing competitor spend while Google's share is still low, because a competitor channel's turbulence is exactly the best window to walk in and pitch share. Fourth, the back half carries sixty percent. So seasonality is actually on my side: the remaining window carries the bulk anyway, so the gap is less scary than it looks. But the flip side is that actions must front-load, budget closed in Q3 becomes Q4 spend. Move now, or Q4 is gone. And fifth, the target is locked. So it's a fight to the finish. I'd decompose the gap into three verifiable blocks: seasonal reversion recovers some points, the share war takes some, dormant reactivation contributes some. Sum the blocks against the gap, and whichever block covers the most gap gets the most resources. So putting it together: I'd track all three blocks weekly, and lead with the share war, because it's the fastest to pay off, and the competitor-turbulence window is the only window. Seasonality is waited for, share is seized, reactivation is ground out, three different clocks. [Branch two] Now, if it's a point gap, two or three majors dropped, then the realized spend is real, but the story is different. The gap sits in a few majors, so this isn't pipeline repair, it's client rescue: one major coming back closes half the gap. I'd deep-dive each one: account diagnosis, volume, cost, structure shifts, plus business diagnosis: is their own business in trouble? If it's an account problem, I fix the account. If it's a business problem, I help them spend leaner, shrink the spend, never the service, and be first in mind when they recover. Competitor pressure, assume none, this is client-side. Seasonality, neutral. And the target, negotiable, but only with a plan: if the deep dive says "structural decline, no near-term return," I take that conclusion to the table. The chips are diagnostic data, not complaints. [Branch three] But what if the forty percent is pipeline value? Then the number itself is unreliable, opportunity value is "what I think will close," and realized is probably lower. So first move: audit the pipeline, stage and close-probability tagged on every opportunity, weighted, and read the true number. Surface gap, moderate competitor pressure, and I'd re-pull the seasonal baseline and hold the target for now. Fix pipeline management first: stage definitions, probability weighting, weekly refresh. Only then do we know whether the real gap is forty or fifty-five percent. Before that, every remediation action is straining against the wrong number. [Branch four] And if the target is negotiable? Then the negotiation itself is the strategy. I'd walk in with three things: the gap's structural decomposition, market data, and a specific resource ask, "either adjust the target, or resource the gap." The gap's structure decides the ask: a competitor-pressure gap asks for ammunition, beta slots, marketing budget; a market-down gap asks for a yardstick, "beat the market" replacing "absolute growth." And the posture is never "I can't make it", it's "I've run the math, and this gets us closest, provided this resource gap gets filled." A manager can refuse a plea. He can't refuse arithmetic. What I hand over isn't a heroic overtime plan. It's the gap's ingredient list: seasonality recovers some points, the share war seizes some, reactivation grinds some, the yardstick loosens some. Every ingredient has a number, an action, and a checkpoint. Half the fear goes away once it's decomposed. The other half becomes a to-do list.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 判断一个客户值不值得投，标准不能从客户身上正推，得从我的时间预算反推。同样一家客户，我一周有 5 小时和有 15 小时，答案不一样。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要先确认三件事：第一，这个客户是主动找上门的 inbound，还是我主动去挖的 outbound？第二，我现在的 pipeline 瓶颈出在数量还是质量，是手上没有足够多的潜在客户，还是聊了一堆最后都不投？第三，我每周能拿出多少小时花在新客身上？ 【主分支：outbound + 质量瓶颈 + 每周 5 小时，最典型的 Scaled 场景】 逐项处理： 第一，outbound，我要主动筛选，那就用成本最低的信息先行：应用商店榜单和评分、公开的投放素材库（他有没有在别处投过广告、投了多久）、融资新闻（有没有新钱）、下载量曲线。十五分钟的 desk research，能在触达之前就毙掉一半。 第二，质量瓶颈，聊很多都不投，说明问题不在 leads 数量在"能投的"比例：标准要收紧而不是放宽。我设三道硬门槛：变现模式清晰（IAP/IAA 跑得通）、月度投放预算过线（低于线的进 1-to-many 池子，不占我的 1v1 时间）、有真实的 UA 意愿信号（已经在别处投、或明确说要在 Q 几扩量），三道全过才值得单独见。 第三，每周 5 小时，只够 10 到 15 个有效触达，所以每一次触达都必须过门槛。5 小时还决定了我跟进的节奏：首触达没回应，两周一轮 follow，三轮不回就移出名单，时间花在门槛内的客户身上，不花在感动自己上。 综合：硬门槛筛掉绝大多数，软性评分（品类增速、Google 契合度、决策链简单程度）给过门槛的排序。quals 之外一概不 1v1。 【分支二：数量瓶颈，手上没有 leads】 第二，如果瓶颈是数量，问题根本不在 qualify，在源头：先解决 list building。三个 list 来源：品类下载榜和收入榜（谁的 App 在涨谁就有投放需求）、竞品投放数据库（在 Meta/TikTok 投得猛但 Google 没投的，就是现成名单）、融资新闻（新融资的 App 通用预算已在路上）。 第一，outbound 不变。 第三，每周 5 小时不变，但用途变了：一半时间造 list（一次性动作，可持续产出），一半时间触达。 综合：数量瓶颈下 qualify 标准反而可以放宽一档，先让漏斗有水，再谈水的质量。收紧标准是数量充足时的奢侈。 【分支三：inbound，客户主动找上门】 第一，inbound，主动来的反而要验得更严：第一通电话做三件事，验证预算（"您这个阶段月度预算大概什么量级？"）、验证决策权（"这个决定是您拍还是团队一起？"）、验证时间线（"您希望什么时候跑起来？"）。三个问题过不了，inbound 就只是来比价的。 第二，pipeline 瓶颈在质量，同上。 第三，每周 5 小时给新客，同上。 综合：inbound 的正确姿势是快速响应加严格验证，响应慢丢掉真客户，验证松浪费在比价客身上。二十四小时内回，第一通电话就把三件事问完。 【Call back】 我的答案是一张门槛表，不是一种感觉：硬门槛三条（变现、预算、意愿），软评分三项（增速、契合、决策链），再加一个由我时间预算决定的触达上限。

English 口语逐字稿

Whether a client is worth my time can't be derived from the client. It has to be back-derived from my own hours. The same client is a yes at fifteen hours a week and a no at five. [Clarify — before I answer] Three things. One: is this client inbound, they came to me, or outbound, I'm hunting them? Two: where's my pipeline bottleneck, volume or quality? Is it that I don't have enough prospects, or that I talk to plenty and none of them convert? And three: how many hours a week can I actually give new business? [Main branch — the classic Scaled scenario] So let's say: outbound, quality bottleneck, five hours a week. Here's how I'd work through it. First, it's outbound, so I'm doing the choosing. That means cheapest information goes first: store rankings and ratings, public creative libraries, have they advertised elsewhere, and for how long, funding news, download curves. Fifteen minutes of desk research kills half the list before first touch. Second, the bottleneck is quality. Lots of conversations, few conversions, so the problem isn't lead count, it's the "ready to spend" ratio. That means I tighten the bar, I don't widen it. Three hard gates: clear monetization, IAP or IAA that actually works; monthly budget above the line, below it, they go to the 1-to-many pool, not my 1v1 calendar; and a real intent signal, already spending elsewhere, or a stated quarter for scaling. All three, or no individual meeting. And third, five hours a week. That's room for maybe ten to fifteen effective touches, so every single touch must clear the gates. Those five hours also set my cadence: no response to first touch, follow up every two weeks, three strikes and they're out. Time goes to clients inside the gates, not to impressing myself. So putting it together: hard gates eliminate the majority, and a soft score, category velocity, Google fit, decision-chain simplicity, ranks whoever passes. No 1v1 outside the gates. Period. [Branch two] Now, if the bottleneck is volume, I don't have enough leads, then the problem isn't qualification at all, it's the source. So the first job is list building. Three sources: category download and revenue charts, whoever's app is rising has UA needs; competitor-spend databases, spending hard on Meta and TikTok but not on Google is a ready-made list; and funding news, freshly funded apps have budget already inbound. Outbound, as before. Five hours, same, but reallocated: half to building lists, which is a one-time effort with recurring yield, and half to outreach. And here's the counterintuitive part: under a volume bottleneck, the qualification bar can actually loosen one notch. Get water into the funnel first, then worry about water quality. Tightening is a luxury for when volume is abundant. [Branch three] But what if the client is inbound, they came to me? Then here's my rule: the ones who come to you get vetted harder, not softer. Because inbound has a specific failure mode, price-shopping. So the first call does three things: verify budget, "roughly what monthly scale at this stage?"; verify authority, "is this your call, or the team's?"; and verify timeline, "when do you want this running?" If the three don't hold, the inbound is just shopping. Same quality bottleneck, same hours. So the right posture is fast response plus strict verification: reply within twenty-four hours, and finish all three verifications on call one. Slow response loses the real clients; loose verification burns your time on shoppers. So my answer is a gate table, not a gut feel. Three hard gates: monetization, budget, intent. Three soft scores: velocity, fit, decision chain. Plus a touch cap set by my time budget.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 为什么停，比怎么救重要。原因没搞清就发券召回，等于把错的事做一百遍。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要搞清楚四件事：第一，这批客户停投前是什么体量、跑了多久？第二，停投是同时发生的还是陆续发生的？第三，他们有没有共同特征，同品类、同市场、还是同批开户？第四，有没有停投前的最后表现数据？ 【主分支：腰部体量 + 陆续停 + 同批开户都没接 MMP + 最后表现 CPI 偏高但量能跑，最典型的技术卡点型沉睡】 逐项处理： 第一，停投前是腰部客户（月耗五到十万）、跑了半年以上，有底子。这意味着不是从零说服，是"重新点亮"：召回一个老客户的成本远低于获取一个新客，这类沉睡是 pipeline 里最便宜的增量。 第二，陆续停，不是市场事件（政策突变会让所有人同一周停），是个体决策。个体决策可以逐家击破，也可以按共同根因批量解决。 第三，共同特征是同批开户、都没接 MMP，技术卡点是共同根因：归因没接好，看不到真实效果，自然停投。修一个共性问题能救一批人，这是杠杆最高的激活方式。 第四，最后表现 CPI 偏高但量能跑出来，说明产品有真实需求，问题在效率不在流量：重新接好归因、调出价策略，有明确的改善空间可以讲。 综合：这批客户的激活方案是"技术修复型"打法，先救三家做出标杆（免费帮对接 MMP、配置追踪、跑两周给数据），用标杆案例批量触达剩下的人："和你同批的三家，接完之后 CPI 降了多少"，同批客户的证言比我说一百句都管用。 【分支二：最后表现是真的差，量都跑不出来】 第四，如果停投前的数据是量都跑不动，那问题可能不是投放，是产品市场匹配：素材再好也救不了一个没人要的 App。 第一，停投前是腰部客户、跑了半年以上，同上。 第二，停投节奏，陆续发生，同上。 第三，假设无共同技术特征。 综合：这时候要做的是冷酷分层：先做产品诊断（留存曲线、评分趋势、竞品表现），产品逻辑还在的客户值得救（可能是投放策略错了），产品逻辑崩了的礼貌放手，召回成本高于客户价值，把资源让给能救的。敢于放弃也是 activation 策略的一部分。 【分支三：同品类 + 同时停，品类级事件】 第三，如果共同特征是同品类（比如一批短剧客户）， 第二，同时停，两个信号叠加，指向品类级事件：政策变化、大盘冷却、或头部竞品的垄断效应。 第一，体量分布此时不那么关键。 第四，拉停投前数据做品类健康度判断。 综合：先判断品类还能不能投：品类逻辑还在（短期波动），就做品类解决方案打包召回，"新政策下短剧怎么投"的一场线上分享加一套配置模板，一次性触达全品类；品类逻辑不在了，把精力转向迁移，这些客户的团队和预算还在，帮他切到健康品类去，比救一个死品类诚实得多。 【分支四：都是小体量长尾】 第一，如果这批全是月耗几千到两万的长尾，单家召回成本可能超过单家价值，1v1 激活在数学上不成立。 第二，停投是陆续发生的，同主分支。 第三，共同特征是同批开户、都没接 MMP，同主分支。 第四，最后表现 CPI 偏高但量能跑出来，同主分支。 综合：规模化的问题用规模化的解法：一场线上 re-engagement 直播（新功能、新政策、成功案例）加自动化的邮件序列（停投原因一键调研，本身也是分层信号：填了的有意愿，没填的放手）。长尾激活的唯一正确姿势是 1-to-many。 【Call back】 第一件交付物是一张沉睡归因表：技术卡点型修技术、效果失望型给数据、关系断裂型重建关系、产品失效型放手、长尾型批量触达。分完层，每层的激活成本和预期回收都能算出来，预算先给最便宜能救活的那一层。

English 口语逐字稿

Why they stopped matters more than how to revive them. Sending discount-style winbacks before you know the reason is doing the wrong thing a hundred times. [Clarify — before I answer] Four things I'd want to know. One: what was their spend and tenure before going dormant? Two: did they stop simultaneously, or gradually? Three: do they share traits, same category, same market, same onboarding cohort? And four: do I have their last-performance data before the stop? [Main branch — the classic technical-blocker dormancy] So let's say the answers come back: they were mid-tier, fifty to a hundred thousand a month, six-plus months of history, they stopped gradually, they share one trait, same onboarding cohort, none of them integrated with an MMP, and their last performance showed elevated CPI but deliverable volume. Here's how I'd work through it. First, mid-tier with real foundations. So this isn't persuading from zero, it's relighting. Recalling a dormant client costs far less than acquiring a new one, this is the cheapest increment in my pipeline. Second, gradual stops. So it's not a market event, a policy shock stops everyone the same week. These are individual decisions. And individual decisions can be solved one by one, or by fixing a shared root cause. Third, and this is the key one, same cohort, none integrated with an MMP. The technical blocker is the common root: attribution never worked, they never saw real results, they stopped. Fixing one shared problem saves a whole batch, the highest-leverage reactivation there is. And fourth, high CPI but volume delivered. That tells me the product has real demand, the problem was efficiency, not traffic. Re-integrate attribution, restructure bidding, and there's a concrete improvement story to tell. So putting it together: the play is "technical repair." Save three first as lighthouse cases, free MMP integration, tracking setup, two weeks of data, then batch-touch the rest with proof: "three clients from your cohort, here's how much CPI dropped after integration." A testimonial from their own cohort beats a hundred of my sentences. [Branch two] Now, what if the last performance was genuinely bad, volume never came? Then the pre-stop data tells a different story: the problem may not be the advertising at all, it's product-market fit. No creative saves an app nobody wants. Size as before, gradual stops, no shared technical trait. So now it's ruthless segmentation: run a product diagnosis first, retention curve, rating trend, competitor performance. Clients whose product logic still holds are worth saving, it's probably a strategy error. Clients whose product logic broke get a graceful release, recall cost exceeds client value, and the resources go to the savable. And knowing what to drop is part of the activation strategy too. [Branch three] But what if they're all the same category and stopped at the same time? Then the two signals together point to a category event, policy change, market cooling, or a top competitor's dominance. Size matters less here, and I'd pull the pre-stop data to judge category health. First question: is the category still investable? If the logic holds, short-term turbulence, then a packaged category recall: one webinar on "how to run this category under the new rules" plus a config template, touching the whole category at once. If the logic is gone, then pivot: their teams and budgets still exist, and helping them switch to a healthy category is more honest than reviving a dead one. [Branch four] And what if the whole batch is small long-tail, a few thousand to twenty thousand a month? Then the per-account recall cost can exceed per-account value, and 1v1 reactivation fails the math. Other assumptions as in the main branch. So scale problems get scale solutions: one re-engagement webinar, new features, new policies, success stories, plus an automated email sequence with a one-click "why did you stop" survey. And that survey is itself the segmentation signal: those who answer have intent; those who don't are released. The only correct posture for long-tail activation is 1-to-many. My first deliverable isn't a winback script, it's a dormancy attribution table. Technical blockers get the fix. Performance-disappointed get data. Broken relationships get rebuilt. Dead products get a clean release. Long tail gets batch outreach. Once it's layered I can cost out each layer's revival and expected recovery, and the budget goes to whichever layer is cheapest to bring back.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 定目标是在组织要的和客户能给之间找一个可信的数。可信的标准很朴素：每个增量都得说得出由哪个变量加我哪个动作撑着。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要先确认五件事：第一，目标从哪来，公司 top-down 压的，还是让我 bottom-up 提？第二，考核口径，只看消耗，还是也看毕业率、激活数、产品采用？第三，客户结构，top 10 占多少消耗？第四，历史增速和季节性，过去两年自然增长率多少？第五，明年市场预期，顺风还是逆风？ 【主分支：top-down + 消耗为主毕业率为辅 + top 10 占 60% + 自然增速 12%、Q4 占 35% + 温和顺风，最常见的组合】 拿到答案我逐项处理： 第一，目标是 top-down 压下来的，我的角色是拆解和找路径，不是预测和承诺：公司给的数是约束条件，我的工作是让它可信、可达。 第二，考核口径是消耗为主、毕业率为辅，所以目标表是两列结构：金额列是主表，毕业列挂在旁边。金额定到客户层，毕业定到名单层（哪几家明年送上去）。 第三，top 10 占 60% 消耗，高度集中。这个答案直接决定我 bottom-up 的拆法：主战场就是这十几家，逐家过 Base 加 Upside；腰部 25 家不算金额算升层概率（几家能进头部）；长尾 40 家算激活漏斗（历史激活率乘基数）。三层加总，才是我敢签字的数，"头部 15 家"不是我拍脑袋选的，是从 60% 集中度里推导出来的。 第四，过去两年自然增速 12%、游戏客户 Q4 占全年 35%，12% 是 Base 的锚：历史外推，不算任何动作；Q4 占 35% 是钱的节奏：意味着 Q1 到 Q3 的动作都在为 Q4 蓄水，年度目标的一半要看 Q4 的临门一脚。 第五，明年温和顺风，Upside 的来源定了：帮客户接住市场红利，新市场进入、新品上线、顺势加预算。顺风年里不需要份额战这种高成本动作。 综合：公司数对着 bottom-up 数做差，差值就是我要赢的关键战役。差值怎么补：每个 Upside 必须对应两到三个具体动作，"客户 A 明年 Q2 进日本，我配冷启动方案，预计增量 X"，答不出动作的 Upside 一律砍掉。如果怎么算都够不着，带着差值结构去谈：要么调目标，要么给资源，筹码是拆解，不是抱怨。 【分支二：bottom-up 让我提数 + 结构分散 top 10 只占 35%，口径一变算法就变】 逐项过： 第一，bottom-up，角色从拆解者变成预测者：我提的数必须是我敢签字的数，宁可提一个能超的，不提一个要解释的。 第二，考核口径是消耗为主、毕业率为辅，同上。 第三，结构分散，top 10 只占 35%：单家翻不动大盘，目标是组合行为：靠腰部升层和长尾激活抬整条曲线，而不是押十几家。分散结构下更显式留 10% 到 15% 给"未知新客"，长尾每年出黑马，这是 Scaled 岗的结构性红利，分散盘子里这个红利占比更大。 第四，历史锚同主分支。 第五，明年温和顺风，同上。 综合：同样三层拆解，但权重完全不同：主分支是头部驱动的目标，这个分支是漏斗驱动的目标，腰部升层数量和长尾激活率，比任何单家的 Upside 都重要。 【分支三：top-down 不变 + 市场逆风，环境变了 Upside 的来源就变了】 逐项过： 第五，逆风，这是本分支的核心变量：顺风年 Upside 来自接红利，逆风年 Upside 只能来自份额战（从竞品渠道抢预算）和内部再分配（提升 Google 占客户总预算的比例），两种动作的成本和确定性完全不同，逆风年的 Upside 要打更大的折扣。 第四，历史外推的 Base 也要重估：12% 的自然增速建立在顺风历史上，逆风市场自然增速可能只剩 5%，Base 不调整，目标从根上就是虚的。 第一，top-down 不变。 第二，考核口径，消耗为主、毕业率为辅，不变。 第三，top 10 占 60% 消耗，同主分支。 综合：逆风年我在年初就把口径谈在前面，把"绝对增长"重新定义为"跑赢市场"：市场跌 10%、我跌 2%，等于相对赢 8 个点。这个口径必须一月谈，不能十二月解释。 【分支四：考核口径以毕业率和激活为主，口径变了目标表的形态就变了】 逐项过： 第二，口径以毕业率、激活数、产品采用为主，金额变成结果指标而不是目标指标：目标不再下放到"每家背多少消耗"，而是下放到"毕业 N 家、激活 M 家、新功能采用 X 家"，每类目标配一张动作包。 第三，top 10 占 60% 消耗（同主分支），但名单逻辑变了：不是"谁能贡献最多消耗"，而是"谁离毕业线最近、谁最可能被激活"。 第一，目标是 top-down 压下来的，同主分支。 第四，自然增速 12%、Q4 占 35%，同主分支。 第五，明年温和顺风，同主分支。 综合：多维口径下的目标是一张动作表而不是一张金额表，金额是动作表的输出。这其实是我最喜欢的口径：动作比金额可控，我管理动作，金额自己来。 【Call back】 我交的是一张三层目标表：每层配指标类型、数值、支撑动作、检查点。月度看动作完成率，季度看金额，每季复盘预测偏差滚动校准。

English 口语逐字稿

Setting a target means finding a number that's credible to the org and to the client at the same time. The credibility test is simple: every increment has to answer which known variable plus which action of mine supports it. [Clarify — before I answer] Five things I'd want to understand. One: where does the target come from, is it top-down from the company, or bottom-up from me? Two: the evaluation metric, spend only, or also graduation, activation, adoption? Three: portfolio structure, how much does the top ten contribute? Four: historical growth and seasonality, what's the organic rate over the past two years? And five: next year's market, tailwind or headwind? [Main branch — the most common combination] So let's say the answers come back: top-down target, spend primary with graduation secondary, the top ten carry sixty percent, twelve percent organic growth with Q4 at thirty-five percent, and a mild tailwind. Here's how I'd work through it. First, it's top-down. So my role is decomposition and path-finding, not forecasting and committing. The company's number is a constraint; my job is making it credible and reachable. Second, the metric is spend primary, graduation secondary. That tells me the target sheet has two columns: the money column is the main sheet, the graduation column sits beside it. Money targets go down to account level; graduation targets go to the list level, which named accounts am I shipping up next year. Third, the top ten carry sixty percent. Highly concentrated. And this answer basically decides my bottom-up build: the main battlefield is those dozen-plus accounts, so I walk each one through Base plus Upside. The middle twenty-five don't carry money, they carry promotion probability: how many reach the top tier this year. The bottom forty carry the activation funnel, historical activation rate times the base. Sum the three layers, and that's the number I'll sign. And here's the point, "top fifteen accounts" isn't a number I picked out of thin air. It's derived from that sixty percent concentration. Fourth, twelve percent organic, gaming clients putting thirty-five percent of the year into Q4. The twelve percent anchors my Base, pure trend extrapolation, no actions counted. And the Q4 number gives me the rhythm of the money: Q1 through Q3 is all watering the Q4 crop. Half the annual target rides on Q4's final kick. And fifth, a mild tailwind. That sets where the Upside comes from: helping clients catch the market, new geos, new launches, riding the wave with more budget. A tailwind year doesn't need the high-cost share-war motion. So putting it all together: I hold the company's number against my bottom-up, and the gap is the campaign I have to win. Every Upside has to map to two or three concrete actions, "Client A enters Japan in Q2, I support the cold start, estimated increment X." Any Upside that can't name its action gets cut. And if the math won't close, I take the gap structure to the table: either adjust the target, or resource the gap. The chips are the decomposition, not complaints. [Branch two] Now, flip it, if it's bottom-up, I propose the number, and the structure is dispersed with the top ten only at thirty-five percent. Then my role shifts from decomposer to forecaster: the number I propose must be one I'll sign, better a number I can beat than a number I have to explain. Metric as before. But the dispersed structure changes everything: no single account moves the board, so the target is portfolio behavior, the curve rises on middle-tier promotions and tail activations, not on a dozen bets. And in a dispersed book I'd reserve even more explicitly, ten to fifteen percent for unknown new clients, because the tail produces dark horses every year, and that advantage looms larger when the book is dispersed. Historical anchor and tailwind as before. So same three-layer build, completely different weights: the main branch is a head-driven target; this one is a funnel-driven target. [Branch three] But what if the target stays top-down and the market turns into a headwind? Then the core variable flips: tailwind Upside comes from catching the market; headwind Upside only comes from a share war, taking budget from competitor channels, and internal reallocation, growing Google's share of each client's wallet. Different costs, different certainty, headwind Upside deserves a much bigger haircut. And the Base needs re-anchoring too: twelve percent organic was built on tailwind history, in a headwind, organic might only run five. If Base isn't adjusted, the target is hollow at the root. Top-down, metric, structure as before. And in a headwind year, I'd align the yardstick in January: redefine "absolute growth" as "beat the market." Market down ten, me down two, that's eight points of relative outperformance. Agreed in month one, never explained in month twelve. [Branch four] And what if the metric weights graduation and activation most? Then the target sheet changes shape entirely: money becomes an outcome indicator, not a target. Targets no longer allocate "how much spend per account", they say "N graduations, M activations, X new-product adoptions," each with an action pack. Structure as in the main branch, but the list logic flips: not "who contributes most spend," but "who's nearest the graduation line, who's most activatable." Top-down, history, market as before. And honestly, that's my favorite yardstick, because actions are more controllable than money. I manage the actions; the money comes on its own. So what I deliver isn't a number. It's a three-tier target sheet: metric type, value, supporting action, checkpoint on every tier. Monthly I review action completion. Quarterly, the money. And every quarter I re-check my forecast error and recalibrate.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 25% 这个数我不会硬扛。市场在缩的时候，我先想的是能换什么：换增长来源、换目标口径、还是换资源投入。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要确认：第一，萎缩范围，某个垂直在萎缩，还是整个买量市场？第二，萎缩原因，需求侧（广告主没钱了）还是供给侧（政策、隐私、平台变化）？第三，我的客户里有没有逆势增长的，他们做对了什么？第四，25% 是死命令还是可谈？第五，我有没有额外弹药，beta、市场费用、专家时间？ 【主分支：全市场萎缩 + 供给侧变化 + 有逆势客户 + 死命令 + 有 beta 弹药，最有得打的一手牌】 逐项处理： 第一，全市场萎缩，没有赛道可以换：增长不能来自"换个涨的地方"，只能来自抢份额。 第二，萎缩原因是供给侧变化（比如 iOS 归因变难吓退了一批广告主），这是我最喜欢的局面：别人撤退留下的空间。帮客户搞定 SKAN 和 MMP 对接，是竞品渠道给不了的服务，归因问题解决了，量自然向我集中。 第三，有逆势增长的客户，他们是免费的 playbook：把做对的事（新市场、变现调整、品类切换）拆解出来，复制给同类客户。这是成本最低的 Upside。 第四，25% 是死命令，那就全力冲，同时诚实汇报风险：给老板一张最坏情境表（市场跌 X% 时我的最优解和底线），证明这一仗是 war-game 过的，不是硬着头皮接的。 第五，有 beta 弹药，份额战有武器：给高潜客户独家的 early access，是逆风期撬预算最硬的钩子。 综合：三条线并行，份额线（主攻竞品加投但 Google 占比低的客户）、服务线（用供给侧变化做竞品做不到的事）、复制线（逆势 playbook 批量推广）。逆风期客户听得进"效率"听不进"增长"，所以每一次 pitch 都从"帮你把账算清"切入，绝不从"多投点"切入。 【分支二：只有垂直在萎缩，短剧凉了但工具在涨】 逐项过： 第一，垂直萎缩，有赛道可以换：做组合再平衡，萎缩品类只求"跑赢品类"（目标定成相对值），增量获取全部押在增长品类的新客上。 第四，目标不变，但目标的结构变了：同一个 25%，主分支里靠份额战打，这个分支里靠品类迁移换。 第二，萎缩原因是供给侧变化，同主分支。 第三，有逆势增长的客户，同主分支。 第五，有 beta 弹药，同主分支。 综合：目标不变，客户结构变，这就是组合管理的价值：市场此消彼长的时候，组合本身就是一个增长来源。 【分支三：需求侧萎缩，广告主真的没钱了】 逐项过： 第二，需求侧萎缩，最真实的收缩：没有结构性机会可抓，beta 也变不出客户不存在的预算。 第三，逆势客户变成最重要的资产，他们是在"没钱的市场"里还在赚钱的人，他们的打法就是所有人的答案。 第五，弹药有没有都要先做效率战，精耕效率是需求侧萎缩唯一的正面战场。 第一，全市场萎缩，同主分支。 第四，25% 是死命令，同主分支。 综合：效率战加 playbook 复制加口径谈判三件套。这种年份里，帮客户"把钱花得更值"比任何增长故事都更能留住预算，他省下来的每一块钱，都是我下一年的存量。 【分支四：25% 可谈，把口径谈判做成第一动作】 逐项过： 第四，25% 可谈，那谈判本身就是策略：带着市场数据（大盘跌多少）和 bottom-up 测算（我的最优解是多少）去，把"绝对增长 25%"谈成"跑赢市场 X 个点"。市场跌 10%、我跌 2%，等于相对赢 8 个点：这个口径对老板同样可以交代：份额在涨，位置在涨。 第一，全市场萎缩，同主分支。 第二，萎缩原因是供给侧变化，同主分支。 第三，有逆势增长的客户，同主分支。 第五，有 beta 弹药，同主分支。 综合：能谈的年份先谈口径再打仗，口径对了，仗才打得赢也论得清。谈判的筹码永远是数据，不是情绪。 【Call back】 我不会把 25% 当成孤勇的数字去硬扛，会拆成三条能验证的线：份额战拿几点、结构转移贡献几点、服务差异化赢几点，看数据决定哪条加码。逆风期我的第一指标是相对表现，我的增速减市场增速。

English 口语逐字稿

I'm not going to absorb twenty-five percent as some lone-brave number. When the market is contracting I ask what I can swap: the source of growth, the yardstick, or the resources. [Clarify — before I answer] One: the scope, is a vertical shrinking, or the whole UA market? Two: the cause, demand-side, meaning advertisers are broke, or supply-side, policy, privacy, platform shifts? Three: do I have any counter-trend clients, and what are they doing right? Four: is the twenty-five percent hard or negotiable? And five: extra ammunition, betas, budget, specialist time? [Main branch — the strongest hand] So let's say the answers come back: the whole market is shrinking, the cause is supply-side, I do have counter-trend clients, twenty-five percent is a hard number, and I've got beta ammunition. Here's how I'd work through it. First, the whole market is shrinking. So there's no lane to switch to: growth can't come from "finding a rising corner", it can only come from taking share. Second, the cause is supply-side. Say, iOS attribution chaos scaring a batch of advertisers away. And honestly, that's my favorite scenario, because it's the space others leave behind. Helping clients nail SKAN and MMP integration is service competitor channels can't match. Solve the attribution problem, and spend concentrates toward me naturally. Third, counter-trend clients exist. They're a free playbook: decode what they did right, new markets, monetization shifts, category switches, and replicate it across similar clients. The cheapest Upside there is. Fourth, twenty-five percent is hard. Then full speed, with honest risk reporting: I'd hand my manager a worst-case table, my best play and my floor if the market drops X, proving the fight was war-gamed, not accepted blindly. And fifth, beta ammunition exists. So the share war has weapons: exclusive early access for high-potential clients is the hardest hook for prying budget loose in a downturn. So putting it together: three lines in parallel. The share line, clients increasing competitor spend with low Google share. The service line, supply-side shifts let me do what competitors can't. And the replication line, counter-trend playbooks rolled out in batch. And one rule for every conversation: in a downturn, clients hear "efficiency," not "growth." Every pitch starts from "let's make your math work", never "spend more." [Branch two] Now, if only the vertical is shrinking, short-drama cooling while utilities rise, then there is a lane to switch to, and the move is portfolio rebalancing. In the shrinking category, only aim to beat the category, relative targets. All incremental acquisition goes to the growing categories. Cause, counter-trend clients, ammunition as before. And the twenty-five percent holds, but its structure changes: the same twenty-five percent the main branch wins through a share war, this branch wins through category migration. The target stays; the client structure changes. And that's the value of portfolio management: when the market reshuffles, the portfolio itself is a growth source. [Branch three] But what if it's demand-side, advertisers genuinely broke? Then that's the most honest contraction: no structural openings to seize, and no beta can conjure budget that doesn't exist. Scope hard, twenty-five percent still hard. Counter-trend clients now become the most important asset, they're the ones still making money in a broke market, and their playbook is everyone else's answer. With or without ammunition, the efficiency war comes first, grinding for efficiency is the only front line in a demand-side contraction. So: the efficiency war, playbook replication, and yardstick negotiation, the three-piece set. In a year like this, helping clients spend smarter retains more budget than any growth story, every dollar they save is carried-over budget for me next year. [Branch four] And what if the twenty-five percent is negotiable? Then the negotiation itself is the strategy. I'd walk in with market data, how much the market is down, and a bottom-up model of my best achievable number, and trade "absolute twenty-five percent" for "beat the market by X points." Market down ten, me down two, eight points of relative outperformance. And that framing holds up with any manager: share rising, position rising. Scope, cause, clients, ammunition as in the main branch. In a negotiable year, align the yardstick before fighting the war, with the right yardstick, the war is both winnable and scoreable. And the chips at that table are always data, never emotion. I don't treat twenty-five into a shrinking market as a number to absorb on courage. I split it into three lines I can verify: points the share war can take, points the structural shift contributes, points service differentiation can win. Then the data tells me which line to fund. And in a down market my first metric is relative performance: my growth minus the market's growth.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 拆解不是把大数除以客户数。每一层背得动的指标类型本来就不一样。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要确认四件事：第一，客户分层结构，头部、腰部、长尾各多少家、贡献多少？第二，考核口径是纯消耗还是多维？第三，每个客户明年有什么已知变量，大促、新品、市场扩张计划？第四，历史上客户升降层的流动率怎么样？ 【主分支：二八结构 + 多维考核 + 已知变量充分 + 年升层率 10-15%，最典型场景】 逐项处理： 第一，二八结构，top 10 到 15 家贡献大部分消耗。这个答案决定拆解的第一层：年度目标本质上是十几家客户的事，头部客户背金额目标，Base 加 Upside。 第二，多维考核，那腰部和长尾不背金额：腰部背升层目标（几家今年进头部，金额是结果、动作才是指标），长尾背漏斗目标（激活数、毕业数，好处是可控：我的动作直接影响漏斗，间接影响金额）。 第三，已知变量充分（哪家 Q2 进日本、哪家有大促、哪家发新品），这是 Upside 的原料：每个 Upside 必须由"已知变量加我的动作"支撑，"客户 A Q2 进日本，我配冷启动方案，预计增量 X"才叫 Upside，"我觉得他会涨"不是。答不出变量的 Upside 一律砍掉。 第四，历史年升层率 10% 到 15%，腰部 25 家里明年能长出 3 到 5 家新头部。所以"升层动作包"（筛出最可能升层的五到八家，配专项动作）是全盘回报率最高的投资：升层一家，等于白得一个新头部。 综合：产出一张三层活表，层、指标类型、数值、支撑动作、季度检查点、升降级规则。每季度重新分层：升层的把指标从漏斗换成金额，掉层的反之。总盘永远留 10% 到 15% 给新客和意外。 【分支二：纯消耗考核，口径变单薄，权重更向头部集中】 逐项过： 第二，纯消耗口径，那长尾连漏斗目标都不用背了：他们的金额贡献并入总盘统一兜底，绝不硬性下放到每一家。原因很简单：长尾单量小且不可控，背了金额只会逼出假动作，为凑数开户、为凑数烧钱。 第一，二八结构，top 10 到 15 家贡献大部分消耗，同主分支。 第三，已知变量充分，同主分支。 第四，年升层率 10% 到 15%，同主分支。 综合：纯消耗口径下，目标重心进一步向头部集中，腰部依然背升层（因为升层最终产出金额），长尾只做动作不打指标。口径越单一，越要靠分层防止目标变形。 【分支三：扁平结构，没有明显头部】 逐项过： 第一，扁平结构，没有现成的大客，那明年的头部要从今年的腰部里长出来：权重从"头部金额"移到"腰部升层"，升层动作包从投资项变成主战略。 第四，流动率这时候是关键校验：扁平结构如果流动率也低，说明不是"还没长出来"而是"长不出来"，要先诊断服务模型哪里卡住了。 第二，多维考核，同主分支。 第三，已知变量充分，同主分支。 综合：扁平盘子的拆解是"播种式"的：腰部全员配升层路径，赌其中五到八家明年撑起头部。这比守着长尾做激活激进，但数学上成立，头部是从腰部升上去的，从来不是从长尾里冒出来的。 【分支四：流动率很低，分层是死的】 逐项过： 第四，年升层率不到 3%，先别急着拆目标，先回答为什么不动：是客户质量天花板（拿到的就是长尾命），还是服务模型瓶颈（没人推他们升层）。前者接受现实，目标全押存量深耕；后者是系统性问题，拆解前先修服务。 第一，二八结构，top 10 到 15 家贡献大部分消耗，同主分支。 第二，多维考核，同主分支。 第三，已知变量充分，同主分支。 综合：流动率是拆解的前置体检，它决定"升层投资"这个动作在我的盘子里是主战略还是幻觉。 【Call back】 我交的是一张三层活表：每层配它背得动的指标，每个指标配说得出的动作，每季度重洗一次。健不健康看一个朴素的标准，每层客户都觉得这个数跳一跳够得着。

English 口语逐字稿

Decomposition isn't the big number divided by client count. Each tier can carry a different kind of metric. [Clarify — before I answer] Four things. One: the tier structure, how many in top, middle, and tail, and what's the contribution split? Two: spend-only evaluation, or multi-dimensional? Three: known per-client variables next year, promos, launches, market expansions? And four: the historical tier-migration rate? [Main branch — the classic scenario] So let's say: 80/20 structure, multi-dimensional evaluation, known variables available, and an annual promotion rate of ten to fifteen percent. Here's how I'd work through it. First, the 80/20 structure: the top ten to fifteen accounts carry most of the spend. That sets the first layer of decomposition: the annual target is really a dozen clients' story, and the top tier carries spend targets, Base plus Upside. Second, multi-dimensional evaluation. That means the middle and the tail don't carry money: the middle carries promotion targets, how many reach the top tier this year, where spend is the outcome and actions are the metric. The tail carries funnel targets, activation counts, graduation counts. And the beauty of funnel targets is controllability: my actions drive the funnel directly, and the money indirectly. Third, known variables are available: who enters Japan in Q2, who has a major promo, who launches. These are the raw material of Upside. Every Upside must be backed by a known variable plus an action of mine, "Client A enters Japan in Q2, I support the cold start, estimated increment X", that's an Upside. "I feel they'll grow" is not. Anything that can't name its variable gets cut. And fourth, promotion runs ten to fifteen percent a year. So the middle twenty-five will grow three to five new top-tier accounts next year. Which means the promotion action pack, screen the five to eight most likely, attach dedicated actions, is the highest-ROI investment in the book. Promote one, and you've gained a new head account for free. So putting it together: the deliverable is a three-tier living table, tier, metric type, value, supporting actions, quarterly checkpoints, promotion and demotion rules. Re-tier every quarter: promoted clients switch metrics from funnel to spend, demoted ones reverse. And the total always keeps ten to fifteen percent for new clients and surprises. [Branch two] Now, if the evaluation is spend-only, then the thinner yardstick pushes the weight even further into the head. Tiering, variables, migration as before. In this case, the tail doesn't even carry funnel targets: their money contribution gets absorbed into the portfolio total, never force-allocated per account. And the reason is simple: tail volumes are small and uncontrollable, and spend targets there just force fake actions, accounts opened to hit a number, budget burned to hit a number. The middle still carries promotion, because promotion eventually produces spend. The tail does actions, not targets. The thinner the yardstick, the more tiering matters to keep the target honest. [Branch three] But what if the structure is flat, no obvious head? Then there's no ready-made major account, so next year's head has to grow out of this year's middle. Weight shifts from "head spend" to "middle promotion", and the promotion action pack graduates from an investment line to the main strategy. Variables, evaluation as before. But here's the check: flat structure plus low migration doesn't mean "not grown yet", it means "can't grow." So I'd diagnose where the service model is stuck before decomposing anything. The decomposition for a flat book is sowing-style: the whole middle tier gets promotion paths, betting five to eight of them prop up next year's head. More aggressive than grinding tail activations, but mathematically sound, because heads grow out of middles. They never pop out of tails. [Branch four] And what if the migration rate is very low, under three percent? Then before decomposing the target, I have to answer why nothing moves. Structure, evaluation, variables as before. Two possibilities: a client-quality ceiling, what we onboarded is long-tail destiny, or a service-model bottleneck, nobody's pushing them up. The former means accept reality and invest everything in deepening the existing base. The latter is a systemic problem, fix the service before the decomposition. Migration rate is the pre-decomposition health check: it decides whether promotion investment is my book's main strategy, or a delusion. So the deliverable isn't a number-allocation sheet, it's a three-tier living table. Each tier carries the metric it can bear, each metric carries an action someone can explain, and it gets reshuffled every quarter. The health check is plain: does every tier feel the number is within reach.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 客户说没效果，问题往往不在效果上。口径、配置、预期、生意本身，四种病的药完全不同，所以先诊断。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我会问四个问题：第一，您说的"没效果"具体指什么，跑不出量，还是有量但不赚钱？第二，您是拿什么对比得出"没效果"的，自己的预期，还是别的渠道？第三，转化追踪接的什么、出价用的 tCPA 还是 tROAS、目标设了多少？第四，您现在是情绪上想撤，还是有真实的预算压力？ 【主分支：有量不赚钱 + 和 TikTok 对比 + tROAS 目标设太紧 + 情绪和压力都有：最常见的复合场景】 逐项处理： 第一，"没效果"是有量但不赚钱，这是回收问题不是流量问题：账户有能力跑量，问题出在出价和转化链路的配置上。方向对了，修起来就快。 第二，对比基准是 TikTok，那第一步先把度量衡对齐：同市场、同时间段、同素材、归因窗口一致吗？TikTok 归因窗口更宽松，原始数据直接比是失真的。大部分"没效果"在口径对齐之后会自动变成"有差距但可解释"。 第三，tROAS 目标设得太紧，系统性 bid 压缩：量被压死、CPI 被推高、看起来又贵又慢，这是个配置问题，不是平台问题。修复动作明确：先松目标跑出量级基线，再逐步收紧。 第四，情绪加真实压力都有：那我的顺序是先人后事：先共情（"两周确实不到下结论的时候，您的着急我理解"），绝不辩护；然后用诊断代替说服，我拿出数据说话，他看到的不是我辩解，是我在帮他查问题。 综合：三步走，共情稳住人、诊断定位问题（口径对齐加配置检查）、给 30 天计划（修配置、跑数据、设加码阈值："达到 X 我们加预算，达不到我们再谈"）。设两周一次的复盘节点：把"撤不撤"的一次性决定，变成"看数据"的持续对话。 【分支二：根本跑不出量，流量问题】 逐项过： 第一，跑不出量，那要看三样东西：预算（日均预算太低系统学习不充分）、素材（两周没换过素材基本必死，冷启动期素材就是量）、出价（没有竞争力等于让系统别跑）。 第二，假设对比基准是自己的预期。 第三，追踪假设正常。 第四，假设情绪为主。 综合：素材诊断优先，两周本来就是素材测试期，不是效果结论期。给他看同品类客户的素材迭代节奏（一周几套新素材），把"没效果"重新定义为"测试还没跑完"。同时给量级预期管理：冷启动期多少量是正常的，让他知道现在在哪个阶段。 【分支三：对比的是自己不切实际的预期】 逐项过： 第二，他的"没效果"是拿心里的预期（"别人家案例一个月翻倍"）比的，这是预期管理问题，不是账户问题。 第一，假设量正常。 第三，假设配置正常。 第四，假设情绪为主。 综合：给他看基准线而不是给承诺：同品类、同阶段、同预算量级客户的真实数据分布，中位数是多少、头部是多少、他现在在哪个位置。预期锚点从"传说"换成"同类"，落差自然收窄。绝不在没有数据支撑的情况下为了留他而承诺数字，留一个预期错的客户，只是把冲突推迟到下个月。 【分支四：真实的预算压力，生意本身缺钱】 逐项过： 第四，真实预算压力，那这不是广告问题，是生意问题：他不是不信 Google，是烧不起。 第一，量的层面，跑量正常、回收正常，账户本身没有毛病。 第二，对比基准，不存在口径问题，"没效果"的根源不在度量衡。 第三，配置层面，追踪和出价都健康，无需修复。 综合：帮他把有限预算的效率做到最高，缩减规模但保结构（留表现最好的 campaign、砍测试性的），给他一个"缓过来就能重启"的方案。缩量不缩服务：他缓过来的时候第一个想到的人，决定我明年有没有这个客户。 【Call back】 先诊断再行动：口径问题给对齐表、配置问题给修复方案、预期问题给基准线、生意问题给一个体面的收缩方案。唯一不能做的是不诊断就挽留，那等于告诉客户我只关心我的消耗。

English 口语逐字稿

When a client says it isn't working, the problem usually isn't performance. Yardstick, configuration, expectations, or the business itself all get different medicine, so I diagnose first. [Clarify — before I answer] Four questions I'd ask. One: what exactly does "no results" mean, can't get volume, or volume that doesn't pay back? Two: what are you comparing against, your own expectation, or another channel? Three: what's the conversion tracking setup, tCPA or tROAS bidding, and what target? And four: is this an emotional reaction, or real budget pressure? [Main branch — the most common compound scenario] So let's say the answers come back: volume that doesn't pay back, benchmarked against TikTok, tROAS set too tight, and both emotion and real pressure. Here's how I'd work through it. First, "no results" means volume that doesn't pay back. So it's a recovery problem, not a traffic problem: the account can deliver volume, the issue sits in bidding and conversion-chain configuration. Right direction, fast fix. Second, the benchmark is TikTok. Then step one is aligning the yardstick: same market, same window, same creatives, aligned attribution? TikTok's attribution window is looser, comparing raw numbers is distorted. Most "no results" cases become "a gap, but explainable" once the methodology is aligned. Third, the tROAS target is set too tight. That creates systematic bid suppression: volume choked, CPI pushed up, looking expensive and slow. And that's a configuration problem, not a platform problem. The fix is concrete: loosen the target to establish a volume baseline first, then tighten stepwise. And fourth, both emotion and real pressure. So my order is people before business: empathize first, "two weeks really is too early to conclude anything, and I understand the anxiety", and never defend. Then replace persuasion with diagnosis: I bring data, so he sees me investigating his problem, not defending my platform. So putting it together: three steps. Empathize to steady the person. Diagnose to locate the problem, yardstick alignment plus configuration check. Then a thirty-day plan: fix the config, run the data, set a scaling threshold, "hit X and we add budget; miss it and we talk." And review checkpoints every two weeks, turning the one-time "stay or go" decision into an ongoing "let's look at the data" conversation. [Branch two] Now, what if there's no volume at all? Then I check three things. Benchmark, tracking, emotion, assume his own expectation, normal tracking, emotion-driven. The three things: budget, daily budget too low for the system to learn; creatives, two weeks without new creatives is near-certain death, because in cold start, creative IS the volume; and bidding, uncompetitive bids tell the system not to run. Creative diagnosis comes first, two weeks is a creative-testing window, not a results-verdict window. Show him the creative iteration cadence of same-category clients, new sets per week, and reframe "no results" as "the test isn't finished." Plus volume expectation-setting: what's normal at cold start, and where he sits on that curve. [Branch three] But what if he's benchmarking against an unrealistic expectation, "someone's case study doubled in a month"? Then it's expectation management, not an account problem. Volume normal, configuration normal, emotion-driven. So I show him the baseline, not a promise: the real distribution of same-category, same-stage, same-budget clients, the median, the top, and where he stands. Once the anchor moves from "legend" to "peers," the perceived gap narrows on its own. And one rule: never promise numbers without data just to keep him. Retaining a client on a wrong expectation only postpones the conflict to next month. [Branch four] And what if it's genuine budget pressure, the business itself is short of money? Then this isn't an advertising problem, it's a business problem: he doesn't distrust Google, he just can't afford to burn. Volume, benchmark, configuration, assume account performance is normal. So I help him squeeze maximum efficiency from what he has: shrink the scale but keep the structure, keep the best campaigns, cut the experimental ones, and hand him a "restart-when-you-recover" plan. Shrink the spend, never the service. Because the person he thinks of first when he recovers, that decides whether I still have this client next year. Diagnose before you act. A yardstick problem gets an alignment table, a configuration problem gets a fix plan, an expectation problem gets a baseline, a business problem gets a graceful way to contract. Four conditions, four different prescriptions. The one wrong move is retaining without diagnosing, which tells the client I care about my spend and not his problem.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 价格异议的第一层几乎都是度量问题，两边算的压根不是同一个东西。度量衡没对齐就谈价值，会变成辩论。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我会先问清楚：第一，这个对比的口径，同市场、同时间段、同素材吗？两边归因窗口一致吗？第二，您真正的 KPI 是什么，CPI 本身就是目标，还是您真正关心的是回收？第三，后端数据能拉吗，MMP 或 Firebase 里有没有分渠道的 cohort？第四，TikTok 是您的主渠道还是也在测试期？ 【主分支：口径没对齐 + 真正关心回收 + 能拉后端 + TikTok 是主渠道，最典型场景】 逐项处理： 第一，口径没对齐，TikTok 的归因窗口比 Google 宽松，原始 CPI 直接比是失真的。这是第一嫌疑：先做一张对齐口径后的对比表（同窗口、同市场、同素材类型），很多时候"贵一倍"在对齐之后自动变成"贵两成"。 第二，真正关心的是回收不是 CPI，那对话就该升维：CPI 是过程指标，D30 回收才是 KPI。把比较从"CPI 对比"换成"分渠道 cohort 回收对比"，在回收维度上重新定价 Google 的贵。 第三，能拉后端数据，证据在手：拉分渠道 cohort，看留存曲线和付费表现。Google 的量通常质量更优，贵在 CPI，赢在 LTV：便宜的量如果 D7 就流失，真实成本反而更高。 第四，TikTok 是主渠道，那就不能正面攻击他的主力：定位 Google 为"高质量补充量"，先拿 20% 预算做对照实验，让数据自己说话。攻击主渠道的谈话会变成保卫战，对照实验的谈话是共同探索。 综合：交付两样东西，一张对齐口径后的对比表（处理度量问题），一个对照实验提案（处理价值问题：同预算、同素材、同出价策略跑四周，用回收数据定输赢）。这类客户不需要被说服，需要有人看懂他的数据。 【分支二：CPI 本身就是 KPI，比如重度 IAA 的工具类 App】 逐项过： 第二，CPI 就是他的目标 KPI（IAA 变现、赚广告分成，成本敏感到分），那"贵"的批评在 his 口径下成立，我不硬拗。 第一，假设口径对齐后仍然贵。 第三，假设能拉后端。 第四，假设测试期。 综合：把 CPI 换算成"有效 CPA"，按激活质量折算：低价渠道的量如果次留只有 5%，真实成本要除以留存折损。给他算这笔账，而不是 defend CPI 的数字。如果算完确实还是贵，那诚实地说这个客户品类可能真不适合主力投 Google，帮他做渠道组合的优化配置。承认边界比硬卖更值钱：他会在适合的场景想起我。 【分支三：两边都在测试期，TikTok 也不是主力】 逐项过： 第四，TikTok 也在测试期，那他其实在做渠道选型，这是最开放的客户状态：不需要辩论，需要实验设计。 第一，口径先对齐（测试期的对比尤其容易口径混乱）。 第二，假设关心回收。 第三，假设能拉数据。 综合：直接提案科学的 A/B 结构：同预算、同素材库、同出价策略、四周周期、预先约定评估指标（不是 CPI 是回收）。测试期客户信的是流程不是话术，我给他一个干净的实验，输赢都认。用实验设计赢，永远比用辩论赢走得远。 【分支四：后端数据拉不到】 逐项过： 第三，后端数据拉不到（没接 MMP 或数据没分渠道），那一切对比都是盲比，他说的和我说的一样没有证据。 第一，对比口径没对齐，同主分支。 第二，真正关心的是回收，同主分支。 第四，TikTok 是主渠道，同主分支。 综合：第一动作不是反驳，是帮他接好 MMP 和分渠道追踪，这本身既是解决问题的前提，也是黏性：接数据的过程就是深度服务的过程。数据接好之前，我把结论挂起："两周后我们拿真实数据谈。"不凭感觉下结论的客户关系，才走得长。 【Call back】 顺序是：先校准度量衡（不花钱），再升维到回收（换 KPI 重新定价），最后用对照实验让数据裁决。验证这场对话的不是我赢没赢辩论，是 30 天后 Google 在他总预算里占多少。

English 口语逐字稿

The first layer of a price objection is almost always measurement. The two sides aren't computing the same thing. Talk value before the yardstick is aligned and it turns into a debate. [Clarify — before I answer] I'd ask four things. One: the methodology, same market, same window, same creatives? Attribution windows aligned? Two: your real KPI, is CPI itself the goal, or is payback what you actually care about? Three: can we pull backend data, per-channel cohorts in your MMP or Firebase? And four: is TikTok your main channel, or also in test? [Main branch — the classic scenario] So let's say: methodology not aligned, the real concern is payback, backend data is available, and TikTok is the main channel. Here's how I'd work through it. First, methodology not aligned. TikTok's attribution window is looser than Google's, so comparing raw CPI is inherently distorted. This is suspect number one: build an aligned comparison table first, same window, same market, same creative types. Very often, "twice as expensive" becomes "twenty percent more" on its own after alignment. Second, the real concern is payback, not CPI. Then the conversation should move up a dimension: CPI is a process metric; D30 payback is the KPI. So I'd swap the comparison from "CPI versus CPI" to "per-channel cohort payback", re-pricing Google's premium in the recovery dimension. Third, backend data is available. So the evidence is in hand: pull per-channel cohorts, look at retention curves and paying behavior. Google's volume is typically higher quality, expensive on CPI, winning on LTV. Cheap installs that churn by day seven actually cost more in real terms. And fourth, TikTok is the main channel. So no frontal attack on his flagship.
