I'd position Google as the high-quality complement: take twenty percent of budget into a controlled experiment, and let the data speak. A conversation attacking the main channel becomes a defense war. A controlled experiment is a joint exploration. So putting it together, I'd deliver two things: an aligned comparison table, which handles the measurement problem, and a controlled-experiment proposal, which handles the value problem: same budget, same creatives, same bidding strategy, four weeks, judged on payback. And the key insight about this client: he doesn't need persuading. He needs someone who can read his data correctly. [Branch two] Now, what if CPI itself is the KPI? Say it's a heavily IAA-monetized utility app, ad-revenue arbitrage, cost-sensitive to the cent. Then the "expensive" criticism holds within his yardstick, and I don't fight it head-on. Methodology, assume it's still expensive after alignment. Backend data available, TikTok in test. So my move is to convert CPI into "effective CPA", adjusted for install quality: if the cheap channel's day-one retention is five percent, the real cost has to be divided by the retention haircut. I run that math for him, rather than defending the CPI number. And if the math still says expensive, then I'll say honestly: this category may not fit Google as the primary channel, and here's how to optimize your channel mix. Admitting boundaries beats hard-selling. He'll remember me when a fitting scenario comes along. [Branch three] But what if both channels are in test, TikTok isn't the flagship either? Then he's actually doing channel selection, the most open client state there is. He doesn't need debate; he needs experiment design. Methodology aligned first, test-phase comparisons are especially prone to methodological chaos. Payback concern, data pullable, as before. So I'd propose a clean A/B structure directly: same budget, same creative pool, same bidding, four weeks, with the evaluation metric agreed in advance, payback, not CPI. A test-phase client trusts process, not rhetoric. Give him a clean experiment, and he accepts the outcome either way. Winning by experiment design always travels further than winning by argument. [Branch four] And what if the backend data can't be pulled, no MMP, or no per-channel split? Then every comparison is a blind comparison: his claim and mine carry equal, zero, evidence. Methodology, KPI, channel status as in the main branch. So the first move isn't rebuttal, it's helping him integrate an MMP and per-channel tracking. And that's simultaneously the precondition for solving anything and the stickiness itself: the data-integration process IS deep service. Until the data is in, I suspend my conclusion: "two weeks from now, we talk with real numbers." Client relationships that don't conclude on vibes, those are the ones that last. The sequence doesn't change. Calibrate the yardstick first, which costs nothing. Elevate to payback second, re-pricing under the right KPI. Then let a controlled experiment decide. What validates this conversation was never whether I won the argument. It's Google's share of his total budget thirty days later.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 带一个新客户跑通，交付的不是账户，是他自己的判断力。我不在场的时候他还能不能决定该不该加预算，这才是标准。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要弄清五件事：第一，变现模式，IAP、IAA 还是混合？第二，有没有在别的渠道投过，哪怕很小？第三，App 处在什么阶段，刚上架冲量，还是稳定期要增长？第四，技术资源，有开发能接 SDK 吗，还是创始人自己糊？第五，他对"多久见效"的心理预期是什么？ 【主分支：IAP 游戏 + 投过 TikTok + 稳定期要增长 + 有开发 + 预期一个月见效，最顺的配置】 逐项处理： 第一，IAP 变现，KPI 链条定了：首充成本和 D7 ROAS 是北极星。从第一天就把回收模型立起来，不是"先跑量再算账"，是每个阶段都对着回收看。 第二，投过 TikTok，有投放素养：不用教基础概念（出价、素材、预算节奏他都懂），教的是 Google 的差异，搜索意图流量、Play 商店流量、PMax 的素材逻辑和 TikTok 信息流的根本不同。有素养的客户上手快，但也带着旧习惯，差异讲清楚能少走弯路。 第三，稳定期要增长，有历史数据可锚：冷启动目标直接参照他的 TikTok 基准（同素材逻辑下的合理预期区间），不用从零摸索。 第四，有开发，SDK 和 MMP 对接顺畅：技术底座一周内立起来。这一步是后面一切的地基，数据没接对，跑得越快错得越远。 第五，预期一个月见效，合理，但要用小步验证的结构兑现：前两周 tCPA 小预算验证（找到量级和成本的基线），后两周放量（确认基线能 scale）。一个月给他的不是"效果"，是"可复制的基线加放量判断"。 综合：启动路径四步，技术底座、配置正确、小步验证、放量，顺序不可变，每步设通过标准（底座：数据回传准确；验证：成本在目标区间内稳定三天；放量：加预算后成本波动小于 15%）。四步都过，这个客户就从"我带着跑"变成"他自己会跑"。 【分支二：完全没投过，创始人自己动手】 逐项过： 第二，完全没投过、创始人自己摸索，零投放素养：先教语言再教工具。第一周不开户，先聊"你的生意在广告里怎么表达"：用户是谁、为什么下载、付钱买什么，这三个问题答不清，投什么都白投。 第一，假设 IAA 或 IAP 不确定，变现模式本身可能都没跑顺。 第三，假设刚上架。 第四，假设没有开发。 第五，预期往往最不切实际，"投一千赚一万"。 综合：教育型 onboarding，慢就是快：先帮他验证变现模型（没变现的 App 投广告是烧钱），再走四步路径。这种客户的价值在于：他一旦跑通，忠诚度最高，他记得是谁把他从零带起来的。 【分支三：IAA 工具类：回收周期完全不同】 逐项过： 第一，IAA 变现，eCPM 和 ARPU 的平衡是核心：回收周期天然长（用户看广告是渐进的），冷启动期账面上必然"亏"， 第五，所以预期管理变成第一优先级：IAA 是长跑，回本周期以月计不是以周计。这个预期不在投放前对齐，两周后必有一场"没效果"的对话。 第二，投过 TikTok，同主分支。 第三，稳定期要增长，同主分支。 第四，有开发资源，同主分支。 综合：先修预期（给他看同类 IAA App 的回本曲线），再上四步路径，路径一样，节奏完全不同：验证期更长、放量更谨慎。用 IAP 的节奏带 IAA 的客户，是新手 AM 最常犯的错。 【分支四：预期两周就见效，不合理预期】 逐项过： 第五，预期两周见效（大投放量、立刻回本），预期不对齐，跑得再好也会"没效果"：两周的数据本来就不足以支撑任何结论。 第一，IAP 变现，同主分支。 第二，投过 TikTok，同主分支。 第三，稳定期要增长，同主分支。 第四，有开发资源，同主分支。 综合：第一步不是开户，是把"什么叫跑通"定义清楚：量级（能不能跑到目标日耗）、成本（CPI/CPA 在什么区间）、稳定性（成本波动多大），三个维度都定了基线，"见效"才是个可讨论的词。定义不清就开跑，两周后他会拿着自己的想象力和我的数据打架。 【Call back】 四步路径：技术底座、配置正确、小步验证、放量，顺序不能变。前提是"跑通"这个词先对齐。检验标准只有一条，我不在的时候他自己做了正确的加预算决策。

English 口语逐字稿

Getting a new client running delivers an account, but what I'm really delivering is his own judgment. Whether he can decide to raise budget without me in the room is the actual test. [Clarify — before I answer] Five things I'd want to understand. One: monetization, IAP, IAA, or hybrid? Two: have they advertised anywhere else, even small? Three: app stage, fresh launch, or stable and scaling? Four: technical resources, a developer who can integrate an SDK, or the founder DIY-ing? And five: their mental timeline for "seeing results"? [Main branch — the smoothest configuration] So let's say: IAP game, has run TikTok, stable stage seeking growth, has a developer, expects results in a month. Here's how I'd work through it. First, IAP monetization. So the KPI chain is set: first-purchase cost and D7 ROAS are the north stars. And we build the recovery model from day one, not "run volume first, do the math later," but checking every stage against payback. Second, has run TikTok. So there's real advertising literacy: no need to teach basics, bidding, creatives, budget pacing, he knows them all. What needs teaching is Google's difference: search-intent traffic, Play Store traffic, and how PMax's creative logic fundamentally differs from TikTok's feed. Literate clients ramp fast, but they carry old habits, so spelling out the differences early saves detours. Third, stable stage seeking growth. So there's historical data to anchor on: cold-start benchmarks can directly reference his TikTok baselines, a reasonable expectation band under the same creative logic. No need to explore from zero. Fourth, has a developer. So SDK and MMP integration goes smoothly, and the technical foundation stands within a week. And this step is the groundwork for everything after, with data misconfigured, the faster you run, the further off you end up. And fifth, expects results in a month. That's reasonable, but it has to be delivered through a small-step validation structure: weeks one and two, tCPA small-budget validation to find the volume and cost baseline; weeks three and four, scale, confirming the baseline scales. So what a month delivers isn't "results", it's a replicable baseline plus the judgment to scale it. So putting it together, the onboarding path is four steps: foundation, correct configuration, small-step validation, scale. The order is inviolable, and each step has a pass bar. Foundation: data callbacks accurate. Validation: cost stable inside the target band for three days. Scale: cost fluctuation under fifteen percent after adding budget. When all four pass, the client moves from "I run it with them" to "they can run it themselves." [Branch two] Now, what if they've never advertised, the founder is doing everything solo? Then zero advertising literacy: teach the language before the tool. Monetization, possibly not even settled; the model itself may not be proven. Fresh launch, no developer, and the expectations are usually the most unrealistic, "spend a thousand, make ten thousand." So week one isn't account setup, it's "how does your business express itself in advertising": who's the user, why do they download, what do they pay for. If those three can't be answered clearly, anything they run is wasted. It's education-style onboarding, slow is fast. First help validate the monetization model, advertising an unmonetized app is just burning money, then the four-step path. And the value of this client type: once they're running, loyalty is highest. They remember who took them from zero. [Branch three] But what if it's an IAA utility app? Then the eCPM-and-ARPU balance is core, and the payback cycle is naturally long, users watch ads gradually, so the cold-start period will mathematically lose money. Which means expectation management becomes priority one: IAA is a long race, and payback is measured in months, not weeks. If that expectation isn't aligned before spending, a "no results" conversation is guaranteed two weeks in. TikTok experience, stable stage, developer, one-month expectation as before. So: fix expectations first, show him comparable IAA apps' payback curves, then the four-step path. Same path, completely different cadence: longer validation, more cautious scaling. Running an IAA client at IAP pace is the most common rookie-AM mistake. [Branch four] And what if they expect results in two weeks, big volume, instant payback? Then with misaligned expectations, even good performance reads as "no results", because two weeks of data can't support any conclusion. Monetization, channel experience, stage, resources as in the main branch. So step one isn't account setup, it's defining what "working" means: volume, can we hit target daily spend; cost, what CPI or CPA band; stability, how much cost fluctuation. Only when all three baselines are set does "working" become a discussable word. Start without definitions, and in two weeks he'll be fighting my data with his imagination. Four steps: foundation, configuration, validation, scale. The order is fixed. The precondition is agreeing on what "working" means before anything runs. And there's only one test: he makes the right call on raising budget when I'm not in the room.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 一个人推 50 家，靠体力推不动。我关心的是每一步动作能不能复用，对接包、案例库、筛选清单，都是做一次用五十次的东西。 【Clarify · 先问再答】四件事：第一，采用的障碍是什么，客户根本不知道（零接触）、技术对接门槛、还是要重构现有 campaign？第二，这 50 家里谁真的受益，所有人，还是只有某一段？第三，有没有早期采用的成功案例或数据？第四，有没有时间窗口，beta 名额有限、季度 deadline，还是长期都行？ 【主分支：技术对接是障碍 + 只有 20 家真受益 + 有 2-3 个早期案例 + beta 名额有限，最典型场景】 逐项处理： 第一，障碍是技术对接，那就把技术支持前置化：先做一个"对接包"，一步一步的 checklist 加常见报错库加一个能答疑的群。技术对接型的障碍，用产品化的支持解决，不用我逐家陪跑。 第二，50 家里只有 20 家真受益，那就不做全量推送：先筛出目标 20 家，剩下 30 家这次不碰，对不受益的客户推产品，只会消耗信任和我的时间。 第三，有 2-3 个早期成功案例，素材现成了：案例视频加数据截图就是最好的销售材料。早期案例不是拿来庆祝的，是拿来复制打法、拿来当弹药用的。 第四，beta 名额有限，稀缺性就是武器："先到先得、名额有限"给客户一个现在就行动的理由，也给我的跟进一个天然的节奏点。 综合：三波推进，第一波用案例客户打样（把早期案例做成素材，全量可见）；第二波定向推送目标 20 家（案例素材加对接包，一对一跟进有明确话术）；第三波用名额稀缺性收割犹豫者（"还剩 5 个名额"）。每一波有转化指标：第一波看触达率，第二波看对接启动率，第三波看关闭率。 【分支二：零接触障碍，客户根本不知道这个产品】 逐项过： 第一，障碍是认知，客户不是不愿意用，是压根没听说过。认知问题用内容解决不用人力解决：一条三分钟的演示视频加一封说清"对你的价值"的邮件，一次覆盖全部 50 家。 第二，假设受益面较广（认知型产品通常如此）。 第三，有 2 到 3 个早期成功案例，同主分支。 第四，beta 名额有限，同主分支。 综合：认知层用 1-to-many（视频加邮件），意向层才用 1v1（看完视频来问的那批人，就是我的目标名单，内容本身就是筛选器）。单人推新品的正确顺序：先让内容干活，人力只接内容筛出来的热线索。 【分支三：没有早期案例】 逐项过： 第三，没有任何案例和数据，没案例就先造案例：免费给两三家最配合的客户做 pilot，用他们的数据说话。宁可慢两周，不打无准备之仗，没有证据的推广，消耗的是 50 家客户对我的信任额度。 第一，障碍是技术对接，同主分支。 第二，50 家里只有 20 家真受益，同主分支。 第四，beta 名额有限，同主分支。 综合：pilot 阶段选客户有讲究：选配合度高、问题典型的，pilot 的产出不只是数据，是"和你类似"的参照系，让后面 48 家都能对号入座。 【分支四：没有时间窗口，长期都行】 逐项过： 第四，没有 beta 名额、没有 deadline，稀缺性驱动失效：那就靠常态化机制而不是运动式推广：月度产品邮件、案例库持续更新、客户会主动来问的时机（季度复盘、新市场扩张）自然植入。 第一，障碍是技术对接，同主分支。 第二，50 家里只有 20 家真受益，同主分支。 第三，有 2 到 3 个早期成功案例，同主分支。 综合：把推广做成系统而不是 campaign：campaign 是一次性的爆发，系统是持续的水滴，单人作战，只有系统能对抗时间。 【Call back】 结构是漏斗加资产：Clarify 的四个答案决定每一层的形态（认知层用内容、意向层用案例、关闭层用稀缺性），每层沉淀下来的对接包、案例库、筛选清单是资产，下一个产品推广时启动成本减半。

English 口语逐字稿

One person pushing fifty accounts can't do it on effort. What I care about is whether each step is reusable, whether the integration pack, the case library, the screening list are things I build once and use fifty times. [Clarify — before I answer] Four things. One: the barrier, is it zero-touch, technical integration, or restructuring live campaigns? Two: among the fifty, who gets real value, everyone, or a segment? Three: any early-adopter proof or data? And four: a time window, limited beta slots and a quarterly deadline, or open-ended? [Main branch — the classic scenario] So let's say: the barrier is technical integration, only twenty of the fifty truly benefit, there are two or three early cases, and beta slots are limited. Here's how I'd work through it. First, the barrier is technical integration. So I productize the support before touching anyone: build an integration pack, a step-by-step checklist, a common-errors library, and a group chat that can answer questions. Technical barriers get solved with productized support, not by me hand-holding each account. Second, only twenty of the fifty truly benefit. So no full-list blast: screen the target twenty first, and the other thirty aren't touched this round. Pushing a product at clients who don't benefit only burns trust, and my time. Third, two or three early success cases exist. So the material is ready: a case video plus data screenshots is the best sales asset there is. And early cases aren't for celebrating, they're for replicating the playbook and serving as ammunition. And fourth, beta slots are limited. Scarcity is the weapon: "first come, first served, limited slots" gives clients a reason to act now, and gives my follow-ups a natural rhythm. So putting it together: three waves. Wave one, showcase with the case clients, turn early cases into material, visible to all. Wave two, targeted push to the twenty, case material plus integration pack, with a clear talk track for one-on-one follow-up. Wave three, harvest the hesitators with scarcity, "five slots left." And each wave carries a conversion metric: wave one, reach rate; wave two, integration-start rate; wave three, close rate. [Branch two] Now, what if the barrier is zero-touch, clients don't even know the product exists? Then they're not unwilling, they've simply never heard of it. And awareness gets solved with content, not headcount. Benefiting segment, assume it's broad, which is usually true for awareness-stage products. Cases and window as before. So: one three-minute demo video plus one email stating "what this means for you" covers all fifty in one shot. Awareness layer runs 1-to-many, video plus email. The intent layer runs 1v1: those who ask after watching are my target list, the content itself is the filter. And that's the right order for a solo product push: let content do the work, and spend human hours only on the warm leads content surfaces. [Branch three] But what if there are no early cases, no proof, no data? Then build cases first: free pilots for the two or three most cooperative clients, and let their data speak. Barrier, benefiting segment, window as in the main branch. Better two weeks slower than an unprepared push, promoting without evidence burns my trust budget across all fifty. And pilot-client selection matters: pick high-cooperation clients with typical problems, because the pilot's output isn't just data, it's a reference frame of "someone like you," letting the other forty-eight see themselves in it. [Branch four] And what if there's no time window at all, no beta slots, no deadline, whenever? Then the scarcity lever is dead, and I rely on standing mechanisms instead of campaign-style pushes. Barrier, segment, cases as before. A monthly product email, a continuously updated case library, and natural insertion at the moments clients come asking, quarterly reviews, new-market expansions. Make promotion a system, not a campaign. A campaign is a one-time burst; a system is a persistent drip. Fighting alone, only a system outlasts time. The structure is funnel plus assets. The four Clarify answers shape each funnel layer: awareness through content, intent through cases, closing through scarcity. And whatever each layer deposits, the integration pack, the case library, the screening list, is an asset. Next product launch, my startup cost halves.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 1v1 和一对多是两种产品，不是同一件事的豪华版和经济版。把标准问题拿去做 1v1，是最常见的浪费。 【Clarify · 先问再答】五件事：第一，客户阶段，新客、成长期还是成熟期？第二，预算规模，月耗什么量级？第三，问题复杂度，标准配置问题，还是多市场、增长瓶颈这种定制问题？第四，关系深度，熟到随时能约，还是只是名单上的名字？第五，我当前的精力带宽怎么样？ 【主分支：成熟头部客户 + 月耗 50 万+ + 复杂问题 + 关系深 + 精力有限，1v1 的教科书场景】 逐项处理： 第一，成熟期客户，业务稳定、需求明确，1v1 的每一分钟都花在刀刃上，不存在冷启动的教育成本。 第二，月耗 50 万以上，金额决定了服务密度的经济性：这个量级客户掉 10% 的消耗，等于长尾十家客户全停。资源天然应该向这里倾斜。 第三，问题复杂（多市场协同、增长瓶颈诊断），批量方法论覆盖不了：复杂问题需要看他的数据、他的组织、他的节奏，只有 1v1 能做到。 第四，关系深，1v1 不只解决问题，1v1 本身就是关系维护：成熟关系是需要"被看见"的，季度当面复盘就是被看见。 第五，精力有限，正因为有限，1v1 必须配一个纪律：每次 1v1 产出可复用的东西，这个客户的解法能不能沉淀成同品类的方案模板。可复用性是 1v1 的溢价条款：没有它，1v1 就是纯消耗。 综合：1v1 的判断标准不是"客户大"，是三个维度同时高，金额、复杂度、关系。三个都高，1v1 是投资；只有金额高，1v1 是慈善。 【分支二：问题标准化，常见配置、常规优化】 逐项过： 第三，问题是标准化的（配置错误、常规扩量、报表解读），标准问题必须标准化解决：月度线上 office hour、自助文档库、自动化的账户健康报告。对标准问题做 1v1，是浪费我的时间，更是浪费客户的时间，他等排期的时间比看文档的时间长。 第一，成熟期头部客户，同主分支。 第二，月耗 50 万以上，同主分支。 第四，关系深，同主分支。 第五，精力有限，同主分支。 综合：1-to-many 不是低配服务，是把标准答案产品化：文档写得比我说得清楚，因为它迭代过五十次。客户体验的差别不在渠道，在"多快拿到正确答案"。 【分支三：长尾教育型新客】 逐项过： 第一，长尾新客，教育需求为主：量最大、单价值最低，1v1 在数学上不成立。 第二，预算小。 第三，问题不复杂但频次高（同样的开户、配置、基础问题反复问）。 第四，关系浅，还没有到值得 1v1 的信任浓度。 第五，精力有限。 综合：1-to-many 养池子（onboarding 直播课、邮件序列、FAQ 库），1v1 收网，但收网要有触发器："毕业信号"（消耗到某个量级、出现明确增长信号、主动提出战略问题）自动把客户从批量池升级到我的 1v1 名单。触发器要自动化，靠人脑记住谁该升级，一定会漏。 【分支四：关系出现裂痕】 逐项过： 第四，关系出问题（上次交付不满意、被竞品撬过、投诉过），关系修复没有规模化方案，只有面对面：客户在情绪里的时候，任何批量动作都会被解读为"不被重视"。 第一，成熟期头部客户，同主分支。 第二，月耗 50 万以上，同主分支。 第三，问题复杂，多市场、增长瓶颈，同主分支。 第五，精力有限，同主分支。 综合：修复期的 1v1 优先级高于一切商业逻辑，先修信任再谈生意。这一条没有例外：数据可以批量看，情绪必须当面接。 【Call back】 判断框架一句话：金额和复杂度决定值不值得 1v1，可复用性决定这个 1v1 做得值不值，关系状态决定今天要不要立刻做。至于一对多，它是另一种产品：文档写得比我说得清楚，直播讲得比我一对一讲得省时间。做得好的一对多，比敷衍的 1v1 更让客户觉得被尊重。

English 口语逐字稿

1v1 and one-to-many are two products, not the deluxe and the economy version of one service. Spending 1v1 on a standard problem is the most common waste I see. [Clarify — before I answer] Five things. One: client stage, new, growing, or mature? Two: budget scale, monthly spend at what level? Three: problem complexity, standard configuration, or multi-market, growth-bottleneck custom work? Four: relationship depth, close enough to book anytime, or just a name on the list? And five: my current bandwidth? [Main branch — the textbook 1v1 case] So let's say: mature head client, five hundred K-plus a month, complex problem, deep relationship, limited bandwidth. Here's how I'd work through it. First, mature-stage client. Stable business, clear needs, every 1v1 minute lands on the blade, no cold-start education tax. Second, five hundred K-plus a month. Scale decides the economics of service density: this client dropping ten percent equals the entire long tail of ten clients going dark. Resources naturally tilt here. Third, the problem is complex, multi-market coordination, growth-bottleneck diagnosis. Batch playbooks can't cover it: complex problems require their data, their org, their rhythm. Only 1v1 gets there. Fourth, deep relationship. And this one's subtle: 1v1 isn't just problem-solving, the 1v1 itself IS relationship maintenance. Mature relationships need to be seen, and the quarterly in-person review is being seen. And fifth, limited bandwidth. Precisely because it's limited, 1v1 carries one discipline: every session must produce something reusable, can this client's solution become a category template? Reusability is 1v1's premium clause. Without it, 1v1 is pure consumption. So putting it together: the 1v1 test isn't "the client is big", it's three dimensions high at once: money, complexity, relationship. All three high, 1v1 is an investment. Only money high, 1v1 is charity. [Branch two] Now, what if the problems are standardized, routine configuration and optimization? Stage, budget, relationship, bandwidth as before. Then standard problems must be solved standardly: monthly online office hours, a self-serve doc library, automated account-health reports. Doing 1v1 for standard problems wastes my time, and, more importantly, the client's: their wait for a slot outlasts reading the doc. And here's the reframe: 1-to-many isn't a downgrade, it's productizing the standard answer. Documentation explains better than I speak, because it's been iterated fifty times. The client-experience difference isn't the channel, it's "how fast do I get the right answer." [Branch three] But what if it's long-tail educational new clients? Then: new stage, small budgets, problems not complex but high-frequency, the same setup, config, and basic questions asked again and again. Shallow relationships, not yet at the trust concentration that earns 1v1. Bandwidth still limited. So: 1-to-many farms the pond, onboarding live classes, email sequences, an FAQ library. 1v1 closes, but closing needs a trigger: graduation signals, spend crossing a threshold, a clear growth signal, proactively raising strategic questions, automatically promote a client from the batch pool onto my 1v1 list. And the trigger must be automated. Relying on memory to catch who's ready guarantees misses. [Branch four] And what if the relationship is cracked, a disappointing delivery, poached by a competitor, a complaint filed? Stage, budget, complexity, bandwidth as before. Then relationship repair has no scaled solution, only face-to-face. Because when a client is in an emotional state, every batch motion reads as "not valued." Repair-phase 1v1 outranks all commercial logic: fix trust before discussing business. And this one has no exceptions. Data can be read in batch. Emotions must be received in person. My judgment framework in one line: money and complexity decide whether 1v1 is worth it, reusability decides whether that 1v1 was done well, relationship state decides whether it needs to happen today. And one-to-many isn't a consolation prize, it's a product. A good one-to-many respects the client more than a perfunctory 1v1 does.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 毕业不是看谁够大。量级、需求、时机三个条件同时成立才走，缺一个就是错的。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要确认四件事：第一，公司有没有明确的毕业标准，比如消耗门槛？第二，上一层团队的服务模型，人均管多少家、提供什么深度？第三，这个客户最近的需求有没有变化，开始要定制方案、多市场扩张、季度战略会了？第四，毕业后我的业绩归属怎么算？ 【主分支：有软性标准无硬门槛 + 上层人均 15 家 + 客户开始要定制方案 + 毕业率计入我的考核，最需要主动判断的场景】 逐项处理： 第一，公司只有软性标准、没有硬门槛，判断权在我手上：判断越自由，越要用数据说话，不能凭感觉。 第二，上层人均管 15 家深度服务，接收容量有限：毕业要有节奏，不能一口气推五家，推得太多上层接不住，服务密度稀释，最后伤害的是客户。 第三，客户开始要定制归因方案、多市场扩张、季度战略会，这是最超前也最可靠的毕业信号：我的标准答案已经喂不饱他了。当客户的需求超出我的服务模型，毕业时钟就已经启动，这时候等消耗达标才动手，交接必然仓促。 第四，毕业率计入我的考核，那推动毕业是我的业绩动作而不是牺牲：主动识别、主动推进。 综合：建一个"毕业候选雷达"，自动监控"消耗接近门槛、且需求信号出现"的客户，提前一个季度标记，提前和上层团队沟通容量。交接做三件事：30 天 co-pilot 保证增长曲线不断；一场三方会议（我、接收的 AM、客户）把毕业包装成客户的"升级"，他的感受必须是升级，不是被转手；留一份档案，成长路径、有效打法，既是交接质量，也是可复用到下一批同类客户的方法论资产。 【分支二：公司没有明确的毕业标准】 逐项过： 第一，公司完全没有标准，那我不会自己拍脑袋定，会先和上层团队对齐判断框架：拿两三个真实客户讨论"这个该不该毕业"，把隐性标准显性化。这本身就有价值：标准不透明的时候，要么该毕业的客户被拖着（客户受损，我的模型喂不饱他），要么不该毕业的被硬推上去（上层服务成本浪费，大炮打蚊子）。 第二，上层人均管 15 家，同主分支。 第三，客户开始要定制方案，同主分支。 第四，毕业率计入我的考核，同主分支。 综合：把标准谈出来的过程，也是和上层建立"客户共同所有"意识的过程，毕业管道是两个团队之间的事，不是我单方面的动作。 【分支三：消耗到了但没有任何升级需求】 逐项过： 第三，客户消耗到了门槛但从没提过任何定制需求，可能是"大而不深"的客户：量级到了但服务深度不需要升级，硬推上去上层接不住（他的问题还是标准问题，只是更贵了）。 第一，假设有硬门槛，门槛触发了流程，但流程不等于正确。 第二，上层人均管 15 家，同主分支。 第四，毕业率计入我的考核，同主分支。 综合：先确认增长是结构性的还是一次性的：结构性（多市场铺开、品类扩张）才值得毕业；一次性（一波爆款素材带起来的）等三个季度看稳定性。数字达线只是毕业的必要条件，需求才是充分条件。 【分支四：毕业后业绩全额转出且不计功】 逐项过： 第四，业绩全额转出、毕业不计功，激励错位出现了：制度告诉我"留住客户"，客户利益告诉我"送他上去"。但拖延交接伤害客户增长（我的模型喂不饱他），长期看客户流失了我什么都没有。 第一，公司有软性标准、无硬门槛，同主分支。 第二，上层人均管 15 家，同主分支。 第三，客户开始要定制方案，同主分支。 综合：正确动作是推动机制而不是消极怠工：带着系统性论证去谈，"Scaled 岗的激励如果不认毕业，没有人愿意把客户养大，graduation pipeline 会枯竭，这是组织级风险，不是我个人的委屈。"同时用事实积累筹码：持续输出毕业成果，让上层团队公开认可我的客户质量。机制谈判靠论证，绝不靠抱怨。 【Call back】 三个条件是与门：量级接近上层经济线、需求超出我的模型、时机不打断增长曲线，三个都亮才走。验证看毕业后 90 天的消耗曲线，继续涨说明交接成功，掉头说明毕业太早。

English 口语逐字稿

Graduation isn't about who's big enough. Scale, demand and timing all have to be true at once. Miss one and graduating them is wrong. [Clarify — before I answer] Four things. One: does the company have an explicit graduation bar, a spend threshold? Two: the next tier's service model, accounts per rep, depth provided? Three: has this client's demand changed lately, asking for custom solutions, multi-market expansion, quarterly strategy sessions? And four: how is my credit calculated after graduation? [Main branch — the scenario demanding proactive judgment] So let's say: a soft standard with no hard bar, the tier above runs fifteen accounts per rep, the client has started asking for custom work, and graduation counts in my evaluation. Here's how I'd work through it. First, only a soft standard, no hard bar. So the judgment call is mine. And the freer the judgment, the more it must speak in data, never gut feel. Second, the tier above runs fifteen accounts per rep. So receiving capacity is finite: graduation needs cadence. Pushing five at once overwhelms the receiving team, dilutes service density, and the client pays the price. Third, the client has started asking for custom attribution, multi-market expansion, quarterly strategy sessions. That's the earliest and most reliable graduation signal: my standard answers no longer feed them. And when a client's needs exceed my service model, the graduation clock has already started. Waiting for the spend number to trigger means a rushed handover. And fourth, graduation rate counts in my evaluation. So driving graduation is a performance motion, not a sacrifice: identify proactively, push proactively. So putting it together: I'd build a graduation-candidate radar, automatically watching for "spend approaching the bar plus demand signals appearing," flagging one quarter ahead and syncing capacity with the tier above early. And the handover runs on three moves: a thirty-day co-pilot, so the growth curve never breaks. A three-way meeting, me, the receiving AM, the client, that packages graduation as the client's upgrade: their felt experience must be promotion, never being palmed off. And a file left behind, growth path, plays that worked, both handover quality and a methodology asset reusable for the next cohort. [Branch two] Now, what if the company has no graduation standard at all? Bar, service model, demand signals, credit as before. Then my first move isn't inventing one alone, it's aligning a judgment framework with the tier above: take two or three real clients and debate "should this one graduate," making the implicit standard explicit. And that process is valuable in itself, because when the standard is opaque, either deserving clients get dragged, the client suffers, my model can't feed them, or undeserving ones get pushed up, the tier above wastes service cost, a cannon on a mosquito. And here's the bonus: the process of negotiating the standard is also the process of building shared ownership of clients between teams. The graduation pipeline is a two-team affair, never my unilateral move. [Branch three] But what if the spend has arrived, but the client has never raised a single custom request? So there's a hard bar, it triggered the process, but process isn't the same as correct. Service model and credit as before. Then this might be a "big but shallow" client: the scale arrived, but their service depth doesn't need upgrading, pushing them up means the tier above can't serve them well, because their problems are still standard problems, only more expensive now. So first, confirm the growth is structural, not one-off: structural, multi-market rollout, category expansion, earns graduation. One-off, a single viral creative wave, waits three quarters for stability. Hitting the number is the necessary condition. Demand is the sufficient one. [Branch four] And what if credit transfers in full and graduation earns nothing? Then there's an incentive misalignment: the system tells me "keep the client," the client's interest tells me "send them up." Bar, service model, demand signals as before. But delaying the handover hurts their growth, my model can't feed them, and long-term, a churned client leaves me with nothing. So the right move is pushing the mechanism, not quiet sabotage. I'd bring the systemic argument: "if Scaled incentives don't recognize graduation, no one will raise clients to full size, and the graduation pipeline runs dry. That's an organizational risk, not my personal grievance." And meanwhile, accumulate chips with facts: keep shipping graduation outcomes until the tier above publicly vouches for my client quality. Mechanism negotiations run on arguments, never on complaints. Three conditions, and it's an AND gate: scale near the tier's economic line, demand beyond my model, timing that doesn't break the growth curve. All three on, or no go. Validation is the ninety-day post-graduation spend curve.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 不想丢是人之常情，但怎么做由两件事定：制度的归属规则，和客户自己的增长利益。规则先问清楚。 【Clarify · 先问再答】我要弄清三件事：第一，业绩归属规则，毕业后消耗全额转出、有过渡期共享、还是毕业贡献另算？第二，我的考核里毕业率占多大权重？第三，拖延交接会不会伤害客户增长，答案几乎必然是会。 【主分支：过渡期共享 + 毕业率核心权重 + 拖延伤客户，制度友好型，答出深度即可】 逐项处理： 第一，业绩归属是过渡期共享（毕业后一到两个季度分成），制度已经部分缓冲了损失：我的动作是把过渡期用满，30 天 co-pilot 期我继续深度参与，分成照拿，客户体验无损。 第二，毕业率在考核里占核心权重，那"送走客户"本身就是我业绩的一部分：这道题里的"损失感"是个伪命题，我损失的是盘子里的存量，换来的是考核里的毕业指标。 第三，拖延交接伤害客户增长，客户利益给了明确答案：立刻交。我的服务模型喂不饱一个长大的客户，拖延不是留业绩，是慢性失去他，真被竞品或被客户自己的不满弄丢了，存量也保不住。 综合：关键是把毕业贡献显性化：季度复盘里明确量化，"本季度毕业 3 家、合计贡献多少增量消耗、其中 1 家是我从零养起来的。"这些数字不说出来，老板看到的就只是我的盘子变小了。同时每次毕业做三件事：三方会议让客户感到升级而不是被转手；一份交接档案（客户画像、增长路径、有效打法、风险点），专业度的证明；30 天 co-pilot 过渡，曲线不断，口碑不倒。 【分支二：全额转出 + 毕业不计功，制度不友好，修制度不修行为】 逐项过： 第一，业绩全额转出、毕业不计功，那我要修的是制度，不是我的行为：消极拖延是最差解，伤客户、伤口碑、最后存量也守不住。 第二，毕业率权重为零，激励彻底错位。 第三，拖延依然伤客户，这个物理规律不因制度而改变。 综合：带着系统性论证去推动制度："Scaled 岗的激励如果不认毕业，没有人愿意把客户养大，graduation pipeline 会枯竭，这是组织级风险。"同时用事实积累谈判筹码：持续输出毕业成果，让上层团队公开认可我的客户质量，当毕业成为我的标签，制度迟早追上事实。机制谈判靠论证，绝不靠抱怨。 【分支三：毕业率权重很低，考核主要看消耗】 逐项过： 第二，考核里毕业率权重很低、主要看消耗，那激励和正确动作是错位的：制度实际上在奖励"把长大的客户藏着"。 第一，假设归属规则模糊。 第三，拖延伤客户。 综合：主动找老板对齐两件事：要么调权重（把毕业贡献写进考核），要么把我的毕业贡献放进 review 叙事，"我的盘子今年毕业出去 400 万，这 400 万是公司视角的净增量，不是我的损失。"制度改不了的时候，用叙事和可见性补位，可见性本身也是谈判筹码。 【分支四：上层没容量接，客户长大了但接不走】 逐项过： 第三，这个问题换了个方向：不是我不想交，是上层接不住（人均饱和），但客户的需求不会等上层有空：我的模型喂不饱他的每一天，都是竞品的机会窗口。 第一，业绩归属是过渡期共享，同主分支。 第二，毕业率在考核里占核心权重，同主分支。 综合：和上层一起排交接时间表（哪怕分阶段交：先交战略层，我保留执行支持），过渡期我继续深度服务但要明确"毕业倒计时"，无限期的"暂缓交接"等于无限期地用小杯装大客户，杯具的是客户。 【Call back】 每一次毕业客户的方法论我都沉淀下来用到下一批，毕业产能会越养越高。验证看三个数：毕业后 90 天客户的消耗曲线、我的年毕业量、以及上一层团队愿不愿意优先接我的客户。

English 口语逐字稿

Not wanting to lose the revenue is human. But what I do gets set by two things: the rule on who owns the account, and what's best for the client's growth. I ask about the rule first. [Clarify — before I answer] Three things. One: the credit rules, full transfer after graduation, a shared transition window, or a separate graduation ledger? Two: how heavily does graduation rate weigh in my evaluation? And three: would delaying the handover hurt the client's growth, and the answer is almost certainly yes. [Main branch — a friendly system] So let's say: the credit follows a shared transition, one to two quarters of split credit post-graduation, graduation carries core weight in my evaluation, and delay would hurt the client. Here's how I'd work through it. First, the shared transition window. The system has already buffered part of the loss, so my move is to use the window fully: during the thirty-day co-pilot, I stay deeply involved, collect the shared credit, and the client experience never dips. Second, graduation at core weight. Then "sending the client up" IS part of my performance. The "loss" in this question is a false premise: what I lose is stock in my book; what I gain is the graduation metric in my review. And third, delay hurts the client. The client's interest gives the clear answer: hand over now. My service model can't feed a grown client, and delay isn't retaining revenue, it's slowly losing them. If churn or frustration takes them, the stock is gone too. So the key motion: make graduation contribution visible. I'd quantify it explicitly in quarterly reviews, "three graduations this quarter, this much combined incremental spend, one of them grown from zero by me." Because if I don't say it, all the manager sees is my book shrinking. And every graduation runs three moves: a three-way meeting so the client feels promoted, not transferred; a handover file, client profile, growth path, plays that worked, risk points, proof of professionalism; and a thirty-day co-pilot transition, curve unbroken, reputation intact. [Branch two] Now, what if it's full transfer and graduation earns nothing? Then the system is unfriendly: what needs fixing is the mechanism, not my behavior. Delay still hurts the client, that law of physics doesn't change with the org chart. And passive foot-dragging is the worst option, it hurts the client, the reputation, and ultimately fails to keep the stock anyway. So I'd push the mechanism with a systemic argument: "if Scaled incentives don't recognize graduation, no one raises clients to full size, the graduation pipeline runs dry. That's an organizational risk, not my grievance." And meanwhile, accumulate negotiating chips with facts: keep shipping graduation outcomes until the tier above publicly vouches for my client quality. When graduation becomes my label, the mechanism eventually catches up to the facts. [Branch three] But what if graduation barely weighs, evaluation keys on spend? Then incentives and correct action are misaligned: the system effectively rewards hiding grown clients. Credit rules ambiguous, delay still hurts. So I'd proactively align with my manager on two things: either adjust the weighting, write graduation contribution into the scorecard, or put my graduation contribution into the review narrative: "four million in spend graduated out of my book this year, from the company's view, that's net increment, not my loss." And when the mechanism can't change, bridge with narrative and visibility. Visibility itself is negotiating capital. [Branch four] And what if the tier above has no capacity, the client grew, but can't be received? Then the question flips: I'm not reluctant, they're saturated. But the client's needs won't wait for their calendar: every day my model underfeeds a grown client is a competitor's window. Credit and weight as in the main branch. So I'd build a phased handover schedule with the tier above, even staged: strategy layer first, I retain execution support, continuing deep service through the transition, but with an explicit graduation countdown. Because an indefinite deferral means indefinitely serving a big client in a small cup. And the tragedy lands on the client. Every graduation leaves me a methodology I deposit and reuse on the next cohort, so my graduation throughput compounds. Other roles defend stock; I run a production line of incremental clients. I check three numbers: the ninety-day post-graduation spend curve, my annual graduation count, and whether the tier above wants my clients first.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 排查的顺序比全面性重要。先查不花钱的（口径），再看数据（素材、产品），最后才动钱（竞价）。顺序错了钱就白花。 【Clarify · 先问再答】五件事：第一，掉的是哪条产品线，PMax 主力、搜索、还是整体？第二，是季节性波动还是真问题，去年同期掉没掉？第三，归因数据最近有没有变更，SKAN 口径、MMP 配置动过吗？第四，素材多久没换了？第五，竞品最近有没有大动作，新游上线、加价抢量？ 【主分支：PMax 主力掉 + 非季节 + SKAN 口径最近变更 + 素材 3 周没换 + 竞品在加价，多因素叠加的最真实场景】 逐项处理： 第一，掉的是 PMax 主力 campaign，影响面最大、优先级最高，但也最可能是多因素叠加（PMax 是黑盒，输入的素材和数据变了，输出就变）。 第二，去年同期没掉，排除季节性：这是真问题，不是波动，需要动手。 第三，SKAN 口径最近变更过，第一嫌疑：归因口径变了，数据就变了，实际效果可能没变。验证动作：拉 MMP 侧的数据交叉对比，如果 MMP 显示平稳而 ads 侧掉了，那是口径问题不是效果问题，一分钱不用花。 第四，素材 3 周没换，第二嫌疑：素材疲劳。PMax 尤其吃素材新鲜度，验证动作：看素材的展示份额和 eCPM 趋势，衰退的素材换新跑一周对照组。 第五，竞品在加价抢量，第三嫌疑：竞价环境恶化推高 CPA。验证动作：看 auction insight 的竞品渗透率变化，和分时段的 CPC 趋势。 综合：三个嫌疑按成本排序处理，先查口径（零成本：数据交叉验证）、再查素材（低成本：换新对照）、最后查竞价（高成本：调出价或避锋）。每个假设对应一个验证动作和一个预期结果，验证完自然收敛到根因。给客户的输出是一张排查表：现象、嫌疑、验证、结论、动作，他看到的是方法论，不只是答案。 【分支二：口径没变、素材也新，排除了免费嫌疑之后】 逐项过： 第三，SKAN 口径没动、MMP 正常，排除口径问题。 第四，素材每周都在换，排除素材疲劳。 第五，竞品无异常。 那剩下的嫌疑指向产品端，App 自己变了：版本更新改了内购定价、评分下滑、或者留存出了问题，广告端的 ROAS 是产品健康度的放大器。 第一，掉的是 PMax 主力 campaign，同主分支。 第二，去年同期没掉，非季节性，同主分支。 综合：和客户的产品团队对数据：版本更新时间线和 ROAS 下滑时间线对齐看，如果时间吻合，根因在产品不在投放。这时候我能做的是帮他把投放结构适配新的产品现实（比如收缩到留存还健康的市场），同时等产品侧修复。查不完的锅，要诚实地指出在哪。 【分支三：去年同期也掉了 18%，季节性假阳性】 逐项过： 第二，拉两年数据对比发现去年同期掉了 18%，那这基本是季节性规律（节后回落、暑期结束），20% 的跌幅里有 18% 是"每年都有"，真问题只有 2 个点。 第一，掉的是 PMax 主力 campaign，同主分支。 第三，SKAN 口径最近变更过，同主分支。 第四，素材 3 周没换，同主分支。 第五，竞品在加价抢量，同主分支。 综合：给客户看两年的曲线叠加，预期管理而不是紧急动作："这是您品类的季节性，每年这个月都掉，Q4 会回来。"客户看到规律就不慌了。只有偏离规律的异常才值得动作，这也是数据分析的第一课：单点数据没有意义，基准线才有。 【分支四：竞品大动作，头部竞品游戏上线抢量】 逐项过： 第五，竞品头部新游上线、全渠道加价，环境性恶化：同样的钱买不到原来的量，这是所有广告主的共同处境，不是他的账户问题。 第一，掉的是 PMax 主力 campaign，同主分支。 第二，去年同期没掉，非季节性，同主分支。 第三，SKAN 口径最近变更过，同主分支。 第四，素材 3 周没换，同主分支。 综合：环境战打不起就避战：短期保 ROI 收预算（等竞品的爆量期过去，新游的投放高峰通常 4-6 周），同时找竞品覆盖弱的时段和市场补量。给客户的数据：竞品上线前后全品类的 CPC 变化，证明这是潮汐不是他一个人的问题。潮汐来了不硬游，等潮退。 【Call back】 我交的是一张排查表：口径、素材、产品、竞价四个嫌疑，每个配一个验证动作和成本标签，按成本从低到高查。给客户的不只是修复，还有一次"问题是怎么被系统排查出来的"的示范。

English 口语逐字稿

Sequence matters more than coverage. Check the free things first, meaning methodology. Then the data, meaning creative and product. Then the things that cost money, meaning the auction. Get the order wrong and the money is wasted. [Clarify — before I answer] Five things. One: which line dropped, the PMax flagship, search, or everything? Two: seasonal, or a real problem, did it drop this time last year? Three: has attribution changed lately, SKAN methodology, MMP configuration touched? Four: how long since creatives were refreshed? And five: competitor moves, a big launch, aggressive bidding? [Main branch — the multi-factor reality] So let's say: the drop sits on the PMax flagship, it didn't drop last year, SKAN methodology was recently changed, creatives are three weeks stale, and competitors are bidding up. Here's how I'd work through it. First, the drop sits on the PMax flagship. Biggest blast radius, highest priority, but also most likely multi-factor, because PMax is a black box: change the creative inputs or the data, and the output moves. Second, no drop this time last year. Seasonality excluded: this is a real problem, not noise. Action required. Third, SKAN methodology recently changed. That's suspect number one: the attribution yardstick moved, so the data moved, actual performance may not have. Verification: cross-check the MMP side. If MMP shows flat while the ads side is down twenty percent, it's a methodology artifact, not a performance problem. Cost: zero. Fourth, creatives three weeks stale. Suspect number two: creative fatigue, and PMax especially feeds on freshness. Verification: check the creatives' impression share and eCPM trends; refresh the decaying ones and run a one-week control. And fifth, competitors bidding up. Suspect number three: auction deterioration inflating CPA. Verification: auction-insight overlap trends, and CPC by daypart. So putting it together: I process the three suspects in cost order, methodology first, zero cost, just a data cross-check. Creatives next, low cost, refresh and control. Bidding last, high cost, adjust or sidestep. Every hypothesis pairs with one verification and one expected outcome, the root cause converges by elimination. And what the client receives is a diagnostic table: symptom, suspect, verification, conclusion, action. He sees methodology, not just an answer. [Branch two] Now, what if the methodology is unchanged and the creatives are fresh? Then the free suspects clear: SKAN untouched, MMP normal, creatives refreshed weekly, no competitor anomaly. And that points the remaining suspect at the product, the app itself changed. A version update repricing IAP, ratings sliding, retention cracking, because advertising ROAS is an amplifier of product health. Line and seasonality as in the main branch. So I'd align timelines with the client's product team: version-release dates against the ROAS slide. If they coincide, the root cause is product, not media. What I can do then: restructure spend around the new product reality, contracting to markets where retention still holds, while the product side repairs. And when the trail leads out of my lane, I say so honestly. [Branch three] But what if it dropped eighteen percent this same week last year? Then pulling two years of data shows a seasonal pattern, post-holiday slump, summer ending. Eighteen of the twenty points are "every year," leaving two points of real problem. Line, methodology, creatives, competitors as before. So: expectation management, not emergency motion. Show the client the two-year overlay: "this is your category's seasonality, it drops every year this month, and it returns in Q4." A client who sees the pattern stops panicking. Only deviations from the pattern deserve action, and that's data analysis lesson one: a single point means nothing. Only a baseline does. [Branch four] And what if there's a major competitor move, a flagship game launching and buying everything? Then it's environmental deterioration: the same money no longer buys the same volume, and it's every advertiser's condition, not his account's fault. Line, seasonality, methodology, creatives as before. So: when you can't outswim the tide, don't. Short-term, protect ROI and pull budget back, competitor launch peaks typically run four to six weeks, while filling volume in the dayparts and markets the competitor under-covers. And give the client the data: category-wide CPC before and after the launch, proving it's a tide, not his solo problem. When the tide comes in, you don't fight it. You wait for it to go out. What I deliver isn't a tweak, it's a diagnostic table. Four suspects: methodology, creative, product, auction. Each with a verification and a cost tag, worked from zero cost upward. And what the client gets isn't only the fix. It's the first time he's watched a problem get systematically run down.

中文逐字稿

【开场定位】 80 家客户做检查，它必须是个产品而不是一个任务。产品设计就两处：什么算异常，异常之后先看谁。 【Clarify · 先问再答】四件事：第一，团队结构，有优化师支持吗，几个？第二，有没有自动化工具，脚本、平台、还是纯手动？第三，异常的阈值怎么设，有没有明确定义？第四，发现异常后我有没有时间跟进，这决定异常清单能有多长。 【主分支：有 2 个优化师 + 有基础脚本 + 阈值未定义 + 时间有限，最常见起点】 逐项处理： 第一，有 2 个优化师，人可以做深度诊断，但先算人效：80 家全人工过一遍，两周一轮都赶不上，人只应该处理机器处理不了的。 第二，有基础脚本，可以自动拉数：消耗趋势、CPI/ROAS 水平、素材更换频率、账户结构变化，先跑全量扫描，把 80 家变成一张表。 第三，阈值未定义，这是最关键的缺口：没有阈值就没有"异常"这个概念，扫描出来的只是数据不是信号。阈值按分层设：头部客户敏感（周环比波动超 15% 报警，他们掉一点就是大钱）、长尾客户只看断崖（停投、掉 50% 以上，小幅波动不值得人工看）。另外加一类结构性异常：素材两周没换、追踪配置报错，不等业绩掉就先亮灯。 第四，时间有限，异常清单必须分级：P0（头部客户急变）当天处理，P1（腰部异常）三天内，P2（长尾小异常）进周度批量。清单长度由跟进能力反推，宁可阈值收紧一点，也不要一张没人处理的清单。 综合：三层漏斗，脚本全量扫描（每周一）、阈值过滤出异常（同一时刻）、分级处理（P0 当天、P1 三天、P2 周度批量）。优化师接 P0 和 P1 的深度诊断，我处理需要客户对话的部分。周五复盘漏斗指标：扫描覆盖率、异常检出数、P0 清零率，监控本身也要被监控。 【分支二：完全没有工具，纯手动】 逐项过： 第二，没有任何自动化，那第一动作是造工具：哪怕最粗糙的（表格加公式加每周手动导数），也比人肉强。80 家的检查，人海战术必漏，漏的不是数据，是漏在没人记得哪家上次就不对劲。 第一，有 2 个优化师，同主分支。 第三，阈值未定义，同主分支。 第四，时间有限，同主分支。 综合：最粗糙的自动化也优于最勤奋的人工：第一周搭一个表格模板（80 行、10 个字段、3 条条件格式规则），以后每周只花两小时维护。工具不必完美，必须存在。 【分支三：纯单人、无优化师】 逐项过： 第一，纯单人无优化师，1v1 跟进做不完所有异常：我的时间只够处理"自动化解决不了的"（客户对话、策略调整）。 第二，有基础脚本，同主分支。 第三，阈值未定义，同主分支。 第四，时间有限，同主分支。 综合：把人力留给只有人能做的事：P2 级异常用模板化建议批量触达（"检测到您的素材两周未更新，建议参考附件 checklist"），P0 和 P1 才亲自上。单人的杠杆不在勤奋，在模板，每一类异常都该有一个标准回应模板。 【分支四：发现异常后没时间跟进】 逐项过： 第四，检查发现了异常但没人有时间跟进，那检查本身是浪费：没有闭环的监控只是仪表盘装饰，还消耗维护成本。 第一，有 2 个优化师，同主分支。 第二，有基础脚本，同主分支。 第三，阈值未定义，同主分支。 综合：宁可降低检查频率也要保证跟进闭环：从每周一次降到双周一次，但每次检出的异常 100% 有归属、有时限。监控的意义在闭环，closure rate 是这套系统唯一的成功指标。 【Call back】 一套三层漏斗：扫描、过滤、分级。两条设计原则，阈值按分层设（头部敏感、长尾看断崖），清单长度由跟进能力反推。这套系统每周输出的除了异常清单，还有 80 家客户的动态分层依据。

English 口语逐字稿

Across eighty accounts a health check has to be a product, not a task. And a product only has two design points: what counts as an anomaly, and who I look at first once I have one. [Clarify — before I answer] Four things. One: team structure, optimizer support, how many? Two: any automation, scripts, platforms, or pure manual? Three: how are anomaly thresholds defined, explicitly, or not at all? And four: do I have time to follow up on flagged accounts, because that decides how long the anomaly list can be. [Main branch — the most common starting point] So let's say: two optimizers, basic scripts, thresholds undefined, limited time. Here's how I'd work through it. First, two optimizers. Humans can do deep diagnosis, but do the math first: eighty accounts fully manual means you can't even keep a biweekly cadence. People should only touch what machines can't. Second, basic scripts exist. So automation can pull the data: spend trends, CPI and ROAS levels, creative refresh frequency, account-structure changes. Run the full scan first, turning eighty accounts into one table. Third, thresholds undefined. And this is the critical gap: without thresholds, there is no concept of "abnormal", a scan produces data, not signals. So I'd set thresholds by tier: head accounts run sensitive, flag weekly swings over fifteen percent, because a small dip there is big money. Long-tail only flags cliffs, spend stopped, or down fifty-plus percent, minor noise doesn't deserve human eyes. Plus a structural class: creatives two weeks stale, tracking errors, lights on before performance even drops. And fourth, limited time. So the anomaly list must be triaged: P0, acute change on head accounts, same day. P1, mid-tier anomalies, within three days. P2, minor tail anomalies, into the weekly batch. And list length is back-derived from follow-up capacity: better a slightly tighter threshold than a list nobody processes. So putting it together: a three-layer funnel. Full scan by script, every Monday. Threshold filter producing flags, same moment. Triaged handling, P0 same day, P1 in three, P2 batched weekly. Optimizers take the P0 and P1 deep dives; I handle everything requiring a client conversation. And Friday, I review the funnel metrics: scan coverage, flags raised, P0 closure rate. The monitoring itself must be monitored. [Branch two] Now, what if there's no tooling at all, pure manual? Team, thresholds, time as before. Then move one is building the tool, even the crudest version: a sheet, some formulas, a weekly manual export. That still beats muscle. Because for eighty accounts, manual-only guarantees misses, and what gets missed isn't the data. It's that nobody remembers which account was already shaky last time. The crudest automation still beats the most diligent manual work. Week one, build the sheet template, eighty rows, ten fields, three conditional-format rules. From then on, two hours a week of upkeep. The tool doesn't have to be perfect. It has to exist. [Branch three] But what if I'm solo with no optimizers? Then 1v1 follow-up can't cover every flag, so my time goes only to what automation can't solve: client conversations, strategy changes. Tooling, thresholds, time as before. Everything else gets batch treatment: P2 anomalies get templated outreach, "we noticed your creatives are two weeks stale, see the attached checklist", while P0 and P1 get me personally. A solo operator's leverage isn't diligence. It's templates: every anomaly class deserves a standard response template. [Branch four] And what if flags get raised but no one has time to act? Team, tooling, thresholds as before. Then the checking itself is waste: monitoring without a closed loop is dashboard decoration, and it burns maintenance cost. So: cut the check frequency before ever cutting the closure. From weekly to biweekly if needed, but one hundred percent of flags get an owner and a deadline. The meaning of monitoring is the loop. Closure rate is this system's only success metric. Three layers: scan, filter, triage. Two principles: thresholds set by tier, sensitive for the heads and cliffs for the tails, and list length back-derived from how much I can actually follow up on. What the system outputs each week isn't only an anomaly list, it's a live re-tiering of all eighty accounts.
5BQ
先对一遍映射表，缺的再补，STAR 里 R 必须有数字（24 条）

中文

【怎么用】左边是这轮大概率会问的原题（LCS 大客户线的 BQ 基本跑不出这些），右边是手册里已经打磨好的故事。直接翻本手册 BQ 章节对应条目念，不要在现场现编。 面试官原题（英文）用哪个故事切题的一句话手册位置Tell me about a time you used data to solve a problem双十一超级直播 ROI 虚高不只看板，下钻到归因逻辑，发现预算被达播吃掉BQ #1Tell me about a time you focused on the customer接手陌生家清客户，先搞定销售再三维分层客户不是一个人，先搞清楚他要什么BQ #2Tell me about a time you dealt with ambiguity老板 brief 模糊，拆结果/过程指标模糊的 brief 要自己定义成可衡量的指标BQ #5Tell me about a time you led without authority / cross-functionalD11 作为渠道 owner 统筹跨部门不直接管人，但要让每个 stakeholder 的计划 on trackBQ #7Tell me about a time you disagreed with someone与老板新老客策略分歧先做 deep dive 再反驳，用数据不讲立场BQ #8Tell me about a time you negotiated阿里 JBP 280w 谈到 180w（或李佳琦无现金谈年框）锚定 + 三大数据 + 三轮磋商BQ #9 / #4Tell me about a time you took ownership达播离职主动 take，618 二梯队十倍增长没人做的事我接，接了就做出结果BQ #10Tell me about a time you were innovative站内广告 + 站外 CRM 打通用现有资源组合出新能力，成本省 100 倍BQ #11Tell me about a time you made a good judgment call大促/平销错峰投放短期 ROI 和长期客群结构，我选了后者的解法BQ #12Tell me about a time you learned something fast零基础接自播间，2 天速学新领域不靠培训，靠拆解 + 上手BQ #13Tell me about a time you exceeded expectations年货节自播会员周（或肖战痛店会员中心）在既有资源上做出超目标的增量BQ #14 / #15Tell me about a time you acted with urgency双十一叫停虚高 ROI 放量，挪预算发现问题当天就动，不等复盘BQ #16Tell me about a time you earned trust和财务健康透明合作，不被降价裹挟信任来自透明，不来自讨好BQ #17Tell me about a time you failed见本手册 BQ-N1（新稿）承认判断错、讲怎么发现的、讲改了什么新增Tell me about a difficult stakeholder见本手册 BQ-N2（新稿）关系紧张时先给价值，再谈配合新增Tell me about working across cultures / languages见本手册 BQ-N3（新稿，需你填真实案例）中英双语工作场景新增Tell me about a time you had to say no见本手册 BQ-N4（新稿）拒绝时要给替代方案，不是只说不行新增 ■ 使用提醒 1. 手册里每道题的中英都是打磨过的口语逐字稿，直接念英文版就行，不要现场重新组织语言。 2. 每个故事讲完都要补一句"这件事放到 Google 的岗位上"的迁移句，否则面试官会问"这跟我们要的岗位有什么关系"。 3. 中文（逐字照搬）

S 情境对归因问题下钻，从而优化广告投放策略帮助品牌降本增效 在24年双十一的时候，我负责LP天猫渠道站内广告的投放，在最初，我的计划是以ROI为衡量指标，寻找最优广告产品进行投放，从而最大化整体ROI。 但是在双十一的第一天，我们就出现了问题，当时放量最多同时ROI最大的广告点位叫超级直播，类似于Google的PMAX和video广告的结合，仅作用于品牌直播间。当时双十一首日开播前三个小时，我们的ROI已经到了7以上，几乎是平时3-4的两倍，所以在当时基于计划我决定让投手继续加投从而达到效果最大化T 任务但是问题随即出现，大促首日我会让DP by小时报数，确保最大爆发期是on track，但我在第三个小时的数字中发现了问题。在细分销售渠道中，我发现达人直播的生意非常高，几乎110%的完成目标，但是我自己的品牌直播间却只有不到50%完成率，这和我在广告投放看板上看到的ROI=7的数字完全不吻合 超级直播既然展示的点位是品牌自播间，为何我的生意并没有实现增长？A 行动当时在我下钻问题问题之前，我先让DP减少了对超级直播的投放，从数据表面已经能看出对我自身渠道没有起到正向作用，所以我需要在有限的资源中作出快速判断 其次，我就叫来了DP团队一起下钻分析这个问题的根因，我首先从产品本身的定义出发去做基本理解，发现这个广告产品的归因逻辑是末次点击归因，只要在下单前最后一次被广告曝光过，就会被归因在这个点位的成交。其次，我看了品牌整个直播渠道的流量来源，发现达人直播有很大一部分成交来源于广告，但我实际上并没有投放这部分链接，说明很多消费者实际上在直播点位先被超级直播曝光，虽然因为比价选择在更划算的达人直播间购买。最终导致的结果是广告费用被用在了本不需要额外引流的达人渠道，而我自己的品牌直播间生意并没有得到促进R 结果在得出了这样的结论之后，我准备先做一轮验证。我让DP把省下来的预算，先挪30%到直通车点位，类似谷歌Search广告，利用货架电商的搜索心智抢占消费者，虽然ROI没有超级直播那么高，但是他的入口和直播间完全不同，可以最大程度避免人群之间的重叠导致跳转达播比价。在做了这一轮调整之后，直播间的生意在第四个小时立马出现抬头，单小时成交GMV环比前三个小时的均值要高出50%，有了这样的测试结果，我就立马将预算重新做了调整，帮助我的直播间在接下来的双十一时间完成了全周期目标。在之后我们广告的投放中，这个learning也一直警醒我们看数不要看表面 这件事的learning就是无论是做平台还是做品牌，不要被表面的数据迷惑，可能有的时候客户找你抱怨数据差，是因为他们没有下钻到更深层找出广告或者其他运营动作真正作用的根源所在，而我们作为销售，就需要帮助客户看的更深，看的更远

English 口语逐字稿

SituationSo this one goes back to Double 11 in 2024. I was owning LP's on-site advertising on Tmall, and my plan going in was pretty simple — use ROI as the yardstick, find whichever ad product performs best, and keep pouring budget into it to maximize overall ROI. Now, on day one we hit a snag. The placement with the biggest volume and the best ROI was this product called Super Live — think of it as a mix of PMax and video ads, but it only serves inside the brand's own livestream room. And in the first three hours, our ROI was already above 7 — nearly double our usual 3 to 4. So following my plan, I told the buyer to keep scaling it.TaskBut then the numbers started disagreeing with each other. Since it was day one of the promo, I had my data provider reporting hourly, just to make sure the peak stayed on track. And in hour three, I spotted it: influencer livestreams were sitting at almost 110% of target, but our own brand room was below 50%. That just didn't match the ROI of 7 I was seeing on the ads dashboard. So the question in my head was — if Super Live only shows in our own room, why isn't our own business growing?ActionSo before digging any further, I made a quick call — I had the DP cut back the Super Live spend right away, because even the surface data said it wasn't helping our own channel. When resources are tight, you make the fast call first and investigate second. Then I sat down with the DP team to find the root cause. I started from the product definition itself, and it turns out this placement runs on last-click attribution — as long as the ad was the last touch before an order, the sale gets credited to it. Then I looked at where our livestream traffic actually came from, and here's the thing — a big chunk of influencer-room sales were credited to ads, but I never placed ads on those links. So what was really happening: consumers got exposed to Super Live in our room, then compared prices and ended up buying in the cheaper influencer room. Our ad money was feeding traffic to channels that didn't need it, while our own room got nothing.ResultOnce I had that conclusion, I wanted to validate it before going all-in. So I moved 30% of the saved budget into Direct Train — basically Google Search's cousin — to capture shelf-commerce search intent. The ROI wasn't as high as Super Live, but its entry point is completely different from livestreams, which means much less audience overlap and less price-comparison jumping. And sure enough, in hour four our own room turned around — hourly GMV ran 50% above the average of the first three hours. With that proof in hand, I rebalanced the budget right away, and the room went on to hit the full-cycle target for the rest of Double 11. And the learning has stuck with me ever since: never read numbers at face value. Whether you're on the platform side or the brand side, sometimes a client complains the numbers look bad simply because nobody's drilled down to where the ad or the action is really working. And as sellers, that's exactly where we add value — helping clients see deeper, and see further.

中文（逐字照搬）

S 情境刚入职之后，就被assign负责家清纸品的头部客户，如何快速建联，整理自己的客户pipeline并做好客情维护T 任务整个team focus在美妆，几乎没有负责过家清纸品行业，客户不熟，负责的销售也不熟，我们策略中台与销售关系微妙，双方KPI不同，一个以客户广告投放为KPI，一个以GMV增长为KPIA 行动我的策略是：要搞定客户，首先需要搞定销售，因为只有销售有一手的客户资源和信息，如果我盲目花费力气去建联只会浪费更多时间。所以在一开始我就问了自己灵魂三问，我能做什么。销售需要什么，客户需要什么 既然要搞定销售，我首先需要定位自己，我有什么资源，这些资源有哪些可以赋能销售和客户。然后再去看销售需要的时候，有什么可以帮助他们促进KPI。以终为始，我决定从销售最需要的行业趋势着手，帮助他们一起看清整个大盘。因为大客户销售更多时候着眼于单客的陪跑，但往往会忽略整个大盘的趋势以及热点，从而错失商机，而我们广告BU和小店BU的数据流通非常闭塞，行业分析的数据源也只有我们部门可以产出，这既是我能为销售带来的价值，同样也可以帮助我快速了解整个行业 在搞定了销售之后，我就需要快速了解每一个客户的具体情况，比起一个个去cold call，从销售这里获得整合的信息会更加便捷，但我一共有24个客户需要负责，每家经营状况与组织架构都不相同，在短时间内reach out所有品牌也不现实，所以在了解情况之后我对自己的客户池做了一轮分层 不是传统的按照GMV做降序，而是三维的去看品牌生意，组织架构以及TR。通过这样的分层，我的pipeline出现了四大类客户：分别对应GMV高，组织完善，TR低/高；GMV低，组织待优化，TR高/低。这样的分层既可以将销售的kpi involve进来，同时可以立体的去看到品牌的内部架构，从而多维度入手去运营品牌 有了pipeline之后，我会对不同客户针对性的去运营，通用的客情维护手段是基于我手里的行业资源，去定期给客户做经营上的策略分享以及竞品追踪，同时也会日常处理他们在运营上的困难，以及针对不同品牌的所处阶段，给到他们PR或是流量的扶持。总结下来就是深度陪跑+激励并行来完成和客户的深度建联和客情维护R 结果最终的结果是

English 口语逐字稿

SituationSo, right after I joined Tencent, I got assigned to cover the top clients in home-care and tissue. And the questions sitting in front of me were: how do I build connections fast, how do I organize my pipeline, and how do I keep those relationships healthy.TaskHere's the catch, though. The whole team was focused on beauty — almost nobody had ever touched home-care or tissue. I didn't know the clients, and honestly, the sales reps covering them didn't know them that well either. On top of that, the relationship between my strategy team and the sales team was, let's say, delicate — our KPIs were different. Theirs was client ad spend; ours was GMV growth.ActionSo my strategy was: to win the clients, I had to win the sales reps first. Because they're the only ones with first-hand client resources and information. If I just blindly went out building connections on my own, I'd burn a lot of time for nothing. So from day one, I asked myself three questions — what can I do, what does sales need, and what do the clients need? To win over sales, I first had to figure out my own positioning — what resources do I actually have, and which of those can empower sales and clients? Then I looked at what sales needed to hit their KPIs. And working backwards from that, I decided to start with what they needed most: industry trends, helping them see the whole market. Because big-client reps spend all their time on one-on-one account support, and they often miss the market trends and hotspots — they lose opportunities that way. And here's my edge: the data flow between our Ads BU and the Store BU was totally siloed, and industry analysis was something only my team could produce. So that was my value to sales — and it also helped me learn the whole industry fast. Once sales was on board, I needed to get to know each client quickly. And instead of cold-calling one by one, getting consolidated intel from sales was way more efficient. But I had 24 clients, each with a different business situation and org structure — reaching out to every single brand in a short time just wasn't realistic. So after getting the lay of the land, I ran a tiering exercise on my whole client pool. And I didn't do the traditional GMV descending list — I looked at the business in three dimensions: brand business, org structure, and TR. English 口语逐字稿

SituationSo when I first took over media buying, my manager told me our ad efficiency was too low and needed optimizing. That was the whole brief.TaskAnd step one for me was figuring out what she actually meant. Conversion rate? CPC? Click-through rate? The simplest move was to list all the metrics out and go ask her for confirmation. But her reply was — and I quote — "It's a bit worse than when I ran media myself. Look at whatever needs optimizing and optimize it." So after that beautifully ambiguous answer, I knew I had to rank these metrics myself — starting from the business numbers, with a cost-optimization mindset, and figure out which ones actually mattered most.ActionHere's how I broke it down. I split the metrics into outcome metrics and process metrics. My end goal was to optimize the outcomes — and to get there, I had to fix the process metrics first. The outcome metrics: GMV driven by advertising, and CPC, which drives my buying cost. The process metrics: ROI, CTR, CVR. My starting point was simple. First I pulled the industry benchmark plus our own historical performance, and worked out a stretch target and a bottom-line target for the brand — so I could review performance in real time. Then I ranked the metrics by priority. The most important thing was driving business growth through ads, and the metrics that move GMV are ROI and CVR. To lift conversion, it's about using the right tool to reach the right audience. And reviewing our past campaign structure, I found we were mostly on keyword matching — great for search commerce, but during a big promo, shoppers browse more than they search, so feed ads convert much better at that moment. Our audiences, meanwhile, had only focused on full-size product buyers — but at the peak of a promo, you don't just need existing customers repurchasing; new customers are what drive the business. So I changed the audiences too. And after adjusting both tool and audience — GMV went up, ROI improved. But of course you can't scale spend forever, so I had to watch CPC. That meant raising CTR in a fierce auction was critical. Beyond picking the right audience, creative matters enormously — so I got the designers and marketing to co-work on new ad creatives, and that lifted CTR by 2 points.ResultSo the final result: even though the brief started out vague, after peeling the layers apart I delivered something she was genuinely happy with. And the learning I took from it: when you hit something ambiguous and can't get more useful information, deep-dive on what you already have, and set a KPI for each sub-metric. That's what helps you sort priorities faster — and makes tracking much easier afterwards.

And this was a long project — prep started in early September and ran all the way through November. So with everyone's energy stretched thin, I had to secure Tmall's business without hurting the other channels. I couldn't pile unnecessary workload on people, and I still had to make my own Double 11 execute. So unlike a normal collaborator who just gives support, I had to stand in the leader's position and keep my plan on track. So my first step was to catch up with each department one by one — align on targets and understand what support they could actually give me. And I think this step mattered most. Before each catch-up, I broke my Double 11 target down by channel and by field, listing exactly what support I needed from each department. For example: the building blocks of stock needed supply chain to secure; the incremental lift from ad creatives needed marketing's design support.ActionThe way I saw it, that step was both a KPI alignment and a fast way to size up what each stakeholder could give me — and where there was still a gap to close. Once KPIs were confirmed on both sides, I set up a regular catch-up-and-review rhythm to keep every ask on track. With marketing: during prep, I reviewed creative progress weekly — how each placement's design was coming, whether timelines could be tightened. Mid-campaign, I pulled them into the ad-buying operation itself, updating them in real time on creative performance so they knew exactly when to iterate. The supply-chain catch-up was even more critical — our stock came by sea freight, which is full of uncertainty. So every week I got the latest arrival status, mapped it against my own stock building blocks, and checked whether each phase of Double 11 would have a gap — and what my contingency was if one appeared mid-campaign. And I caught up with finance periodically too, updating sales by phase. That gave her a real feel for progress — and when a gap appeared, she could flag it early and I could go ask for financial support.ResultSo midway through Double 11, there was an incident: the stock supply chain had promised me got delayed at sea — right at our business peak. Based on current inventory, I estimated a gap of around five million. Here's what I did: first I went to supply chain to confirm the earliest arrival of the delayed stock — and got no clear answer. So I went to JD, a sister channel, for stock support. Tmall and JD push different hero products — and the Tmall hero items actually had healthier inventory on JD, with no stock-out risk. Once JD confirmed, I filled the gap. And afterwards, in the Double 11 review, I used the records from my weekly catch-ups to formally request logistics optimization from supply chain — so the brand could secure stock stability during future mega-promos. And what I learned from leading that project: first, set achievable, trackable targets for the team at the very start. Then review regularly and keep records — so the work is quantifiable and documented. And finally, the end-of-project review is essential: what went well, what needs improvement, all backed by data — so both sides learn the lesson and do better next time.

中文（逐字照搬）

S 情境在制定Y26生意目标的时候，基于对新老客的占比（75%比25%）以及新客成交占比在25年出现了5个pts的下滑，我老板认为明年的抓手是对于老客的召回，但是对于我来说，我们目前已经上线的老客召回机制已经拉满，但是YTD的进度依然没有on track，所以我的意见与老板是相左的。虽然我们都是为了能够把生意做好，但是我们对于未来策略的指定是出现了偏差 但在当时我并没有认为我老板的结论就一定是错的，因为新客占比的下滑说明我们品牌的招新能力确实是收到了挑战。为了能够进一步去论证新老客的运营计划，我开始对于老客购买行为进行了一次非常深的deepdiveT 任务具体我是怎么做的。对于老客，我从两个维度对他们进行了segmentation 1、购买recency以及频次 2、购买产品 recency的定义就是以当前时间窗为起始往前看，1一个月是最小时间尺度，目的是为了当前的生意老客是由之前什么时间点的消费者贡献的，而购买频次看的就是一个消费者在购买了一款产品之后他的回购周期有多久，回购频次有几次 从这两个数字中我得出了以下结论：1、我们的老客大部分是大促客，从recency来看生意贡献大部分都是由38，618，D11的老客带来的，那我如果看的再细一点，这一部分老客有80%都来自于在品牌货架电商下单的消费者。而从老客购买频次以及回购周期来看，一般在120天，大部分的回购频次都止于2次。综上数据，我得出的结论就是我们的老客生意大部分是由过往大促的老客base贡献，但他们对于回购的动力大多数人都停止于第二次回购，也就是说品牌忠诚度不够， 而从购买产品的表现来看，我发现消费者购买我们品牌的连带率是远低于行业benchmark的，也就是说消费者买了发膜之后回购洗发水的比例低于行业，在review过往的运营动作之后我发现我们缺乏对这一部分连带specifically的激励，导致消费者缺乏动力尝鲜A 行动那分析完我们老客的情况，我又去看了新客的人群画像，我会把新的segmentation通过来源渠道进行划分，分成直播间新客，派样新客以及自然新客三类 从数据来看，直播新客占据了75%以上，他们来自于李佳奇等各大天猫主播的场域，而其余的20%则来自于派样新客。和老客数据一起看会出现很鲜明的对比，也就是说老客的召回需要我focuson店铺货架的运营，而对于新客，我则需要在直播场域进行深耕。 在有了这样一个发现之后我其实认为我和老板的意见相左其实都是基于数据的一些表面认知，当我们deepdive之后才会发现，在老客召回方面我们其实做的不够，而在拉新场域上，还存在很多机会点。因此我把这个数字与我老板分享之后，我们基于洞察重新给出了策略R 结果for拉新来说，我们需要加大对派样的投资，这是我们可控且能实时tracking的渠道，而对于达人直播，我们需要更加密切的客情维护以及生意合作才能进一步secure品怕在市场的market share 而对于老客召回，我们之前只聚焦于短信和外呼的触达上，却缺乏对机制的深度思考，在看了recency和frequency的数字之后，我们更加明确了触达消费者的时间段需要靠近大促，以及消费者的人群base应该是在货架购买过产品的老客。 而从产品数据得出，如果要drive老客，我们还可以在连带1转2上发力，给买过发膜的消费者推送洗发水来完善他们的洗护路径。 综上根据数据的insight我们重新调整了策略，上线了关于老客的回购/连带机制，而在新客招新上，我们开始参与李佳奇的小样节来帮助我们触达更多的潜客。 在1月份进行修改之后，我们的新客和老客的成交绝对值都提升了20%以上，新客占比因为没有直播的影响暂时没有提升，但是我们派样蓄水的分数同比提升了50%。 从这件事我的learning是，当你和对方在意见上产生分歧的时候，你可以把双方的意见combine在一起去做deepdive。只有这样你才不会被自己的主观意识带领片面地得出结论。同时我认为当你的结论出来之后，需要先和对方进行沟通来确保双方能够对新的数字/结论达成一致，既可以提高工作效率，也可以通过思维的碰撞发现更多insight

SituationSo we were setting the 2026 business target. Based on the existing-versus-new customer split — 75 to 25 — and a 5-point drop in new-customer transaction share during 2025, my manager believed next year's lever was winning back existing customers. But from where I sat, the win-back mechanisms we'd already launched were maxed out, and YTD progress still wasn't on track. So I disagreed with her. We both wanted the business to do well — we'd just diverged on strategy. But here's the thing — I didn't assume she was wrong. The drop in new-customer share genuinely meant our acquisition was being challenged. So to properly test both plans, I ran a really deep dive into existing-customer purchase behavior.TaskSo here's what I did. I segmented existing customers along two dimensions: purchase recency and frequency, and products purchased. Recency means looking backward from the current window — one month as the minimum scale — to see which past cohorts are contributing today's business. Frequency looks at a customer's repurchase cycle after buying one product, and how many times they come back. And from those numbers, I drew a few conclusions. First: most of our existing customers are promo customers. By recency, the existing-customer business mostly came from the 38, 618, and Double 11 promo cohorts — and zooming in, 80% of them had ordered on the brand's shelf commerce. From frequency: the typical repurchase cycle is around 120 days, and most customers stop at two repurchases. So the bottom line: our existing-customer business was being carried by past promo cohorts, but most of them stop after the second purchase — brand loyalty just wasn't there. And looking at products, I found our cross-attach rate was far below the industry benchmark — customers who buy the hair mask just weren't coming back for shampoo at the industry rate. And reviewing past operations, I found we had no specific incentive for that attach behavior — so customers had no reason to try the second product.ActionSo after analyzing existing customers, I turned to the new-customer profiles. I segmented them by acquisition source into three groups: livestream new customers, sampling new customers, and organic. And from the data, livestream new customers made up over 75% — coming from Li Jiaqi and the other major Tmall hosts — while about 20% came from sampling. Put that next to the existing-customer data, and the contrast was sharp: win-back requires focusing on store shelf operations, while acquisition means going deep into the livestream space. And with that finding, I realized my manager and I had both been working from surface reads of the data. Only after the deep dive did it become clear: on win-back, we actually hadn't done enough — and in acquisition, there were still plenty of opportunities. So I shared the numbers with her, and we reset the strategy together based on the insights.ResultSo for acquisition: we increased investment in sampling — a channel we control and can track in real time. And for influencer livestreams, closer relationship management and deeper business cooperation to secure the brand's market share. For win-back: we'd focused only on SMS and outbound calls, without deep thinking on mechanisms. After seeing the recency and frequency numbers, we got clearer on timing — outreach should cluster around promos — and on the audience: existing customers who'd bought via the shelf. And from the product data: to drive existing customers, we could also push the attach from one product to two — recommend shampoo to everyone who bought the hair mask, completing their wash-and-care routine. So we adjusted the strategy: launched repurchase and attach mechanisms for existing customers, and joined Li Jiaqi's sample festival to reach more potential customers. And after the changes went live in January, the absolute transaction value of both new and existing customers grew by more than 20%. New-customer share didn't rise yet because of the livestream cycle — but our sampling reservoir score grew 50% year over year. And my learning from this one: when you disagree with someone, combine both views and run the deep dive together. That's the only way to avoid being led by your own bias into a one-sided conclusion. And once your conclusion is out — communicate first, make sure both sides align on the new numbers. It improves efficiency, and the collision of thinking surfaces more insights.

S 情境品牌知名度不够，但是大促期间生意压力很大，所以会优先选择高ROI的投放点位。但是在 review的时候发现高ROI往往意味着追投品牌老客，但对于我们这样还处在成长上升曲线的品牌而言，过多的老客并不是健康的生意结构T 任务老客在大促期间投放ROI很高，CPC也会比较低，但是对于新客而言竞价会更激烈，同时转化率也会更低 是追求生意上的增长还是品牌长期的建设A 行动我的选择是在大促期间优先secure老客的base，但是在投放工具以及关键词方面，我会选择以品类词作为抓手，一方面是考虑到搜索品牌词的人群本身已经有品牌意识，不需要额外的曝光去吸引进店，另外一方面是通过品类词的投放，我可以精准的抓取到浏览摇摆的新老客，也可以在一定程度上帮我做到大促招新的目的 另外在非大促期间，我则将重点全部投入到对于新客的触达上，一方面可以在平效期收割他们作为我的品牌蓄水人群，另一方面平销期的竞争更少，我的CPC也可以得到优化R 结果最终的结局就是，通过我对于广告投放策略的调整，我们品牌成功在大促期间保持了ROI +9%，同时花费节省85%。而在平销期间，通过广告触达的新客占比整个消费者达到75%，而新科带来的ROI也达到3，高于行业水平的2 这种大促/平销错峰投放收割的方法论在后来的平台大促都得以广泛使用并得到验证，降本增效

English 口语逐字稿

SituationSo the brand's awareness wasn't high, but the business pressure during mega-promos was heavy — so we kept prioritizing the highest-ROI placements. But in review, I noticed something: high ROI usually just means re-targeting the brand's existing customers. And for a brand still on its growth curve, too heavy an existing-customer mix is not a healthy structure.TaskSo here's the tension: during mega-promos, existing customers deliver high ROI and cheaper CPC — while new customers mean fiercer bidding and lower conversion. So — chase the short-term lift, or build the brand for the long run?ActionAnd my call was to do both, but on different clocks. During mega-promos, I secured the existing-customer base first — but in ad tools and keywords, I used category words as the lever. Two reasons. One: people searching brand words already have brand awareness — they don't need extra exposure to walk in. Two: bidding on category words precisely captures the swing shoppers browsing between brands — which also helps me recruit new customers during the promo. Then outside of mega-promos, I shifted nearly all effort into reaching new customers. That way, in the quiet periods I harvest them as the brand's reservoir audiences — and with less competition in normal times, my CPC optimizes too.ResultSo the final outcome: with the adjusted strategy, the brand held ROI at plus-9% during the mega-promo — while cutting spend by 85%. And in normal periods, new customers reached through ads made up 75% of all consumers, with the ROI on new customers hitting 3 — above the industry level of 2. And this staggered promo-versus-peacetime invest-and-harvest playbook got adopted widely across later platform promos and validated repeatedly — lower cost, higher efficiency.
